Dataset: 9 positive, 5 negative examples.
\[
H(S) = -\frac{9}{14}\log_2\left(\frac{9}{14}\right) - \frac{5}{14}\log_2\left(\frac{5}{14}\right) \approx 0.940
\]

After splitting on feature $A$:
\begin{itemize}
\item Branch 1: 6 pos, 1 neg $\implies H = 0.592$
\item Branch 2: 3 pos, 4 neg $\implies H = 0.985$
\end{itemize}
\[
\text{IG} \approx 0.940 - \left(\frac{7}{14} \cdot 0.592 + \frac{7}{14} \cdot 0.985\right) \approx 0.152
\]
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Gini Index}

\begin{definition}[Gini Impurity]
\[
\text{Gini}(S) = 1 - \sum_{k=1}^K p_k^2
\]
Alternative to entropy, computationally simpler (no logarithms).
\begin{itemize}
\item Gini = 0: Pure node
\item Gini = $1 - 1/K$: Maximum impurity (for $K$ classes)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\textbf{Entropy vs Gini}: Both work well in practice. Gini is slightly faster; entropy more theoretically motivated.

\subsection{Tree Growing Algorithms}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{ID3 Algorithm (Iterative Dichotomiser 3)}

\textbf{Input}: Dataset $S$, remaining feature set $A$, and parent-majority default class

\textbf{Base cases}:
\begin{itemize}
\item If $S$ is empty: return a leaf with the parent-majority default class
\item If all examples in $S$ have same class: return leaf with that class
\item If no features left: return leaf with majority class
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Recursive case}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Find best feature: $A^* = \argmax_A \text{IG}(S, A)$
\item Create node for $A^*$
\item For each value $v$ of $A^*$:
\begin{itemize}
\item Add branch for $A^* = v$
\item Recursively build on $S_v$ with remaining features $A\setminus\{A^*\}$ and the current node majority as the default
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Return}: Decision tree
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{remark}[Tree Algorithms]
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{ID3}: Original, uses information gain, only categorical features
\item \textbf{C4.5}: Extension of ID3, handles continuous features and missing values
\item \textbf{CART (Classification and Regression Trees)}: Builds binary trees; classification may use Gini impurity, while squared-error regression uses the leaf/variance criterion below
\end{itemize}
\end{remark}

\subsection{Regression Trees}

For regression, use \emph{variance reduction} instead of information gain:

\begin{definition}[Variance Reduction]
\[
\text{VR}(S, A) = \Var(S) - \sum_{v} \frac{|S_v|}{|S|} \Var(S_v)
\]
where $\Var(S) = \frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i \in S}(y_i - \bar{y})^2$.

At leaves, predict the mean: $\hat{y} = \frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{i \in S} y_i$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Pruning}

\textbf{Problem}: Trees can grow very deep and overfit the training data.

\textbf{Solution}: Pruning---simplify the tree by removing branches.

\begin{definition}[Pre-Pruning (Early Stopping)]
Stop growing the tree early if:
\begin{itemize}
\item Maximum depth reached
\item Node contains fewer than min\_samples\_split examples
\item Information gain below threshold
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Post-Pruning]
Grow full tree, then prune back:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Grow complete tree
\item For each internal node, evaluate: ``Does removing this subtree improve validation performance?''
\item If yes, replace subtree with leaf (using majority class or mean)
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\textbf{Cost-Complexity Pruning}: Balance tree size and error:
\[
\text{Cost} = \text{Error}(\text{tree}) + \alpha \cdot |\text{leaves}|
\]
Increase $\alpha$ to get simpler trees.

\subsection{Interpretability vs Performance}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Advantages of Decision Trees}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Interpretable}: Easy to visualize and explain (``If-then'' rules)
\item \textbf{Non-parametric}: No fixed finite-dimensional distribution family; the tree still imposes a recursive partitioning structure
\item \textbf{Handles mixed data}: Both categorical and numerical features
\item \textbf{Feature interactions}: Automatically captures interactions
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Disadvantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{High variance}: Small data changes can drastically alter tree structure
\item \textbf{Greedy}: Locally optimal splits may not be globally optimal
\item \textbf{Bias toward features with more levels}: Tend to favor categorical features with many values
\item \textbf{Overfitting}: Without pruning, can memorize training data
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use}:
\begin{itemize}
\item When interpretability is crucial (medical diagnosis, loan approval)
\item As a baseline or for feature engineering
\item As components in ensemble methods (Random Forests, Boosting)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsection{Deriving Leaf Predictions and Working Through a Tree}
For a classification leaf with empirical class proportions $\hat p_c$, its mean categorical negative log-likelihood is $-\sum_c\hat p_c\log q_c$. A Lagrange multiplier for $\sum_c q_c=1$ gives the optimum $q_c=\hat p_c$ and minimum entropy $H(\hat p)$. Therefore a weighted entropy reduction is an improvement in the best leaf likelihood, up to the log-base scale. Gini impurity also has a loss interpretation: for one-hot target $\vect e_Y$,
\[
\min_{\vect q}\E\|\vect e_Y-\vect q\|^2
=1-\sum_c\hat p_c^2,
\]
attained at $\vect q=\hat{\vect p}$. These are different impurity criteria and need not rank all candidate splits identically.

\begin{example}[All candidate thresholds, then pruning]
Consider eight observations with two features $x,B$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrrrr}
$x$&1&2&3&4&5&6&7&8\\
$B$&0&0&1&1&0&0&1&1\\
$y$&0&0&0&1&1&1&1&0
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The root contains four of each class, so entropy is one bit. For thresholds halfway between consecutive $x$ values, the information gains are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rrrrrrr}
Threshold&1.5&2.5&3.5&4.5&5.5&6.5&7.5\\\hline
Gain (bits)&.137925&.311278&.548795&.188722&.048795&0&.137925
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
For the best split at $3.5$, the left child is pure and the right has four positives and one negative. Thus the weighted entropy is $(3/8)0+(5/8)H_2(4/5)=0.451205$. Splitting on $B$ gives two balanced children and zero gain. The best root is therefore $x\le3.5$. Splitting its right child at $7.5$ makes both children pure. The resulting rule predicts 1 only for $3.5<x\le7.5$, so a query $(x,B)=(6,1)$ is classified as 1.

Measure pruning risk by the training misclassification fraction, using any fixed rule for the tied root majority. The full tree has risk 0 and 3 leaves; its root-only split (stump) has risk $1/8$ and 2 leaves; a single root leaf has risk $1/2$. Cost-complexity objectives are respectively
\[
3\alpha,\qquad\tfrac18+2\alpha,\qquad\tfrac12+\alpha.
\]
The preferred size changes at $\alpha=1/8$ and $3/8$ (ties at the boundaries). On a separate validation set $x=(2,4,6,8)$ with labels $(0,1,1,1)$, the full tree makes one error and the stump makes none. This can favor the pruned tree even though it has worse training error. The validation labels select the subtree; final test labels do not.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small,align=center}]
\node[draw,rounded corners] (a) at (0,0) {$x\le3.5$?};
\node[draw,fill=orange!15] (b) at (-2,-1.4) {Class 0\\$x=1,2,3$};
\node[draw,rounded corners] (c) at (2,-1.4) {$x\le7.5$?};
\node[draw,fill=blue!15] (d) at (.6,-2.9) {Class 1\\$x=4,5,6,7$};
\node[draw,fill=orange!15] (e) at (3.4,-2.9) {Class 0\\$x=8$};
\draw[->] (a)--node[above left]{yes}(b);\draw[->] (a)--node[above right]{no}(c);
\draw[->] (c)--node[above left]{yes}(d);\draw[->] (c)--node[above right]{no}(e);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The fully grown tree from the eight-row calculation. Feature $B$ is unused because its root information gain is zero. Pruning the lower split merges its two leaves and predicts class 1 throughout $x>3.5$.}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[A regression split with every SSE shown]
A leaf predicts a constant $a$ minimizing $\sum_i(y_i-a)^2$; differentiating gives $a=\bar y$. For $x=(1,2,3,4)$ and $y=(2,3,8,9)$, the root mean is $5.5$ and SSE is $12.25+6.25+6.25+12.25=37$. Splitting at $x=2.5$ gives means $2.5$ and $8.5$, each child SSE $0.5$, and total SSE $1$. The reduction is $36$. Squared-error regression trees choose splits by this reduction, not by classification entropy.
\end{example}

\section{Bias--Variance Tradeoff}
\label{sec:bias-variance}
% =======================

\subsection{Decomposition of Prediction Error}

Fix a test input $\vect{x}$ and suppose $y=f(\vect{x})+\epsilon$, with $\E[\epsilon\mid\vect{x}]=0$ and $\Var(\epsilon\mid\vect{x})=\sigma^2$. The fitted predictor $\hat f$ varies with the training set, which is independent of the test noise.

\begin{theorem}[Bias-Variance Decomposition]
The expected test error at a point $\vect{x}$ is:
\[
\E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - y)^2] = \underbrace{(\E[\hat{f}(\vect{x})] - f(\vect{x}))^2}_{\text{Bias}^2} + \underbrace{\E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - \E[\hat{f}(\vect{x})])^2]}_{\text{Variance}} + \underbrace{\sigma^2}_{\text{Irreducible Error}}
\]
On the left, expectation is over training-set randomness and independent test noise; expectations involving only the fitted predictor are over training sets.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Sketch]
\begin{align}
\E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - y)^2] &= \E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - f(\vect{x}) + f(\vect{x}) - y)^2] \\
&= \E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - f(\vect{x}))^2] + \E[(f(\vect{x}) - y)^2] \\
&\quad + 2\E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - f(\vect{x}))(f(\vect{x}) - y)]
\end{align}
The cross term vanishes since $f(\vect{x})$ and $\hat{f}(\vect{x})$ are independent of $\epsilon = y - f(\vect{x})$.

Expanding the first term:
\begin{align}
\E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - f(\vect{x}))^2] &= \E[(\hat{f}(\vect{x}) - \E[\hat{f}] + \E[\hat{f}] - f)^2] \\
&= \underbrace{\Var(\hat{f}(\vect{x}))}_{\text{Variance}} + \underbrace{(\E[\hat{f}(\vect{x})] - f(\vect{x}))^2}_{\text{Bias}^2}
\end{align}
\end{proof}

\subsection{Understanding Bias and Variance}

\begin{definition}[Bias]
\textbf{Bias} measures how far the average prediction (over many training sets) is from the true value.

\textbf{High bias} = underfitting:
\begin{itemize}
\item Model is too simple to capture the true pattern
\item Example: Linear model for non-linear data
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Variance]
\textbf{Variance} measures how much predictions vary across different training sets.

\textbf{High variance} = overfitting:
\begin{itemize}
\item Model is too sensitive to training data fluctuations
\item Example: Very deep decision tree
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
Think of shooting arrows at a target:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Low bias, low variance}: Arrows cluster tightly around bullseye (ideal)
\item \textbf{High bias, low variance}: Arrows cluster tightly, but away from bullseye (systematic error)
\item \textbf{Low bias, high variance}: Arrows scattered around bullseye (inconsistent)
\item \textbf{High bias, high variance}: Arrows scattered far from bullseye (worst case)
\end{itemize}
\end{intuition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.85]
\foreach \cx/\cy/\bias/\variance in {0/0/Low/Low,5/0/High/Low,0/-5/Low/High,5/-5/High/High} {
  \begin{scope}[shift={(\cx,\cy)}]
    \draw (0,0) circle (0.6);
    \draw (0,0) circle (1.4);
    \fill[black] (0,0) circle (1.2pt);
    \node[text width=3.8cm] at (0,-1.9) {\bias\ bias\\\variance\ variance};
  \end{scope}
}
\foreach \x/\y in {-0.08/0.08,0.08/0.08,-0.08/-0.08,0.08/-0.08} {
  \fill[blue] (\x,\y) circle (2.5pt);
  \fill[blue] ({5.85+\x},\y) circle (2.5pt);
}
\foreach \x/\y in {-0.65/0.5,0.65/0.5,-0.65/-0.5,0.65/-0.5} {
  \fill[orange!80!black] (\x,{-5+\y}) circle (2.5pt);
  \fill[orange!80!black] ({5.85+\x},{-5+\y}) circle (2.5pt);
}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{All four bias-variance combinations (schematic). The black target center is the true value; dots are predictions from different training sets. The high-bias prediction centers are displaced by 0.85 units.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Model Complexity and the Tradeoff}

\begin{keyidea}
In the classical schematic tradeoff, increasing model complexity often has the following tendencies:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Bias decreases}: More flexible models can fit data better
\item \textbf{Variance increases}: More flexibility means more sensitivity to data
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Sweet spot}: Balance bias and variance to minimize total error.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
  width=12cm,height=7cm,xlabel={Model complexity (schematic)},ylabel={Expected squared error},
  xmin=0,xmax=10,ymin=0,ymax=10,legend pos=north east,grid=major]
\addplot[blue,thick,domain=0:10,samples=101] {8/(x+1)};
\addlegendentry{Bias$^2$}
\addplot[orange!80!black,thick,domain=0:10,samples=101] {2*x/9};
\addlegendentry{Variance}
\addplot[black,thick,dashed,domain=0:10,samples=101] {8/(x+1)+2*x/9+1};
\addlegendentry{Total error}
\addplot[gray,thick,dotted,domain=0:10] {1};
\addlegendentry{Noise}
\draw[densely dashed] (axis cs:5,0)--(axis cs:5,3.444444);
\node[anchor=south west] at (axis cs:5,3.444444) {Minimum at 5};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Illustrative classical tradeoff: Bias$^2=8/(c+1)$, variance $=2c/9$, and noise $=1$. Their sum has its minimum at $c=5$. These are schematic functions, not a universal law of model complexity.}
\end{figure}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Examples by Model Type}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
\toprule
Model & Bias & Variance \\
\midrule
Linear Regression & High (if data non-linear) & Low \\
High-degree Polynomial & Low & High \\
KNN with $k=1$ & Low & High \\
KNN with large $k$ & High & Low \\
Shallow Decision Tree & High & Low \\
Deep Decision Tree & Low & High \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Bias-variance characteristics of different models}
\end{table}

\subsection{Controlling the Tradeoff}

\subsubsection{Cross-Validation}

\begin{definition}[$k$-Fold Cross-Validation: preview]
Partition the current non-test training data into $k$ approximately equal
folds. Fit the complete procedure on the other folds and validate on the
held-out fold, repeating once per fold. Use these scores for model selection;
full algorithms, unequal-fold aggregation, uncertainty, and group/time
alternatives are in Sections~\ref{sec:evaluation-uncertainty}
and~\ref{sec:cv-protocol}. This inner selection score is not a final test
estimate of the selected procedure.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Leave-One-Out Cross-Validation (LOOCV)]
Special case with $k = n$ (number of examples). Train on $n-1$ examples, test on 1. Repeat for each example.

\textbf{Pros}: Each fit uses $n-1$ observations, so its training size is close to the final fit. The resulting risk estimate need not be exactly unbiased and can have high variance.

\textbf{Cons}: Computationally expensive ($n$ model fits)
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Regularization}

As discussed earlier, regularization (Ridge, Lasso, ElasticNet) explicitly controls model complexity:
\begin{itemize}
\item Can increase bias by restricting the fitted procedure; the effect is model- and distribution-dependent
\item Can reduce variance; improved total test risk still requires validation
\item Tune $\lambda$ via cross-validation to find optimal tradeoff
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Ensemble Methods}

Preview of Part 3: Averaging predictors with the same expectation preserves their common bias; changing how base models are fitted can also change bias. The precise dependence calculation is in Section~\ref{sec:ensemble-dependence}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Bagging} (e.g., Random Forests): Reduces variance by averaging
\item \textbf{Boosting} (e.g., XGBoost): Sequentially optimizes a declared training loss; bias, variance, and test-risk changes depend on the procedure
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Exact Arithmetic and a Repeated-Training Experiment}
\begin{example}[Computing every term at a fixed input]
Let the true mean at a fixed input be $f(x)=1$, with independent test noise of mean zero and variance $0.25$. Suppose two equally likely training datasets yield predictions $0.8,1.2$ for procedure A. Its mean prediction is 1, bias squared is 0, variance is $[(0.8-1)^2+(1.2-1)^2]/2=0.04$, and expected test MSE is $0.29$. Procedure B yields $0.6,0.8$, so its mean is $0.7$, bias squared is $0.09$, variance is $0.01$, and test MSE is $0.35$. Lower variance alone does not imply lower error.
\end{example}

The decomposition can be estimated by training repeatedly on synthetic datasets where the true mean is known. Here each of 400 datasets has 20 independent inputs uniform on $[-1,1]$, targets $y=x^2+\epsilon$, and $\epsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,0.3^2)$. Fit degree-1, degree-2, and degree-5 polynomials by least squares. At the fixed input $x=0$, let $\bar f$ be the mean of the 400 fitted predictions and use empirical variance with divisor 400:
\[
\widehat{\mathrm{Bias}^2}=(\bar f-0)^2,\quad
\widehat{\mathrm{Var}}=\frac1{400}\sum_{r=1}^{400}(\hat f_r-\bar f)^2.
\]
The displayed estimates are finite Monte Carlo estimates of the population bias and variance, not exact population values or confidence intervals. The empirical identity
$\frac1{400}\sum_r(\hat f_r-f)^2=(\bar f-f)^2+\frac1{400}\sum_r(\hat f_r-\bar f)^2$
is exact; adding the known noise variance gives the corresponding estimated test risk.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{r|rrrr}
\toprule
Degree & Estimated bias$^2$ & Estimated variance & Noise & Sum\\\midrule
1 & .098978 & .009786 & .090000 & .198764\\
2 & .000003 & .009851 & .090000 & .099855\\
5 & .000009 & .021381 & .090000 & .111390\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch2/repeated-fits.pdf}
\caption{Repeated training on the specified quadratic generating process, seed 20261119. Thin curves show the first 30 fits; the thick curve is the mean of all 400 fits; the dashed curve is the true $x^2$. Every panel uses the same datasets, plotting domain, and axis scales.}
\end{figure}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=.88\linewidth]{figures/batch2/bias-variance-numeric.pdf}
\caption{Numerical decomposition at $x=0$ from the same 400 fits. Bar heights are the sums in the table, not training errors. A quadratic is appropriate here because the true mean was deliberately chosen to be quadratic; this is not a universal recommendation of polynomial degree two.}
\end{figure}

Real datasets do not provide repeated independent training samples and a known generating mean. Cross-validation estimates predictive performance of a fitting procedure, not separate ground-truth bias and variance terms. Select preprocessing, model complexity, and regularization inside the validation workflow of Section~\ref{sec:learning-protocol}; do not select them by repeatedly inspecting a final test score.

\subsection{Summary of Part 1}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Key Takeaways]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Linear Regression}: Foundation of supervised learning. Normal equation for closed-form; gradient descent for iterative solution.

\item \textbf{Regularization}: Ridge ($\ell_2$) for shrinkage, Lasso ($\ell_1$) for sparsity. Essential for high-dimensional data.

\item \textbf{Logistic Regression}: Linear model for classification using sigmoid function and cross-entropy loss.

\item \textbf{KNN}: Non-parametric, instance-based learning. Simple but suffers from curse of dimensionality.

\item \textbf{Decision Trees}: Interpretable, handles non-linear patterns. Prone to overfitting without pruning.

\item \textbf{Bias-Variance Tradeoff}: Squared test risk decomposes at a fixed input into squared bias, variance, and noise. Increasing flexibility often reduces approximation bias but can increase variance; the behavior depends on the fitted procedure. Use validation to assess predictive risk.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Next Steps}: Part 2 develops probabilistic models such as Naive Bayes and GDA, then maximum-margin methods such as SVMs. Their scores have different interpretations: an uncalibrated SVM score is not a posterior probability.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Derive the gradient of the Ridge loss with its stated $1/n$ scaling and show that it leads to $\vect{w}^* = (\mat{X}^\top\mat{X} + n\lambda\mat{I})^{-1}\mat{X}^\top\vect{y}$. State whether the intercept is penalized.

\item Show that the mean logistic-loss Hessian is positive semidefinite. Explain why convexity alone does not guarantee a finite MLE on separable data, and state how a penalty changes the problem.

\item For a dataset with 8 positive and 4 negative examples, compute the entropy. If a feature splits it into (6 pos, 1 neg) and (2 pos, 3 neg), compute the information gain.

\item Implement KNN ($k=3$) with positive points $(1,1)$, $(2,2)$, $(3,1)$ and negative points $(6,5)$, $(7,7)$, $(8,6)$. Classify $(4,3)$.

\item For univariate polynomial regression of degree $p$, including an intercept, how many parameters are in the model? How does increasing $p$ affect flexibility and the risk of overfitting?

\item Draw a bias-variance diagram and mark where the following would fall: (a) $k=1$ KNN, (b) Linear regression on non-linear data, (c) Deep decision tree.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

% =======================
% Part 2: Probabilistic Models
% =======================
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 2: Probabilistic Models}
\section*{Part 2: Probabilistic Models}

\section{Naive Bayes}
\label{sec:naive-bayes}

\subsection{Probabilistic Classification Framework}

Generative classifiers model $P(\vect{x},y)$ and use Bayes' rule. Discriminative classifiers instead learn conditional probabilities or a decision function directly, without modeling the input distribution.

\begin{definition}[Bayes' Rule for Classification]
Given features $\vect{x}$ and class $y \in \{1, 2, \ldots, K\}$:
\[
P(y = c | \vect{x}) = \frac{P(\vect{x} | y = c) \cdot P(y = c)}{P(\vect{x})} = \frac{P(\vect{x} | y = c) \cdot P(y = c)}{\sum_{k=1}^K P(\vect{x} | y = k) \cdot P(y = k)}
\]
where:
\begin{itemize}
\item $P(y=c|\vect{x})$ is the \textbf{posterior} probability (what we want)
\item $P(\vect{x}|y=c)$ is the \textbf{likelihood} (how likely are these features given class $c$)
\item $P(y=c)$ is the \textbf{prior} (how common is class $c$ in general)
\item $P(\vect{x})$ is the \textbf{evidence} (normalization constant)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\textbf{Classification rule}: 
\[
\hat{y} = \argmax_{c} P(y=c|\vect{x}) = \argmax_{c} P(\vect{x}|y=c) \cdot P(y=c)
\]
We can ignore $P(\vect{x})$ since it doesn't depend on $c$.

\subsection{The Naive Bayes Assumption}

\textbf{Challenge}: An unrestricted joint probability table for $d$ categorical features with $K$ values each has $K^d-1$ parameters per class. Structured parametric models need not have this exponential parameter count.

\textbf{Solution}: Assume \emph{conditional independence}.

\begin{definition}[Naive Bayes Assumption]
Features are conditionally independent given the class:
\[
P(\vect{x} | y = c) = P(x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_d | y = c) = \prod_{j=1}^d P(x_j | y = c)
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
``Naive'' because we assume features don't interact given the class. For spam detection: knowing an email contains ``free'' doesn't change the probability of ``money'' appearing, given we know it's spam. This is often false in reality, but works surprisingly well in practice!
\end{intuition}

\begin{theorem}[Naive Bayes Classifier]
\[
\hat{y} = \argmax_{c} P(y=c) \prod_{j=1}^d P(x_j | y=c)
\]
In log space (for numerical stability):
\[
\hat{y} = \argmax_{c} \left[\log P(y=c) + \sum_{j=1}^d \log P(x_j | y=c)\right]
\]
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Estimating Parameters}

From training data $\mathcal{D} = \{(\vect{x}_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$:

\begin{definition}[Prior Estimation]
\[
P(y = c) = \frac{\text{count}(y=c)}{n}
\]
Simply the fraction of training examples in class $c$.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Likelihood Estimation]
Depends on the type of features:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Categorical}: $P(x_j = v | y=c) = \frac{\text{count}(x_j=v, y=c)}{\text{count}(y=c)}$
\item \textbf{Continuous}: Assume a distribution (e.g., Gaussian) and estimate parameters
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Variants of Naive Bayes}

\subsubsection{Gaussian Naive Bayes}

For continuous features, assume each feature follows a Gaussian distribution.

\begin{definition}[Gaussian Naive Bayes]
Assume $X_j\mid y=c\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{jc},\sigma_{jc}^2)$ with $\sigma_{jc}^2>0$; its conditional density is:
\[
P(x_j | y=c) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_{jc}^2}} \exp\left(-\frac{(x_j - \mu_{jc})^2}{2\sigma_{jc}^2}\right)
\]
where $\mu_{jc}$ and $\sigma_{jc}^2$ are estimated from training data:
\[
\mu_{jc} = \frac{1}{n_c}\sum_{i: y_i=c} x_{ij}, \quad \sigma_{jc}^2 = \frac{1}{n_c}\sum_{i: y_i=c} (x_{ij} - \mu_{jc})^2
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Medical Diagnosis]
Predict disease (yes/no) from symptoms: temperature and blood pressure.

\textbf{Training data}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Disease=Yes (3 examples): temps = [38.5, 39.0, 38.7], BP = [140, 145, 142]
\item Disease=No (2 examples): temps = [36.8, 37.1], BP = [120, 118]
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Estimates}:
\begin{itemize}
\item $P(\text{Disease}=\text{Yes}) = 3/5 = 0.6$
\item $\mu_{\text{temp,yes}} = 38.73$, $\sigma_{\text{temp,yes}}^2 \approx 0.04222$
\item $\mu_{\text{temp,no}} = 36.95$, $\sigma_{\text{temp,no}}^2 = 0.0225$
\item Similar for blood pressure
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Test}: Patient with temp=38.0, BP=135. Compute both posteriors and predict the larger.
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Multinomial Naive Bayes}

For count data (e.g., word frequencies in documents).

\begin{definition}[Multinomial Naive Bayes]
Each $x_j$ is a token count. Conditional on class and document length $N=\sum_jx_j$, token positions are modeled as independent categorical draws; the resulting counts are multinomial and are not mutually independent for fixed $N$. Model:
\[
P(\vect{x} | y=c) = \frac{\left(\sum_j x_j\right)!}{\prod_j x_j!} \prod_{j=1}^d \theta_{jc}^{x_j}
\]
where $\theta_{jc} = P(\text{feature } j | y=c)$.

\textbf{Parameter estimation} (with Laplace smoothing):
\[
\theta_{jc} = \frac{\text{count}(x_j, y=c) + \alpha}{\sum_{j'} \text{count}(x_{j'}, y=c) + \alpha d}
\]
$\alpha > 0$ is a smoothing parameter (typically $\alpha=1$).
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
Laplace smoothing prevents zero probabilities. If a word never appears in spam training data, we still assign it a small non-zero probability rather than zero (which would make the entire product zero).
\end{intuition}

\begin{example}[Text Classification]
Classify documents as ``sports'' or ``politics''.

\textbf{Vocabulary}: \{game, team, vote, election, win\}

\textbf{Training}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Sports docs: ``game win'', ``team game'', ``team win win''
\item Politics docs: ``vote election'', ``election win''
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Word counts by class}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Sports: game=2, team=2, vote=0, election=0, win=3 (7 tokens)
\item Politics: game=0, team=0, vote=1, election=2, win=1
\end{itemize}

With $\alpha=1$:
\[
\theta_{\text{game,sports}} = \frac{2+1}{7+5} = \frac{3}{12} = 0.25
\]

\textbf{Test}: ``game election''. The document priors are $3/5$ and $2/5$. Omitting the common multinomial coefficient, the two scores are
\[
s_{\mathrm{sports}}=\frac35\frac3{12}\frac1{12}=\frac1{80},\qquad
s_{\mathrm{politics}}=\frac25\frac1{9}\frac3{9}=\frac2{135}.
\]
Normalizing gives $P(\mathrm{sports}\mid\mathrm{doc})=27/59\approx0.458$ and $P(\mathrm{politics}\mid\mathrm{doc})=32/59\approx0.542$, so predict politics.
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Bernoulli Naive Bayes}

For binary features (presence/absence).

\begin{definition}[Bernoulli Naive Bayes]
Each $x_j \in \{0, 1\}$ indicates feature presence:
\[
P(x_j | y=c) = \theta_{jc}^{x_j}(1-\theta_{jc})^{1-x_j}
\]
where $\theta_{jc} = P(x_j=1 | y=c)$.

\textbf{Estimation}:
\[
\theta_{jc} = \frac{\text{count}(x_j=1, y=c) + \alpha}{\text{count}(y=c) + 2\alpha}
\]
\end{definition}

\textbf{Counts vs presence}: Multinomial NB uses word counts. Bernoulli NB uses presence and absence: ``Does word $j$ appear?''

\subsection{Likelihood Derivations and Completed Probability Calculations}
\label{sec:nb-worked}
For class $c$, the labeled-data log-likelihood separates into $n_c\log\pi_c$ and one feature likelihood per class. Maximizing the prior terms subject to $\sum_c\pi_c=1$ gives $\hat\pi_c=n_c/n$. For a Gaussian feature, differentiation gives
\[
\hat\mu_{cj}=\frac1{n_c}\sum_{i:y_i=c}x_{ij},\qquad
\hat\sigma_{cj}^2=\frac1{n_c}\sum_{i:y_i=c}(x_{ij}-\hat\mu_{cj})^2.
\]
The divisor is $n_c$ for MLE, not $n_c-1$. With one example or a constant feature in a class, the estimate is zero and the density formula is degenerate; a variance floor or smoothing rule is needed, fitted using training data only.

\textbf{Why add-one smoothing is a predictive calculation.} For multinomial parameters $\vect\theta$ with Dirichlet prior $\operatorname{Dirichlet}(\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_V)$ and counts $N_j$, the posterior is $\operatorname{Dirichlet}(N_j+\alpha_j)$. Integrating the next token gives predictive probability $(N_j+\alpha_j)/(N+\sum_k\alpha_k)$. Symmetric $\alpha_j=1$ gives add-one smoothing. This is a posterior mean/predictive probability, not the posterior mode: when an interior mode exists, that mode subtracts one from every posterior parameter before normalization.

\begin{example}[Finishing the medical Gaussian NB example]
For the training observations above, the Disease=Yes feature means and MLE variances are
\[
\vect\mu_{\rm yes}=(38.733333,142.333333),\quad
\vect\sigma^2_{\rm yes}=(0.042222,4.222222),
\]
while Disease=No has $\vect\mu_{\rm no}=(36.95,119)$ and $\vect\sigma^2_{\rm no}=(0.0225,1)$. At test input $(38,135)$, compute each score as
\[
s_c=\log\pi_c-\frac12\sum_{j=1}^2\left[\log(2\pi\sigma_{cj}^2)+\frac{(x_j-\mu_{cj})^2}{\sigma_{cj}^2}\right].
\]
With priors $0.6,0.4$, this gives $s_{\rm yes}=-14.223321$ and $s_{\rm no}=-153.357048$. Hence the predicted class is yes; log posterior odds are $139.133727$. Normalize stably by subtracting the largest score before exponentiating. The posterior is numerically extremely close to one under this fitted model, not evidence of real-world certainty: only five training observations and very small fitted variances make this a deliberately fragile illustration, not a medical decision rule.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Multinomial and Bernoulli NB give different answers]
Reuse the five-document sports/politics corpus and vocabulary order (game, team, vote, election, win). The multinomial calculation above gives posterior sports probability $27/59\approx0.457627$ for ``game election''. It counts tokens and ignores the combinatorial coefficient when comparing classes because it is common to both scores.

For Bernoulli NB, count document presence rather than token frequency and include absent words. Add-one probabilities are
\[
\vect\theta_S=(3/5,3/5,1/5,1/5,3/5),\qquad
\vect\theta_P=(1/4,1/4,1/2,3/4,1/2).
\]
The query presence vector is $(1,0,0,1,0)$. Its two unnormalized scores, including the absence factors, are
\begin{align*}
s_S&=\tfrac35\cdot\tfrac35\cdot\tfrac25\cdot\tfrac45\cdot\tfrac15\cdot\tfrac25
=\frac{144}{15625},\\
s_P&=\tfrac25\cdot\tfrac14\cdot\tfrac34\cdot\tfrac12\cdot\tfrac34\cdot\tfrac12
=\frac9{640}.
\end{align*}
Thus $P(S\mid\vect x)=s_S/(s_S+s_P)=2048/5173\approx0.395902$. Repeating the word ``game'' would change a multinomial vector but not this binary vector. The model choice determines which information contributes to the likelihood.
\end{example}

For multinomial NB, the log posterior odds are linear in token counts:
\[
\log\frac{P(S\mid\vect x)}{P(P\mid\vect x)}
=\log\frac{\pi_S}{\pi_P}+\sum_j x_j\log\frac{\theta_{Sj}}{\theta_{Pj}}.
\]
A generative model can therefore induce a linear discriminative rule. Conditional independence is useful for tractability but can count correlated evidence repeatedly, so good classification accuracy need not imply well calibrated posterior probabilities.

\subsection{Applications}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Spam Filtering}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Features: words in email
\item Classes: spam / not spam
\item Use Multinomial or Bernoulli NB
\item Fast training and prediction
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Document Classification}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Categorize news articles, scientific papers
\item Large vocabularies: naive assumption helps reduce parameters
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Sentiment Analysis}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Classify reviews as positive/negative
\item Features: word frequencies or presence
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Simple, fast training and prediction
\item Works well with small training data
\item Handles high-dimensional data naturally
\item Probabilistic predictions (useful for uncertainty estimation)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Strong independence assumption rarely holds
\item Poor at capturing feature interactions
\item Probability estimates can be extreme (overconfident)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\section{Generative vs Discriminative Models}
\label{sec:generative-discriminative}
% =======================

\subsection{Key Distinction}

\begin{definition}[Discriminative Models]
Learn $P(y\mid\vect{x})$ directly or learn a decision function without modeling the input distribution. Standard SVMs learn a margin-based decision function, not posterior probabilities.

\textbf{Examples}: Logistic regression, SVM, decision trees, neural networks

\textbf{Goal}: Find the decision boundary separating classes
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Generative Models]
Learn the joint probability $P(\vect{x}, y) = P(\vect{x}|y)P(y)$ and use Bayes' rule.

\textbf{Examples}: Naive Bayes, Gaussian Discriminant Analysis, HMMs

\textbf{Goal}: Model how data is generated for each class
\end{definition}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{3cm}p{5.1cm}p{5.1cm}@{}}
\toprule
Aspect & Generative & Discriminative \\
\midrule
Learns & $P(\vect{x},y)$ & $P(y\mid\vect{x})$ or decision function \\
Input generation & Possible from the fitted joint model & Not supplied by the conditional model alone \\
Input density & Available from the joint model & Not specified by a classifier alone \\
Missing features & Can marginalize when tractable; missingness assumptions still matter & Requires an explicit handling strategy \\
Statistical assumptions & Models features as well as labels & Models conditional labels or a decision rule \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Generative and discriminative classifiers. Relative predictive accuracy depends on model specification, regularization, and sample size; neither family dominates universally.}
\end{table}

\begin{intuition}
\textbf{Generative}: ``I understand what cats and dogs look like. Let me generate examples of each and see which your image resembles.''

\textbf{Discriminative}: ``I just need to distinguish cats from dogs. Let me find the boundary that separates them best.''

Generative classifiers specify the feature distribution as well as labels. A discriminative classifier avoids that part of the modeling task. Which assumption set predicts better must be assessed with an appropriate validation protocol.
\end{intuition}

\subsection{Gaussian Discriminant Analysis (GDA)}

GDA is a generative classifier that models each class as a multivariate Gaussian.

\begin{definition}[GDA Model]
For binary classification ($y \in \{0, 1\}$):
\begin{align}
y &\sim \text{Bernoulli}(\phi) \\
\vect{x}|y=0 &\sim \mathcal{N}(\vect{\mu}_0, \mat{\Sigma}) \\
\vect{x}|y=1 &\sim \mathcal{N}(\vect{\mu}_1, \mat{\Sigma})
\end{align}
Note: We assume the \emph{same} covariance $\mat{\Sigma}$ for both classes.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[GDA Parameters]
Maximum likelihood estimates from data $\{(\vect{x}_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$:
\begin{align}
\phi &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \indicator{y_i=1}, \\
\boldsymbol{\mu}_0 &= \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n \indicator{y_i=0}\,\boldsymbol{x}_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n \indicator{y_i=0}}, \\
\boldsymbol{\mu}_1 &= \frac{\sum_{i=1}^n \indicator{y_i=1}\,\boldsymbol{x}_i}{\sum_{i=1}^n \indicator{y_i=1}}, \\
\boldsymbol{\Sigma} &= \frac{1}{n} \sum_{i=1}^n \bigl(\boldsymbol{x}_i - \boldsymbol{\mu}_{y_i}\bigr)\bigl(\boldsymbol{x}_i - \boldsymbol{\mu}_{y_i}\bigr)^\top.
\end{align}
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[GDA Decision Boundary]
The decision boundary where $P(y=1|\vect{x}) = P(y=0|\vect{x})$ is linear:
\[
\vect{w}^\top\vect{x} + w_0 = 0
\]
where $\vect{w} = \mat{\Sigma}^{-1}(\vect{\mu}_1 - \vect{\mu}_0)$ and
\[
w_0=-\tfrac12\vect{\mu}_1^\top\mat{\Sigma}^{-1}\vect{\mu}_1
+\tfrac12\vect{\mu}_0^\top\mat{\Sigma}^{-1}\vect{\mu}_0
+\log\frac{\phi}{1-\phi}.
\]
Here $0<\phi<1$ and the shared covariance is positive definite.

This shows GDA produces a linear classifier, similar to logistic regression!
\end{theorem}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{GDA vs Logistic Regression}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Both produce linear decision boundaries
\item GDA makes stronger assumptions (Gaussian distributions)
\item Correct Gaussian assumptions can make GDA statistically efficient; the finite-sample comparison depends on the data and estimator
\item Logistic regression avoids modeling the marginal feature distribution, but is still sensitive to misspecification of the conditional log-odds
\item Shared-covariance GDA implies a logistic conditional probability; fitting the joint and conditional likelihoods generally gives different estimates
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Rule of thumb}: Try both. If data looks roughly Gaussian, GDA may work better. Otherwise, prefer logistic regression.
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Expanding the Joint Likelihood and the Boundary}
Let $\vect r_i=\vect x_i-\vect\mu_{y_i}$, and $\mat S=\sum_i\vect r_i\vect r_i^\top$. Up to a constant the joint log-likelihood is
\[
\ell=\sum_i\{y_i\log\phi+(1-y_i)\log(1-\phi)\}
-\frac n2\log|\mat\Sigma|-\frac12\operatorname{tr}(\mat\Sigma^{-1}\mat S).
\]
The class-prior derivative gives $\hat\phi=n_1/n$. Each mean derivative is $\mat\Sigma^{-1}\sum_{i:y_i=c}(\vect x_i-\vect\mu_c)$, which yields its class sample mean. Writing precision $\mat\Omega=\mat\Sigma^{-1}$, the covariance-dependent part is $(n/2)\log|\mat\Omega|-\operatorname{tr}(\mat\Omega\mat S)/2$. Its derivative $n\mat\Omega^{-1}/2-\mat S/2=0$ gives $\hat{\mat\Sigma}=\mat S/n$. These density calculations require a positive-definite covariance; a singular empirical estimate requires a regularized model.

Bayes' rule cancels the marginal $p(\vect x)$ in posterior odds. Equal covariance also cancels the log-determinants and the $\vect x^\top\mat\Sigma^{-1}\vect x$ terms:
\begin{align*}
\log\frac{P(1\mid\vect x)}{P(0\mid\vect x)}
&=\log\frac\phi{1-\phi}-\tfrac12\bigl[(\vect x-\vect\mu_1)^\top\mat\Sigma^{-1}(\vect x-\vect\mu_1)\\
&\hspace{42mm}-(\vect x-\vect\mu_0)^\top\mat\Sigma^{-1}(\vect x-\vect\mu_0)\bigr]\\
&=\vect w^\top\vect x+w_0.
\end{align*}
Solving these log-odds for the probability gives $P(1\mid\vect x)=\sigma(\vect w^\top\vect x+w_0)$. This is a conditional functional form, not a claim that conditional-likelihood and joint-likelihood estimators coincide.

\begin{example}[Fitting a two-dimensional GDA model]
Use the following class-specific observations:
\begin{align*}
\text{Class 0: }&(-1,0),(1,0),(0,-1),(0,1),\\
\text{Class 1: }&(1,0),(3,0),(2,-1),(2,1).
\end{align*}
Their means are $(0,0),(2,0)$, each within-class scatter is $2\mat I$, and the pooled MLE is $(2\mat I+2\mat I)/8=\tfrac12\mat I$. Priors are equal. The repeated coordinate $(1,0)$ with different labels represents overlapping classes. Consequently $\vect w=(4,0)$ and $w_0=-4$: the equal-posterior boundary is $x_1=1$. At $(0.75,0.2)$, log-odds are $-1$ and $P(1\mid\vect x)=\sigma(-1)=0.268941$.

If instead the class covariances are separately specified as $\mat\Sigma_0=\mat I$ and $\mat\Sigma_1=\tfrac14\mat I$, keeping the same means and priors, the QDA log-odds become
\[
\log4-\tfrac32(x_1^2+x_2^2)+8x_1-8.
\]
The determinant term is $\log4$ in two dimensions. Its zero set is the circle
$(x_1-8/3)^2+x_2^2=16/9+(2/3)\log4$. Unequal covariances leave quadratic terms rather than a linear boundary. These are changed assumptions, not new estimates from the preceding eight points.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch2/gda-qda-boundaries.pdf}
\caption{Equal-prior Gaussian models with means $(0,0),(2,0)$. Left: the fitted shared covariance $\tfrac12\mat I$ from the eight-point example. Right: the hypothetical separate covariances $\mat I,\tfrac14\mat I$. Black curves are equal-posterior boundaries; colored contours mark squared Mahalanobis distances 1 and 4. Squares and circles mark means, not training samples.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Hidden Markov Models (Brief Overview)}

\begin{definition}[Hidden Markov Model (HMM)]
A sequence model with:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Hidden states} $z_1, z_2, \ldots, z_T$ (not observed)
\item \textbf{Observations} $x_1, x_2, \ldots, x_T$ (observed)
\item \textbf{Transition probabilities}: $P(z_t | z_{t-1})$
\item \textbf{Emission probabilities}: $P(x_t | z_t)$
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
Think of speech recognition: hidden states are phonemes (sound units), observations are audio features. We observe the audio but want to infer the underlying phoneme sequence.
\end{intuition}

\textbf{Key algorithms}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Forward-Backward}: Compute $P(\vect{x})$ (likelihood)
\item \textbf{Viterbi}: Find most likely hidden state sequence $\argmax_{\vect{z}} P(\vect{z}|\vect{x})$
\item \textbf{Baum-Welch}: Learn parameters (EM algorithm)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Applications}: Speech recognition, part-of-speech tagging, bioinformatics (gene prediction).

\begin{remark}
The worked example below covers inference with fixed parameters. Learning the parameters with Baum--Welch requires an additional EM derivation. RNNs and Transformers in Part 4 provide other approaches to sequence modeling.
\end{remark}

% =======================
\subsubsection{Forward, Backward, and Viterbi: One Complete Sequence}
Specify initial probabilities $\pi_j=P(z_1=j)$, transitions $A_{ij}=P(z_t=j\mid z_{t-1}=i)$, and emission probabilities $B_j(x)=P(x_t=x\mid z_t=j)$. The first-order Markov and conditional-emission assumptions give
\[
p(\vect z,\vect x)=\pi_{z_1}B_{z_1}(x_1)\prod_{t=2}^T A_{z_{t-1},z_t}B_{z_t}(x_t).
\]
Summing this joint over every path is exponential in $T$; dynamic programming reuses common prefixes. Define
\begin{align*}
\alpha_1(j)&=\pi_jB_j(x_1),&
\alpha_t(j)&=B_j(x_t)\sum_i\alpha_{t-1}(i)A_{ij},\\
\delta_1(j)&=\pi_jB_j(x_1),&
\delta_t(j)&=B_j(x_t)\max_i\delta_{t-1}(i)A_{ij}.
\end{align*}
Forward values sum joint probabilities; Viterbi values retain the best prefix and its maximizing predecessor. The likelihood is $\sum_j\alpha_T(j)$, while the best-path joint probability is $\max_j\delta_T(j)$. Backtracking the stored predecessors recovers the path. Both recurrences cost $O(TK^2)$ for $K$ dense states. For long sequences, use scaled forward values or log-sum-exp; Viterbi uses sums of log probabilities and maxima.

\begin{example}[Two hidden states and three observations]
Let states be $R,S$, symbols $a,b$, and rows denote the previous state for transitions:
\[
\vect\pi=(0.6,0.4),\quad
\mat A=\begin{bmatrix}.7&.3\\.4&.6\end{bmatrix},\quad
\mat B=\begin{bmatrix}.5&.5\\.1&.9\end{bmatrix}.
\]
Columns of $\mat B$ correspond to $a,b$. Observe $(a,b,a)$. For example,
$\alpha_2(R)=.5(.3\cdot.7+.04\cdot.4)=.113$, whereas
$\delta_2(R)=.5\max(.3\cdot.7,.04\cdot.4)=.105$.
The full tables are
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|rr|rr}
&\multicolumn{2}{c|}{Forward $\alpha_t$}&\multicolumn{2}{c}{Viterbi $\delta_t$}\\
$t$&$R$&$S$&$R$&$S$\\\hline
1&.300000&.040000&.300000&.040000\\
2&.113000&.102600&.105000&.081000\\
3&.060070&.009546&.036750&.004860
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Thus $p(a,b,a)=0.069616$. The most likely path is $RRR$ with joint probability $0.03675$; its posterior path probability is $0.03675/0.069616\approx0.527896$. The likelihood includes all eight paths, so it is larger than the single best path's joint probability.

For state marginals, define $\beta_T(i)=1$ and
$\beta_t(i)=\sum_j A_{ij}B_j(x_{t+1})\beta_{t+1}(j)$. Here $\beta_2=(.38,.26)$. Combining both directions gives
\[
P(z_2=R\mid a,b,a)=\frac{\alpha_2(R)\beta_2(R)}{p(a,b,a)}
=\frac{.113\cdot.38}{.069616}=0.616812.
\]
The marginal asks about one time point while summing the other states; Viterbi optimizes an entire joint path. Choosing each marginal mode independently need not produce the best joint path.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small}]
\foreach \i/\s in {1/a,2/b,3/a}{
\node[draw,circle] (z\i) at ({2.5*(\i-1)},1.3) {$z_\i$};
\node[draw,rectangle,fill=blue!10] (x\i) at ({2.5*(\i-1)},0) {$x_\i=\s$};
\draw[->] (z\i)--(x\i);
}
\draw[->] (z1)--node[above]{$\mat A$}(z2);\draw[->] (z2)--node[above]{$\mat A$}(z3);
\node[left] at (-.5,1.3) {$\vect\pi$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The three-step HMM factorization. Arrows encode the model's conditional dependencies; the shaded observation nodes are known. Future observations affect posterior state inference through the backward messages, without becoming available as forecasting features at an earlier time.}
\end{figure}

\section{Support Vector Machines}
\label{sec:svm}
% =======================

\subsection{Intuition: Maximum Margin Classification}

Logistic regression minimizes logistic loss (with regularization when specified). SVM instead optimizes a margin-based objective; hard-margin SVM selects the largest-margin separator when the data are linearly separable.

\begin{definition}[Margin]
The \emph{margin} is the distance from the decision boundary to the nearest data point.

A \emph{maximum margin classifier} maximizes this distance.
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
Imagine two classes of points that are linearly separable. Many hyperplanes separate them, but intuitively, the maximum-margin choice maximizes the smallest training-point distance in the chosen Euclidean geometry. This protects those training decisions against sufficiently small input perturbations; it does not alone guarantee lower test error or robustness to label noise.
\end{intuition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1]
\draw[->] (0,0)--(6.3,0) node[right] {$x_1$};
\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,4.5) node[above] {$x_2$};
\foreach \x/\y in {4/1,4/3,5/1.5,5/3.5}
  \fill[blue] (\x,\y) circle (2.5pt);
\foreach \x/\y in {2/1,2/3,1/1.5,1/3.5}
  \fill[orange!80!black] (\x,\y) circle (2.5pt);
\draw[very thick] (3,0.3)--(3,4.1);
\draw[dashed] (2,0.3)--(2,4.1);
\draw[dashed] (4,0.3)--(4,4.1);
\foreach \x/\y in {2/1,2/3,4/1,4/3}
  \draw[thick] (\x,\y) circle (5pt);
\draw[<->,thick] (3,2.1)--(4,2.1) node[midway,above] {$1$};
\node[orange!80!black] at (1.5,4.5) {Class $-1$};
\node[blue] at (4.7,4.5) {Class $+1$};
\node[anchor=north] at (3,-0.5) {$\vect{w}=(1,0)^\top,\quad b=-3,\quad \gamma=1/\norm{\vect{w}}=1$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{An exact hard-margin solution: the boundary is $x_1=3$, margin lines are $x_1=2,4$, and circled points lie on the margin. The arrow is perpendicular to the boundary.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Mathematical Formulation (Hard Margin)}

\begin{definition}[Linear Classifier]
Decision function: $f(\vect{x}) = \vect{w}^\top\vect{x} + b$

Prediction: $\hat{y} = \text{sign}(f(\vect{x})) = \begin{cases} +1 & \text{if } f(\vect{x}) \geq 0 \\ -1 & \text{if } f(\vect{x}) < 0 \end{cases}$
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Functional and Geometric Margin]
\textbf{Functional margin} of $(\vect{w}, b)$ with respect to $(\vect{x}_i, y_i)$:
\[
\hat{\gamma}_i = y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b)
\]
Positive for a strictly correct score; a zero score follows the declared tie rule and has zero margin.

\textbf{Geometric margin} (actual distance to hyperplane):
\[
\gamma_i = \frac{y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b)}{\norm{\vect{w}}}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Hard Margin SVM (Primal Form)]
To maximize the margin:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\max_{\vect{w}, b} \quad & \frac{1}{\norm{\vect{w}}} \\
\text{subject to} \quad & y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \geq 1, \quad i = 1, \ldots, n
\end{aligned}
\]
Equivalently (minimization form):
\[
\begin{aligned}
\min_{\vect{w}, b} \quad & \frac{1}{2}\norm{\vect{w}}^2 \\
\text{subject to} \quad & y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \geq 1, \quad i = 1, \ldots, n
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{intuition}
The constraint $y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \geq 1$ ensures all points are correctly classified and at least distance $1/\norm{\vect{w}}$ from the boundary. Minimizing $\norm{\vect{w}}^2$ maximizes the margin $1/\norm{\vect{w}}$.
\end{intuition}

\subsection{Lagrange Duality and the Dual Form}

The primal is a constrained optimization problem. We use Lagrange multipliers.

\begin{definition}[Lagrangian]
\begin{equation}\label{eq:svm-lagrangian}
\mathcal{L}(\vect{w}, b, \vect{\alpha}) = \frac{1}{2}\norm{\vect{w}}^2 - \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i[y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) - 1]
\end{equation}
where $\alpha_i \geq 0$ are Lagrange multipliers.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[KKT Conditions]
At the optimum:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\nabla_{\vect{w}} \mathcal{L} = 0 \implies \vect{w} = \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i \vect{x}_i$
\item $\pp{\mathcal{L}}{b} = 0 \implies \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i = 0$
\item $y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i+b)\geq1$ (primal feasibility)
\item $\alpha_i \geq 0$ (dual feasibility)
\item $\alpha_i [y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) - 1] = 0$ (complementary slackness)
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{keyidea}
The complementary slackness condition is crucial:
\begin{itemize}
\item If $\alpha_i > 0$, then $y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) = 1$ (point is on the margin)
\item These points are called \textbf{support vectors}
\item Only support vectors affect the decision boundary!
\item Most $\alpha_i = 0$ $\to$ sparse solution
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\begin{theorem}[Dual Form]
Substituting the KKT conditions into the Lagrangian:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:svm-dual}
\begin{aligned}
\max_{\vect{\alpha}} \quad & \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{j=1}^n \alpha_i\alpha_j y_i y_j \vect{x}_i^\top\vect{x}_j \\
\text{subject to} \quad & \alpha_i \geq 0, \quad i = 1, \ldots, n \\
& \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i = 0
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
This is a \emph{quadratic programming} problem in $\vect{\alpha}$.
\end{theorem}

\textbf{Why dual is useful}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Only depends on dot products $\vect{x}_i^\top\vect{x}_j$ (enables kernel trick!)
\item Sparse solution (most $\alpha_i = 0$)
\item Once solved, prediction: $f(\vect{x}) = \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i \vect{x}_i^\top\vect{x} + b$
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Distance, Duality, and a Solved Hard-Margin Problem}
The closest point on $\vect w^\top\vect x+b=0$ to $\vect x$ is
$\vect x_{\rm proj}=\vect x-(\vect w^\top\vect x+b)\vect w/\|\vect w\|^2$.
Substitution verifies that it lies on the hyperplane; the displacement is normal to it. The distance is therefore $|\vect w^\top\vect x+b|/\|\vect w\|$. Rescaling a separating $(\vect w,b)$ makes the smallest functional margin $y_i(\vect w^\top\vect x_i+b)$ equal to one. The one-sided geometric margin is then $1/\|\vect w\|$, while the full band between the $+1$ and $-1$ hyperplanes has width $2/\|\vect w\|$.

Starting from Equation~\eqref{eq:svm-lagrangian}, stationarity gives
$\vect w=\sum_i\alpha_i y_i\vect x_i$ and $\sum_i\alpha_i y_i=0$.
Substitution cancels the $b$ term and combines the quadratic terms into
Equation~\eqref{eq:svm-dual}.
For separable finite data, scaling a separator makes every constraint strict, giving Slater's condition. Convexity and this strict feasibility justify strong duality and the sufficiency of KKT conditions. Complementary slackness implies that $\alpha_i>0$ only when the point lies on a canonical margin; a margin point can still have zero multiplier in a degenerate solution.

\begin{example}[Solving both the primal and the dual]
Use the one-dimensional dataset
\[
(x_i,y_i)\in\{(1,+1),(2,+1),(4,-1),(5,-1)\}.
\]
This is a different dataset from the earlier two-dimensional illustration. The closest opposing examples are 2 and 4. Solving $2w+b=1$ and $4w+b=-1$ yields $w=-1,b=3$, geometric margin 1, and band width 2. Multipliers $(0,1/2,1/2,0)$ satisfy $\sum\alpha_i y_i=0$ and $w=\sum\alpha_i y_i x_i=-1$. Every primal constraint and complementary-slackness equation holds. The primal objective is $1/2$; the dual is $1-(1/2)(-1)^2=1/2$, certifying optimality. A query $x=3.5$ has score $-0.5$ and predicted label $-1$.
\end{example}

\subsection{The Kernel Trick}

\textbf{Problem}: What if data is not linearly separable?

\textbf{Approach}: Map inputs to a feature space in which a linear separator can represent a nonlinear boundary in the original coordinates. Separability is not guaranteed; conflicting labels at identical inputs, for example, still require a soft margin.

\begin{definition}[Feature Map]
Let $\vect{\phi}: \R^d \to \R^D$ (with $D \gg d$) be a feature map.

Transform: $\vect{x} \to \vect{\phi}(\vect{x})$

Run SVM in the transformed space: $f(\vect{x}) = \vect{w}^\top\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}) + b$
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Simple Kernel]
For $\vect{x} = [x_1, x_2]^\top$, define:
\[
\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}) = [1, \sqrt{2}x_1, \sqrt{2}x_2, x_1^2, \sqrt{2}x_1x_2, x_2^2]^\top
\]
This maps 2D to 6D. Data that's not linearly separable in 2D might be in 6D!
\end{example}

\textbf{Issue}: Computing $\vect{\phi}(\vect{x})$ explicitly for high $D$ is expensive.

\begin{theorem}[Kernel Trick]
We only need dot products $\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}_i)^\top\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}_j)$ in the dual.

Define \textbf{kernel function}: $K(\vect{x}_i, \vect{x}_j) = \vect{\phi}(\vect{x}_i)^\top\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}_j)$

If we can compute $K$ directly without computing $\vect{\phi}$ explicitly, we get efficiency!
\end{theorem}

\begin{definition}[Common Kernels]
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Linear}: $K(\vect{x}, \vect{z}) = \vect{x}^\top\vect{z}$

\item \textbf{Polynomial}: $K(\vect{x},\vect{z})=(\vect{x}^\top\vect{z}+c)^p$ with nonnegative $c$ and positive integer $p$

For $c>0$ the feature map contains monomials through degree $p$; for $c=0$ it contains only homogeneous degree-$p$ monomials.

\item \textbf{RBF (Radial Basis Function / Gaussian)}: 
\[
K(\vect{x}, \vect{z}) = \exp\left(-\gamma\norm{\vect{x} - \vect{z}}^2\right)
\]
where $\gamma > 0$ is a bandwidth parameter.

This corresponds to \emph{infinite-dimensional} feature space!

\item \textbf{Sigmoid form}: $K(\vect{x},\vect{z})=\tanh(\alpha\vect{x}^\top\vect{z}+c)$ is not positive semidefinite for arbitrary parameters and domains. A valid kernel must produce positive-semidefinite Gram matrices; the standard convex kernel-SVM theory requires this condition.
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{The kernel trick magic}:

The polynomial kernel $(x^\top z + 1)^2$ computes the dot product in a space of monomials of total degree at most two:
\[
(x^\top z + 1)^2 = (x_1z_1 + x_2z_2 + 1)^2 = x_1^2z_1^2 + 2x_1x_2z_1z_2 + x_2^2z_2^2 + 2x_1z_1 + 2x_2z_2 + 1
\]
This equals $\vect{\phi}(\vect{x})^\top\vect{\phi}(\vect{z})$ with $\vect{\phi}(\vect{x}) = [1, \sqrt{2}x_1, \sqrt{2}x_2, x_1^2, \sqrt{2}x_1x_2, x_2^2]^\top$.

We computed this in $O(d)$ time instead of $O(D)$ where $D=\binom{d+2}{2}$!

For RBF kernel: we operate in infinite dimensions without ever computing infinite-dimensional vectors!
\end{keyidea}

\begin{theorem}[Kernelized Dual]
With kernel $K$:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\max_{\vect{\alpha}} \quad & \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i - \frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{j=1}^n \alpha_i\alpha_j y_i y_j K(\vect{x}_i, \vect{x}_j) \\
\text{subject to} \quad & \alpha_i \geq 0, \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i = 0
\end{aligned}
\]
Prediction: $f(\vect{x}) = \sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i y_i K(\vect{x}_i, \vect{x}) + b$
\end{theorem}

\subsubsection{An Explicit Kernel Calculation on XOR}
\begin{example}[Features, Gram matrix, and multipliers]
Use the four input points
\[
\vect x\in\{(-1,-1),(-1,1),(1,-1),(1,1)\},
\]
with labels $(-1,+1,+1,-1)$, so $y=-x_1x_2$. No affine line separates opposite corners from the other pair. The interaction $u=x_1x_2$ does separate them with score $-u$.

For $K(\vect x,\vect z)=(\vect x^\top\vect z+1)^2$, one explicit feature map is
\[
\phi(\vect x)=(1,\sqrt2x_1,\sqrt2x_2,x_1^2,\sqrt2x_1x_2,x_2^2)^\top.
\]
Expanding the inner product verifies the kernel identity. In the stated order, the Gram matrix is
\[
\mat K=\begin{bmatrix}9&1&1&1\\1&9&1&1\\1&1&9&1\\1&1&1&9\end{bmatrix}=8\mat I+\vect1\vect1^\top.
\]
Its eigenvalues are $12,8,8,8$, all positive. Multipliers $\alpha_i=1/8$ and $b=0$ give training scores $\sum_i\alpha_i y_i K(\vect x_i,\vect x_j)=y_j$. The corresponding feature-space weight has only the interaction coefficient nonzero, equal to $-1/\sqrt2$. Its norm squared is $1/2$; primal and dual objectives both equal $1/4$, proving a hard-margin optimum.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small}]
\begin{scope}[scale=1.2]
\draw[->] (-1.5,0)--(1.6,0) node[right]{$x_1$};\draw[->] (0,-1.5)--(0,1.6) node[above]{$x_2$};
\foreach \x/\y in {-1/1,1/-1}{\fill[blue!70] (\x,\y) circle (3pt);}
\foreach \x/\y in {-1/-1,1/1}{\fill[orange!80!black] ({\x-.06},{\y-.06}) rectangle ({\x+.06},{\y+.06});}
\end{scope}
\draw[->,thick] (2.3,.4)--node[above]{$u=x_1x_2$}(4,.4);
\draw[->] (4.5,0)--(8.3,0) node[right]{$u$};
\draw[dashed] (6.4,-.8)--(6.4,1);
\fill[blue!70] (5.2,0) circle (3pt);
\node[below=6pt,text width=2.3cm] at (5.2,0) {$u=-1$\\two positives};
\fill[orange!80!black] (7.54,-.06) rectangle (7.66,.06);
\node[below=6pt,text width=2.3cm] at (7.6,0) {$u=1$\\two negatives};
\node[above] at (6.4,1) {boundary $u=0$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{XOR becomes separable after an interaction feature. Overlapping mapped observations are labeled with their multiplicity; they have not disappeared from the optimization problem.}
\end{figure}
Pairwise evaluation can avoid constructing a high-dimensional feature vector, but a dense Gram matrix still requires $O(n^2)$ entries. A kernel trick removes explicit feature expansion, not the dependence on the number of training examples.

\subsection{Soft Margin SVM}

\textbf{Problem}: Real data is rarely perfectly separable. Hard-margin SVM has no feasible solution if no affine separator satisfies every margin constraint.

\textbf{Solution}: Allow some misclassifications with penalty.

\begin{definition}[Slack Variables]
Introduce $\xi_i\geq0$ for each point. At an optimum with $C>0$, $\xi_i=\max(0,1-y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i+b))$, so:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\xi_i = 0$: Point correctly classified with margin $\geq 1$
\item $0 < \xi_i < 1$: Correctly classified but within margin
\item $\xi_i=1$: On the decision boundary (classification depends on the tie convention)
\item $\xi_i>1$: Strictly misclassified
\end{itemize}
Relaxed constraint: $y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \geq 1 - \xi_i$
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Soft Margin SVM]
\[
\begin{aligned}
\min_{\vect{w}, b, \vect{\xi}} \quad & \frac{1}{2}\norm{\vect{w}}^2 + C\sum_{i=1}^n \xi_i \\
\text{subject to} \quad & y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \geq 1 - \xi_i \\
& \xi_i \geq 0, \quad i = 1, \ldots, n
\end{aligned}
\]
$C > 0$ is a hyperparameter controlling the trade-off:
\begin{itemize}
\item Large $C$: Fewer violations allowed (closer to hard margin, may overfit)
\item Small $C$: More violations allowed (more regularization, may underfit)
\end{itemize}
\end{theorem}

\begin{intuition}
Soft margin balances two goals:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Maximize margin (minimize $\norm{\vect{w}}^2$)
\item Minimize hinge-margin violations (minimize $\sum \xi_i$); these are not the number of classification errors
\end{enumerate}
Parameter $C$ controls this trade-off. Use cross-validation to choose $C$.
\end{intuition}

\begin{definition}[Hinge Loss Formulation]
Soft margin SVM can be written as:
\[
\min_{\vect{w}, b} \frac{1}{2}\norm{\vect{w}}^2 + C\sum_{i=1}^n \max(0, 1 - y_i(\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b))
\]
where $\ell_{\text{hinge}}(z) = \max(0, 1-z)$ is the hinge loss.

This connects SVM to other loss functions (logistic loss, squared loss, etc.).
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Slack Variables, Hinge Loss, and the Meaning of \texorpdfstring{$C$}{C}}
At fixed $\vect w,b$, the least feasible slack is $\xi_i=\max(0,1-y_if_i)$ because $C>0$ penalizes every extra unit. Eliminating slacks gives
\[
\tfrac12\|\vect w\|^2+C\sum_i\max(0,1-y_if_i).
\]
Dividing by $nC$ gives the equivalent average-hinge objective $\lambda\|\vect w\|^2/2+\frac1n\sum_i\max(0,1-y_if_i)$ with $\lambda=1/(nC)$. This conversion assumes the sum-loss convention above.

In the constrained Lagrangian, let $\mu_i\ge0$ multiply $-\xi_i$. Stationarity in $\xi_i$ gives $C-\alpha_i-\mu_i=0$, hence $0\le\alpha_i\le C$. Complementary slackness implies: if $0<\alpha_i<C$, then $\xi_i=0$ and $y_if_i=1$; if $\alpha_i=C$, the point may violate the margin, including being misclassified, but need not do so. A point with $\alpha_i=0$ has no dual contribution. Recover $b=y_i-\vect w^\top\vect x_i$ from a free support vector with $0<\alpha_i<C$; if none exists, use the KKT bounds on $b$ rather than assuming an arbitrary support vector has zero slack.

\begin{example}[Small $C$ permits margin violations without classification errors]
On the preceding four-point one-dimensional dataset, take $C=0.05$. One optimum is $w=-0.3,b=0.9$, giving scores $(0.6,0.3,-0.3,-0.6)$ and slacks $(0.4,0.7,0.7,0.4)$. All four labels are correct, yet every point is inside the canonical margin. Set all multipliers to $0.05$. They satisfy the box constraints and label-balance constraint, and imply $w=\sum_i\alpha_i y_ix_i=-0.3$. The primal value is $0.045+0.05(2.2)=0.155$ and the dual value is $0.2-0.045=0.155$. This certifies optimality; the unpenalized intercept is not unique here.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=.8\linewidth,height=5cm,xmin=-2,xmax=3,ymin=0,ymax=3.2,xlabel={signed margin $m=yf(\vect x)$},ylabel={per-example loss},legend pos=north east,samples=100]
\addplot[blue!70,thick,domain=-2:3] {max(0,1-x)};\addlegendentry{Hinge $\max(0,1-m)$}
\addplot[orange!80!black,dashed,thick,domain=-2:3] {ln(1+exp(-x))};\addlegendentry{Logistic $\log(1+e^{-m})$}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Hinge and logistic losses as functions of a signed margin with labels in $\{-1,+1\}$. Logistic cross-entropy continues rewarding larger positive margins; hinge loss becomes zero at margin one. A raw SVM score is not a posterior probability. Any fitted probability calibration needs held-out or out-of-fold data.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Support Vector Regression (SVR)}

SVM can also be used for regression!

\begin{definition}[$\epsilon$-insensitive Loss]
\[
\ell_\epsilon(y, \hat{y}) = \begin{cases}
0 & \text{if } |y - \hat{y}| < \epsilon \\
|y - \hat{y}| - \epsilon & \text{otherwise}
\end{cases}
\]
No penalty for predictions within $\epsilon$ of the true value.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[SVR Formulation]
\[
\begin{aligned}
\min_{\vect{w}, b,\vect{\xi},\vect{\xi}^*} \quad & \frac{1}{2}\norm{\vect{w}}^2 + C\sum_{i=1}^n (\xi_i + \xi_i^*)\\
\text{subject to} \quad & y_i - (\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) \leq \epsilon + \xi_i \\
& (\vect{w}^\top\vect{x}_i + b) - y_i \leq \epsilon + \xi_i^* \\
& \xi_i, \xi_i^* \geq 0
\end{aligned}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
SVR fits a ``tube'' of width $2\epsilon$ around the data. Points inside the tube have no penalty. Points outside pay a linear penalty proportional to their distance from the tube.
\end{intuition}

\subsubsection{Checking an \texorpdfstring{$\epsilon$}{epsilon}-Insensitive Regression Candidate}
For residual $r_i=y_i-f(x_i)$, the least feasible slacks are $\xi_i=\max(0,r_i-\epsilon)$ and $\xi_i^*=\max(0,-r_i-\epsilon)$. Their sum is $\max(0,|r_i|-\epsilon)$. For candidate $f(x)=x$, observations $(0,0.2),(1,1.8),(2,1.6)$, and $\epsilon=0.5$, residuals are $(0.2,0.8,-0.4)$ and losses are $(0,0.3,0)$. With $C=1$, its primal objective is $\tfrac12(1)^2+0.3=0.8$. This evaluates a candidate; it does not claim that $f(x)=x$ minimizes the objective over all lines.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=.8\linewidth,height=5cm,xmin=-.3,xmax=2.4,ymin=-.7,ymax=3,xlabel={$x$},ylabel={$y$},legend pos=north west]
\addplot[blue!70,thick,domain=-.2:2.3] {x};\addlegendentry{Candidate $f(x)=x$}
\addplot[black,dashed,domain=-.2:2.3] {x+.5};\addlegendentry{$\epsilon$-tube boundaries}
\addplot[black,dashed,domain=-.2:2.3] {x-.5};
\addplot[only marks,mark=*,orange!80!black] coordinates {(0,.2)(1,1.8)(2,1.6)};
\addplot[orange!80!black,very thick] coordinates {(1,1.5)(1,1.8)};
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Only the middle observation lies outside the $\epsilon=0.5$ tube around this candidate line. Its excess distance is $0.3$; the other two have zero $\epsilon$-insensitive loss.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Practical Considerations}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Choosing SVM Parameters}:

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Kernel}: Start with RBF (most flexible). Try linear if $d$ is large.
\item \textbf{$C$}: Controls regularization. Use grid search + CV.
\item \textbf{$\gamma$ (for RBF)}: Controls kernel width. Small $\gamma$ = wide kernel (smooth), large $\gamma$ = narrow kernel (complex boundary).
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Feature Scaling}: Critical for SVMs! Since they use distances, features should be normalized (e.g., to $[0,1]$ or standardized to mean 0, std 1).

\textbf{Complexity for a dense kernel formulation}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Forming the Gram matrix takes $O(n^2d)$ work for standard $O(d)$ kernels and $O(n^2)$ storage; additional solver cost depends on the algorithm, tolerance, and data
\item Kernel prediction takes $O(n_{\text{sv}}d)$ for such kernels. A linear model can instead store its weight vector and predict in $O(d)$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use SVMs}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Medium-sized datasets (1000s to 100,000s of examples)
\item High-dimensional data (works well when $d > n$)
\item When a margin-based inductive bias suits the task; generalization bounds still require explicit assumptions
\item Non-linear decision boundaries (with kernels)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When NOT to use SVMs}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Very large datasets ($n > 1$M) - use linear models or neural networks
\item When interpretability is critical - use decision trees or linear models
\item When probabilistic outputs are essential - use logistic regression or Naive Bayes
\end{itemize}
\end{implementationbox}

The \href{https://cs229.stanford.edu/lectures-spring2022/main_notes.pdf}{CS229 lecture notes} provide further treatments of likelihood-based models and SVM duality. The sum-versus-mean loss conversions above should be checked before transferring hyperparameters between formulations.

\subsection{Summary of Part 2}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Key Takeaways]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Naive Bayes}: Generative classifier using Bayes' rule with conditional independence assumption. Fast, works well for text classification despite ``naive'' assumption.

\item \textbf{Generative vs Discriminative}: Generative classifiers model $P(\vect{x},y)$; discriminative classifiers learn $P(y\mid\vect{x})$ or a decision function. Performance and data requirements depend on the model assumptions and the task.

\item \textbf{GDA}: Gaussian class densities with a shared covariance imply linear log-odds. Separate covariances generally give quadratic boundaries (QDA).

\item \textbf{SVM}: Maximum margin classifier. Primal form maximizes margin; dual form enables kernel trick. Soft margin allows violations with penalty $C$.

\item \textbf{Kernel Trick}: Uses inner products in a feature space without explicitly constructing that space. A valid kernel has positive-semidefinite Gram matrices; the RBF kernel is a common nonlinear choice.

\item \textbf{SVR}: Adapts SVM for regression using $\epsilon$-insensitive loss. Fits tube around data.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Next Steps}: Part 3 explores ensemble methods (Bagging, Boosting, Stacking) that combine multiple models and can improve predictive performance when their errors and fitting procedures complement one another.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Given training data for spam (words: ``free''=10, ``money''=8) and ham (``free''=2, ``money''=1), with priors $P(\text{spam})=0.4$, compute $P(\text{spam}|\text{``free money''})$ using Multinomial Naive Bayes with Laplace smoothing ($\alpha=1$).

\item Prove that if we assume Gaussian distributions with same covariance for both classes (GDA), the posterior $P(y=1|\vect{x})$ has the form of logistic regression.

\item For the polynomial kernel $K(\vect{x}, \vect{z}) = (\vect{x}^\top\vect{z} + 1)^2$, explicitly write out the feature map $\vect{\phi}(\vect{x})$ for $\vect{x} \in \R^2$.

\item Show that the RBF kernel $K(\vect{x}, \vect{z}) = \exp(-\gamma\norm{\vect{x}-\vect{z}}^2)$ satisfies the property $K(\vect{x}, \vect{x}) = 1$ and $K(\vect{x}, \vect{z}) \to 0$ as $\norm{\vect{x}-\vect{z}} \to \infty$.

\item Consider a 1D SVM with data $\{(1, +1), (2, +1), (4, -1), (5, -1)\}$. Assuming hard margin is feasible, what are the support vectors?

\item Explain why feature scaling is critical for SVMs but not for decision trees.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

% =======================
% Part 3: Ensemble Methods
% =======================
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 3: Ensemble Methods}
\section*{Part 3: Ensemble Methods}

\section{Introduction to Ensemble Learning}
\label{sec:ensembles}

\begin{definition}[Ensemble Learning]
An \emph{ensemble method} combines predictions from multiple base models (\emph{learners}, not necessarily weak classifiers). Improvement depends on their individual errors, dependence, aggregation rule, and fitting procedure.
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}
``Wisdom of crowds'': Just as a group of people can make better decisions than individuals, combining multiple models can reduce errors. Different models make different mistakes; by aggregating, we can cancel out individual errors.
\end{intuition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Why Ensembles Work}:

From the bias-variance perspective:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Reduce Variance}: Average multiple high-variance models (e.g., deep trees) $\to$ more stable predictions
\item \textbf{Reduce Bias}: Sequentially build models that correct errors of previous ones $\to$ better fit
\item \textbf{Improve Robustness}: Different models capture different patterns; ensemble is less likely to fail
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Three Main Paradigms}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Bagging}: Train models independently on bootstrap samples, then average (reduces variance)
\item \textbf{Boosting}: Train models sequentially, each focusing on previous errors (reduces bias)
\item \textbf{Stacking}: Train a meta-model to combine base model predictions (learns optimal combination)
\end{enumerate}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsection{Averaging, Dependence, and the Bias That Remains}
\label{sec:ensemble-dependence}
Fix an input $\vect x$ and consider fitted predictions $h_1,\ldots,h_M$ as random variables over training samples and algorithmic randomness. For weights $\vect a$ with $\sum_m a_m=1$, the prediction is $H=\sum_m a_mh_m$ and
\[
\Var(H)=\vect a^\top\mat C\vect a,\qquad C_{rs}=\Cov(h_r,h_s).
\]
For equal weights, identical marginal variance $\sigma^2$, and common pairwise correlation $\rho$, expanding the double sum gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ensemble-variance}
\Var(H)=\frac{M\sigma^2+M(M-1)\rho\sigma^2}{M^2}
=\sigma^2\left(\rho+\frac{1-\rho}{M}\right).
\end{equation}
This derivation requires the stated covariance structure, not merely similar individual accuracies. If $\E[h_m]=f(\vect x)+b$ for every model, averaging preserves bias $b$. It cannot remove a shared systematic error.

There is a useful conditional explanation for bagging. Conditional on one training dataset $\mathcal D$, independently generated bootstrap fits may be independent over their random seeds. Unconditionally they still share $\mathcal D$. Writing $\mu_{\mathcal D}=\E[h_m\mid\mathcal D]$ and $v_{\mathcal D}=\Var(h_m\mid\mathcal D)$ gives
\[
\Var(H)=\Var_{\mathcal D}(\mu_{\mathcal D})+\frac1M\E_{\mathcal D}[v_{\mathcal D}].
\]
The shared-data term remains even with infinitely many bootstrap fits. Thus ``independent training jobs'' does not imply statistically independent prediction errors.

\begin{example}[A variance floor and a majority vote]
With $\sigma^2=4$, $\rho=1/4$, and $M=5$, variance is $4(1/4+3/20)=1.6$, compared with 4 for one model. At $M=20$ it is 1.15, and its limit is 1. For binary votes, a different calculation is needed: three independent classifiers with individual correctness probability $0.9$ have majority accuracy $0.9^3+3(0.9)^2(0.1)=0.972$. If their correctness indicators are identical, majority accuracy stays $0.9$. Averaging variance and majority-vote error are related intuitions, not interchangeable formulas.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch3/ensemble-variance.pdf}
\caption{Exact equal-variance averaging formula with $\sigma^2=4$ and three specified nonnegative correlations. These are analytic curves at a fixed input, not measured random-forest performance. Increasing the ensemble size removes only the unshared variance component.}
\end{figure}

\section{Bagging (Bootstrap Aggregating)}
\label{sec:bagging}
% =======================

\subsection{Bootstrap Sampling}

\begin{definition}[Bootstrap Sample]
Given a dataset $\mathcal{D}$ of size $n$, a \emph{bootstrap sample} $\mathcal{D}_i$ is created by:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Randomly sample $n$ examples from $\mathcal{D}$ \emph{with replacement}
\item Some examples appear multiple times, others not at all
\end{enumerate}

The exact expected fraction of original examples included is $1-(1-1/n)^n$, approaching about 63.2\% as $n$ grows.
\end{definition}

\begin{proof}[Why 63.2\%?]
Probability that example $i$ is NOT selected in one draw: $(1 - 1/n)$

Probability NOT selected in $n$ draws: $(1 - 1/n)^n$

As $n \to \infty$: $(1 - 1/n)^n \to e^{-1} \approx 0.368$

So probability of being selected: $1 - 0.368 = 0.632$ or 63.2\%.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Bagging Algorithm}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Bagging for Classification/Regression}

\textbf{Input}: 
\begin{itemize}
\item Training data $\mathcal{D} = \{(\vect{x}_i, y_i)\}_{i=1}^n$
\item Base learning algorithm $\mathcal{A}$
\item Number of models $M$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Training}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{For} $m = 1$ to $M$:
\begin{itemize}
\item Create bootstrap sample $\mathcal{D}_m$ by sampling $n$ examples from $\mathcal{D}$ with replacement
\item Train base learner: $h_m = \mathcal{A}(\mathcal{D}_m)$
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Prediction} for new example $\vect{x}$:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Classification}: $\hat{y} = \text{majority\_vote}(h_1(\vect{x}), h_2(\vect{x}), \ldots, h_M(\vect{x}))$
\item \textbf{Regression}: $\hat{y} = \frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^M h_m(\vect{x})$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Output}: Ensemble predictor $H(\vect{x})$
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{theorem}[Variance Reduction in Bagging]
Under the equal-variance and common-correlation assumptions of
Section~\ref{sec:ensemble-dependence}, the equal-weight variance is
Equation~\eqref{eq:ensemble-variance}; independence reduces it to $\sigma^2/M$.
For fixed nonnegative common correlation, its limit is $\rho\sigma^2$.
These statements concern the specified randomness and do not assert that
bootstrap predictors sharing a dataset are unconditionally independent.
\end{theorem}

\begin{keyidea}
Bagging reduces variance most when:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Base learners have high variance (e.g., deep decision trees)
\item Base learners are diverse (low correlation $\rho$)
\end{enumerate}

Potential gain depends on estimator instability and prediction dependence. Deep trees are often unstable; a linear estimator can also be unstable under a poorly conditioned design. Averaging does not remove shared bias.
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Random Forests}

Random Forests extend bagging by adding another source of randomness.

\begin{definition}[Random Forest]
A Random Forest is an ensemble of decision trees where:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Each tree is trained on a bootstrap sample (like bagging)
\item \textbf{Additional randomness}: At each split, only a random subset of $m$ features is considered (out of total $d$ features)
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\textbf{Feature subset size $m$}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Classification: $m=\max(1,\lfloor\sqrt d\rfloor)$ is common; scikit-learn defaults to \texttt{"sqrt"}
\item Regression: $m=\max(1,\lfloor d/3\rfloor)$ is a traditional heuristic. The scikit-learn regression forest defaults to \texttt{1.0}, using all $d$ features
\item Tune \texttt{max\_features} via cross-validation; see the \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.ensemble.RandomForestRegressor.html}{scikit-learn documentation} for implementation defaults.
\end{itemize}

\begin{intuition}
Why limit features at each split?

Without this, trees in bagging would be similar---they'd all split on the strongest features first. By randomly limiting choices, we force trees to consider different features, increasing diversity and decorrelating predictions.
\end{intuition}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Random Forest Algorithm}

\textbf{Input}: Data $\mathcal{D}$, number of trees $M$, feature subset size $m$

\textbf{For} $i = 1$ to $M$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Draw bootstrap sample $\mathcal{D}_i$ from $\mathcal{D}$
\item Grow tree $T_i$ on $\mathcal{D}_i$:
\begin{itemize}
\item At each node, randomly select $m$ features from $d$ total
\item Choose best split among these $m$ features only
\item Split and recurse until stopping criterion
\item Grow subject to the selected depth, leaf-size, and other tree constraints; classical forests typically use deep trees rather than post-pruning each tree
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Prediction}: Average (regression) or vote (classification) across all $M$ trees.
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,font=\small,
 leaf/.style={draw,rounded corners,text width=1.8cm,minimum height=.7cm},
 root/.style={draw,circle,minimum size=.8cm}]
\node[draw,text width=3cm] (input) at (4.3,1.7) {One new observation};
\foreach \c/\j/\p in {0/1/.51,4.3/2/.51,8.6/3/0} {
 \node[root] (t\j) at (\c,0) {$T_\j$};
 \node[leaf,fill=blue!10] (p\j) at ({\c-1},-1.5) {$p_\j=\p$};
 \node[leaf,fill=gray!8] (o\j) at ({\c+1},-1.5) {Other leaf};
 \draw[->,thick] (input)--(t\j);
 \draw[->,thick,blue!70] (t\j)--(p\j);
 \draw[->,gray,dashed] (t\j)--(o\j);
}
\node[draw,text width=10cm,align=center,fill=orange!10] (agg) at (4.3,-3.2)
 {Hard labels: $(1,1,0)$ $\Rightarrow$ majority class 1\\
 Mean probability: $(.51+.51+0)/3=.34$\\
Probability averaging: class 0 at $t=.5$};
\draw[->] (p1.south)--(agg.north west);
\draw[->] (p2.south)--(agg.north);
\draw[->] (p3.south)--(agg.north east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Binary-tree inference schematic with prescribed class-1 leaf probabilities $p_j$,
not fits to a hidden dataset. Solid paths select one leaf per tree. Hard-label
voting and probability averaging can disagree; specify the forest's aggregation
rule. Bootstrap training and feature sampling are omitted from this inference diagram.}
