Omitting centering would diagonalize the second-moment matrix $\mat\Sigma+\bar{\vect x}\bar{\vect x}^\top=\left[\begin{smallmatrix}6&3\\3&3\end{smallmatrix}\right]$, a different problem whose leading direction need not equal $\vect u_1$.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch3/pca-reconstruction.pdf}
\caption{The exact four-point PCA calculation. Left: observed points, the retained affine axis, and reconstructed points (crosses); dashed segments show discarded directions. Right: the two covariance eigenvalues, with a zero baseline. Overlapping reconstructions at the mean remain two observations in the SSE.}
\end{figure}

Whitening further rescales score coordinate $j$ by $1/\sqrt{\lambda_j}$, giving unit empirical variance under this $1/n$ convention. It changes distances and discards the original variance scale; zero eigenvalues cannot be inverted. Implementations may report covariance eigenvalues using divisor $n-1$, changing numerical variances by $n/(n-1)$ while leaving directions and explained-variance ratios unchanged. Match the covariance convention when checking outputs against the \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.decomposition.PCA.html}{PCA API}. Centering, optional scaling, component count, and any whitening regularization all belong inside the current training fold.

For the exercise covariance $\left[\begin{smallmatrix}4&2\\2&2\end{smallmatrix}\right]$, the characteristic polynomial is $\lambda^2-6\lambda+4$, giving eigenvalues $3\pm\sqrt5$. A corresponding eigenvector is proportional to $(2,\lambda-4)^\top$; the first explained-variance ratio is $(3+\sqrt5)/6$. This is a second covariance example, not the covariance of the four plotted points.

\subsubsection{Choosing Number of Components}

\begin{definition}[Explained Variance Ratio]
Proportion of total variance captured by first $k$ components:
\[
\text{EVR}_k = \frac{\sum_{j=1}^k \lambda_j}{\sum_{j=1}^d \lambda_j}
\]
Common threshold: Keep enough components to explain 90-95\% of variance.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[
    width=11cm,
    height=6cm,
    xlabel={Number of components $k$},
    ylabel={Cumulative explained variance},
    xmin=0, xmax=10,
    ymin=0, ymax=1,
    ytick={0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1},
    yticklabels={0\%, 25\%, 50\%, 75\%, 100\%},
    grid=major,
    legend pos=south east
]
\addplot[blue, thick, mark=*, mark size=3pt] coordinates {
    (0,0) (1,0.45) (2,0.70) (3,0.82) (4,0.89) (5,0.93) (6,0.95) (7,0.97) (8,0.98) (9,0.99) (10,1.0)
};
\addlegendentry{Cumulative variance}
\addplot[orange!80!black,dashed,domain=0:10] {0.95};
\addlegendentry{95\% threshold}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Cumulative explained variance (schematic). A scree plot instead displays the individual eigenvalues.}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{PCA Properties and Limitations}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Key Properties of PCA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Linear transformation}: $\vect{z} = \mat{U}_k^\top(\vect{x} - \bar{\vect{x}})$
\item \textbf{Optimal reconstruction}: Minimizes reconstruction error $\sum_i \norm{\vect{x}_i - \hat{\vect{x}}_i}^2$
\item \textbf{Uncorrelated coordinates}: $n^{-1}\mat{Z}^\top\mat{Z}$ is diagonal; this refers to different component columns, not different sample rows
\item \textbf{Ordered by importance}: First component captures most variance
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Fast, closed-form solution (eigendecomposition)
\item Interpretable (variance explained)
\item Few structural choices: retained dimension, input scaling, and optional whitening; numerical solver settings also matter
\item Allows approximate reconstruction; exact recovery for $k<d$ requires the centered data to lie in the retained subspace
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Linear only}: Cannot capture non-linear structure
\item \textbf{Assumes variance = importance}: High variance might be noise
\item \textbf{Sensitive to scaling}: Center the data; decide whether to standardize based on units and which variances should count as important
\item \textbf{No Gaussian requirement}: Ordinary PCA is a geometric variance/reconstruction method; Gaussian assumptions belong to particular probabilistic interpretations
\item \textbf{Interpretability}: Components are linear combinations, hard to interpret
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use PCA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Preprocessing for supervised learning
\item Visualization (project to 2D/3D)
\item Noise reduction
\item When features are correlated
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Linear Discriminant Analysis (LDA)}

\begin{definition}[LDA]
Unlike PCA (unsupervised), LDA is \emph{supervised} dimensionality reduction. It finds directions that maximize separation between classes.

\textbf{Goal}: Find projection $\vect{w}$ that maximizes:
\[
J(\vect{w}) = \frac{\text{Between-class variance}}{\text{Within-class variance}} = \frac{\vect{w}^\top \mat{S}_B \vect{w}}{\vect{w}^\top \mat{S}_W \vect{w}}
\]
where:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mat{S}_W = \sum_{c=1}^K \sum_{i \in C_c} (\vect{x}_i - \vect{\mu}_c)(\vect{x}_i - \vect{\mu}_c)^\top$ (within-class scatter)
\item $\mat{S}_B = \sum_{c=1}^K n_c (\vect{\mu}_c - \vect{\mu})(\vect{\mu}_c - \vect{\mu})^\top$ (between-class scatter)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[LDA Solution]
When $\mat{S}_W$ is nonsingular, solve $\mat{S}_B\vect{w}=\lambda\mat{S}_W\vect{w}$ and retain directions with the largest eigenvalues. Equivalently these are eigenvectors of $\mat{S}_W^{-1}\mat{S}_B$; singular within-class scatter requires regularization or another appropriate solver.

Maximum number of components: $\min(d, K-1)$ where $K$ is number of classes.
\end{theorem}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{PCA vs LDA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{PCA}: Unsupervised, maximizes input variance using only the current training inputs for inductive evaluation
\item \textbf{LDA}: Supervised, maximizes class separation, uses labels
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use LDA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Dimensionality reduction before classification
\item When classes are well-separated
\item When you have class labels
\end{itemize}

Label information changes the projection objective; whether LDA improves classification requires held-out evaluation.
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Fisher's Criterion and a Case Where Variance Loses the Labels}
\label{sec:lda-worked}
Constrain $\vect w^\top\mat S_W\vect w=1$ when $\mat S_W$ is positive definite. A Lagrange multiplier for maximizing $\vect w^\top\mat S_B\vect w$ gives $\mat S_B\vect w=\lambda\mat S_W\vect w$, proving the generalized eigenproblem. Since the class-mean deviations satisfy $\sum_c n_c(\vect\mu_c-\vect\mu)=0$, their span has dimension at most $K-1$, which bounds the number of nonzero discriminant directions. In the binary case the optimal direction is proportional to $\mat S_W^{-1}(\vect\mu_1-\vect\mu_0)$. The shared-covariance GDA boundary normal in Part 2 points in the same direction; a projection criterion alone does not specify its prior-dependent decision threshold.

\begin{example}[High variance is not necessarily class information]
Use four class-0 points $(x_1,x_2)=(-1\pm0.2,\pm3)$, taking all four sign combinations, and four class-1 points $(1\pm0.2,\pm3)$. The overall mean is zero and
\[
\mat S_W=\begin{bmatrix}.32&0\\0&72\end{bmatrix},\qquad
\mat S_B=\begin{bmatrix}8&0\\0&0\end{bmatrix},\qquad
\mat\Sigma=\frac{\mat S_W+\mat S_B}{8}=\begin{bmatrix}1.04&0\\0&9\end{bmatrix}.
\]
PCA keeps the vertical direction first, explaining $9/10.04$ of total variance but mapping both classes to the same repeated values $\pm3$. Fisher LDA keeps the horizontal direction, with Rayleigh quotient $8/0.32=25$ and perfect separation in these eight training observations. Shared-covariance GDA has covariance $\operatorname{diag}(0.04,9)$ and boundary normal $(50,0)^\top$, agreeing in direction. This example shows an objective difference, not universal superiority of supervised projection on unseen data.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch3/pca-versus-lda.pdf}
\caption{The same eight labeled observations projected to different one-dimensional axes. Squares and circles distinguish classes; dotted segments indicate projection onto the dashed axis. The vertical feature has greater variance while the horizontal feature separates the training classes. Both panels have identical axis limits.}
\end{figure}
LDA uses training labels in fitting its transformation and must be fitted inside every training fold. PCA does not use labels in its objective but still learns from inputs; neither can be fitted globally before an inductive held-out evaluation.

\subsection{Non-Linear Methods}

\subsubsection{t-SNE (t-Distributed Stochastic Neighbor Embedding)}

\begin{definition}[t-SNE]
Non-linear method that preserves local structure. Particularly good for visualization.

\textbf{Idea}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item In high-dimensional space, compute pairwise similarities:
\[
\begin{gathered}
p_{j\mid i}=\frac{\exp(-\norm{\vect{x}_i-\vect{x}_j}^2/(2\sigma_i^2))}{\sum_{k\neq i}\exp(-\norm{\vect{x}_i-\vect{x}_k}^2/(2\sigma_i^2))},\quad j\neq i,\\
p_{ij}=\frac{p_{j\mid i}+p_{i\mid j}}{2n},\quad p_{ii}=0.
\end{gathered}
\]

Each $\sigma_i$ is chosen to match the specified perplexity of the conditional neighbor distribution.

\item In low-dimensional space, use a one-degree-of-freedom Student-t kernel for $i\neq j$, with $q_{ii}=0$:
\[
q_{ij} = \frac{(1 + \norm{\vect{z}_i - \vect{z}_j}^2)^{-1}}{\sum_{k \neq l} (1 + \norm{\vect{z}_k - \vect{z}_l}^2)^{-1}}
\]

\item Minimize KL divergence: $\text{KL}(P||Q) = \sum_{i\neq j} p_{ij}\log\frac{p_{ij}}{q_{ij}}$
\end{enumerate}

Optimized via gradient descent.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{t-SNE Properties}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Produces low-dimensional layouts for exploratory visualization; visual separation is not proof of distinct populations
\item Its objective emphasizes local neighborhood affinities; finite embeddings and optimization can still distort neighbors
\item Non-linear (can capture complex manifolds)
\item Stochastic (different runs give different results)
\item A standard coordinate-only t-SNE fit supplies no general fitted encoder; out-of-sample extensions are separate methods
\item Exact t-SNE uses quadratic pairwise work; approximate algorithms can reduce the cost
\item Hyperparameter sensitive (perplexity, learning rate)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Visualization of high-dimensional data
\item Exploratory analysis
\item \textbf{Not for preprocessing} (use PCA for that)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interpretation caution}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Plotted cluster area is not a calibrated measure of original-space density or importance
\item Distances between plotted clusters need not reflect original-space distances
\item Local neighborhood relationships also need validation and sensitivity checks
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Perplexity, Normalization, and a Three-Point Objective}
For one conditional neighborhood distribution, perplexity is $\exp\{-\sum_{j\ne i}p_{j\mid i}\log p_{j\mid i}\}$, equivalently $2^{H_2}$ when entropy uses base two. Adaptive bandwidth search tries to match this effective neighbor count at each point; it is not a fixed geometric radius. The symmetric $p_{ij}$ and embedding $q_{ij}$ are joint probabilities over \emph{ordered distinct pairs}: each entire matrix sums to one, while individual rows need not.

\begin{example}[Compute both affinity matrices and one gradient]
Take input positions $(0,1,2)$ and, solely for this illustration, a shared bandwidth $\sigma^2=1/(2\log2)$. Unnormalized off-diagonal similarities are $2^{-d_{ij}^2}$, so endpoint conditional probabilities are $(8/9,1/9)$, and the middle point assigns $(1/2,1/2)$ to its neighbors. Symmetrization gives
\[
\mat P=\begin{bmatrix}0&25/108&1/27\\25/108&0&25/108\\1/27&25/108&0\end{bmatrix}.
\]
This fixed-bandwidth example does not impose a common perplexity; endpoint and middle entropies differ. For candidate embedding positions $\vect z=(-1,0,1)$, the Student-t weights for neighboring pairs are $1/2$ and for endpoints $1/5$. Summing over ordered pairs gives normalization $12/5$, hence
\[
\mat Q=\begin{bmatrix}0&5/24&1/12\\5/24&0&5/24\\1/12&5/24&0\end{bmatrix}.
\]
The objective is
\[
C=\frac{25}{27}\log\frac{10}{9}+\frac2{27}\log\frac49=0.037487.
\]
Differentiating the normalized kernel, including its denominator, yields
\[
\frac{\partial C}{\partial\vect z_i}=4\sum_{j\ne i}(p_{ij}-q_{ij})\frac{\vect z_i-\vect z_j}{1+\|\vect z_i-\vect z_j\|^2}.
\]
For these scalar positions, the gradient is $(1/36,0,-1/36)$. A small descent step moves the endpoints outward, reducing their excessive embedding affinity. This is an objective/gradient calculation at a candidate embedding, not a converged visualization.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/batch3/tsne-probabilities.pdf}
\caption{The exact three-point joint probabilities, with a shared color scale and numerical labels. Diagonal entries are defined to be zero, not missing data. Joint probabilities sum over the whole matrix, unlike the row-normalized attention matrix in Part 4.}
\end{figure}
The \href{https://jmlr.org/papers/v9/vandermaaten08a.html}{t-SNE paper} defines this affinity-matching objective. A standard fitted coordinate set does not by itself define an encoder for a new input; the \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/generated/sklearn.manifold.TSNE.html}{standard scikit-learn TSNE API} exposes fitting/embedding rather than a general fitted transform. Parametric and other out-of-sample extensions exist and must be evaluated as their own procedures. Apparent cluster area, spacing, or isolation can depend strongly on perplexity, initialization, and optimization. Inspect neighborhood preservation and sensitivity before inferring population structure from an attractive two-dimensional layout.

\subsubsection{UMAP (Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection)}

\begin{definition}[UMAP]
A neighbor-graph-based embedding method motivated by manifold geometry and fuzzy set constructions. Its theory and objective differ from t-SNE; this does not establish universal superiority.

\textbf{Practical properties}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Often fast with approximate neighbor search; runtime depends on sample size, dimension, metric and implementation. A universal $O(n)$ versus $O(n^2)$ comparison is not justified
\item Can reveal different scales of structure, but global geometry is not guaranteed to be preserved
\item Supports a fitted transform for new inputs; standard coordinate-only t-SNE requires a separate out-of-sample extension
\item Scales to larger datasets
\item Neighbor-graph and embedding parameters control the apparent separation; separation alone is not clustering validation
\end{itemize}

Popular for single-cell RNA-seq visualization and other large-scale applications.
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{From a Fuzzy Neighbor Graph to an Embedding}
UMAP first constructs local neighbor memberships with per-point scales, then combines directed strengths into an undirected fuzzy graph. For two directed strengths $w_{ij}$ and $w_{ji}$, the fuzzy-union rule is
\[
w_{ij}^{\rm union}=w_{ij}+w_{ji}-w_{ij}w_{ji}=1-(1-w_{ij})(1-w_{ji}).
\]
A low-dimensional membership curve has form $q_{ij}=[1+a\|\vect z_i-\vect z_j\|^{2b}]^{-1}$ for fitted positive curve parameters. An idealized graph cross-entropy, up to terms independent of the embedding, sums
$-w_{ij}\log q_{ij}-(1-w_{ij})\log(1-q_{ij})$ over pairs. Attractive and repulsive terms compete. Practical training uses edge sampling and negative sampling; this simplified full-pair expression is not a claim that every implementation computes all pairs each step.

\begin{example}[One graph edge and its competing targets]
If directed strengths are $0.8$ and $0.5$, their union is $0.9$, not their arithmetic mean $0.65$. Set $a=b=1$ for a transparent curve example. Distance 1 gives $q=1/2$ and edge cross-entropy $\log2=0.693147$. Distance $1/3$ gives $q=0.9$ and cross-entropy $-0.9\log0.9-0.1\log0.1=0.325083$. This edge alone prefers its high target membership, but a shared embedding cannot independently realize arbitrary preferred distances for every pair.
\end{example}
The \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1802.03426}{UMAP paper} develops the neighbor graph and embedding objective. Neighbor count and minimum-distance settings change the representation's emphasis; a visually separated group is not automatically a verified cluster. The \href{https://umap-learn.readthedocs.io/en/latest/transform.html}{UMAP transform documentation} describes placing new observations in a learned space. Fit that space only on training inputs for an inductive test, then transform validation/test inputs without refitting. Using labels in a supervised UMAP fit changes the learning problem and requires training-only labels as well.

\subsubsection{Autoencoders}
\label{sec:autoencoder-basics}

\begin{definition}[Autoencoder]
Neural network that learns to compress and reconstruct data.

\textbf{Architecture}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Encoder}: $\vect{z} = f_{\text{enc}}(\vect{x}; \vect{\theta}_{\text{enc}})$ maps $\vect{x} \in \R^d$ to $\vect{z} \in \R^k$ (bottleneck)
\item \textbf{Decoder}: $\hat{\vect{x}} = f_{\text{dec}}(\vect{z}; \vect{\theta}_{\text{dec}})$ reconstructs from $\vect{z}$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Training}: Minimize reconstruction error:
\[
\Loss = \frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n \norm{\vect{x}_i - \hat{\vect{x}}_i}^2
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    layer/.style={rectangle, draw, minimum width=8mm, minimum height=15mm, fill=blue!20},
    latent/.style={rectangle, draw, minimum width=8mm, minimum height=8mm, fill=orange!20}
]
% Input
\node[layer] (in) at (0,0) {Input\\$\vect{x}$};

% Encoder
\node[layer] (e1) at (1.5,0) {};
\node[layer] (e2) at (2.5,0) {};

% Latent
\node[latent] (z) at (4,0) {Latent\\$\vect{z}$};

% Decoder
\node[layer] (d1) at (5.5,0) {};
\node[layer] (d2) at (6.5,0) {};

% Output
\node[layer] (out) at (8,0) {Output\\$\hat{\vect{x}}$};

% Connections
\draw[->, thick] (in) -- (e1);
\draw[->, thick] (e1) -- (e2);
\draw[->, thick] (e2) -- (z);
\draw[->, thick] (z) -- (d1);
\draw[->, thick] (d1) -- (d2);
\draw[->, thick] (d2) -- (out);

% Labels
\node at (2, -1.5) {Encoder};
\node at (6, -1.5) {Decoder};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{An undercomplete encoder--decoder schematic for the reconstruction objective. The latent code is not automatically a probability distribution or a useful downstream feature.}
\label{fig:autoencoder-architecture}
\end{figure}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Types of Autoencoders}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Undercomplete}: Bottleneck $k < d$ (forced to compress)
\item \textbf{Sparse}: Regularize activations in bottleneck
\item \textbf{Denoising}: Train to reconstruct from corrupted input
\item \textbf{Variational (VAE)}: Learn probability distribution in latent space (generative model)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Non-linear (can capture complex structure)
\item Flexible architecture
\item Architectures and reconstruction likelihoods can be designed for different data types
\item Can be applied to new data (just run encoder)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Requires training (no closed form)
\item Many hyperparameters
\item Can be slow to train
\item May overfit with limited data
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsubsection{The Linear Autoencoder--PCA Connection}
For centered column input $\tilde{\vect x}$, take an orthonormal matrix $\mat U_k$ and tied linear encoder/decoder
\[
\vect z=\mat U_k^\top\tilde{\vect x},\qquad
\hat{\vect x}=\mat U_k\vect z+\bar{\vect x}.
\]
Their mean reconstruction objective is exactly the PCA objective in Section~\ref{sec:pca-worked}. Choosing principal directions therefore minimizes it. More general unregularized linear encoder/decoder products can span the same optimal subspace while using different latent coordinates: an invertible latent change of basis can be undone by the decoder. Latent axes are not automatically identified as principal components unless the relevant constraints fix them.

\begin{example}[An autoencoder using the same four PCA points]
For the four-point PCA dataset, let the encoder be $(1,1)/\sqrt2$ on centered inputs and the decoder its transpose, with output bias equal to the training mean $(2,1)$. Latent values are $(\sqrt6,-\sqrt6,0,0)$. Reconstructions and mean squared error are exactly those computed earlier: SSE 4, MSE 1. The encoder maps the centered input $(1,-1)$ to zero and reconstructs the mean; its squared error is 2. This explicitly connects a neural architecture, a geometric projection, and the same loss scaling.
\end{example}

A nonlinear decoder changes the model family; a narrow bottleneck alone does not prevent memorization of finitely many observations. For a denoising autoencoder, corrupt a training input $\tilde{\vect x}\sim C(\cdot\mid\vect x)$ and minimize $\E_C\|\vect x-f_{\rm dec}(f_{\rm enc}(\tilde{\vect x}))\|^2$ against the clean training target. Fitting a corruption rule, tuning the bottleneck, or selecting a reconstruction/anomaly threshold uses training/validation data, with final test examples excluded from fitting. Low reconstruction error need not mean that a latent feature is useful for a downstream label.

\subsubsection{A VAE Bound with an Analytically Checkable Scalar Example}
\label{sec:vae-bound}
A variational autoencoder specifies a prior $p(\vect z)$, decoder density $p_\theta(\vect x\mid\vect z)$, and approximate posterior $q_\phi(\vect z\mid\vect x)$. Rearranging the nonnegative divergence to the true posterior gives
\[
\log p_\theta(\vect x)
=\underbrace{\E_q[\log p_\theta(\vect x\mid\vect z)]-D_{\rm KL}(q_\phi(\vect z\mid\vect x)\|p(\vect z))}_{\mathrm{ELBO}}
+D_{\rm KL}(q_\phi(\vect z\mid\vect x)\|p_\theta(\vect z\mid\vect x)).
\]
Thus maximizing the evidence lower bound (ELBO) trades reconstruction likelihood against a prior-matching cost, not merely MSE against an unexplained penalty. For diagonal Gaussian $q$ and standard-normal prior,
\[
D_{\rm KL}(q\|p)=\tfrac12\sum_j(\mu_j^2+\sigma_j^2-1-\log\sigma_j^2).
\]
Reparameterizing $\vect z=\vect\mu+\vect\sigma\odot\vect\epsilon$ with $\vect\epsilon\sim\mathcal N(\vect0,\mat I)$ permits differentiation through a sample using parameter-independent noise. With a Gaussian decoder of fixed observation variance $\tau^2$, the negative reconstruction log-likelihood contains $\|\vect x-\hat{\vect x}(\vect z)\|^2/(2\tau^2)$ plus a normalization constant. Its scale is determined by a declared density model, not silently identified with Part 1's unweighted MSE.

\begin{example}[Compute the bound, exact evidence, and gap]
Let scalar prior $z\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$, decoder $x\mid z\sim\mathcal N(z,1)$, observed $x=1$, and candidate variational posterior $q(z\mid1)=\mathcal N(1,1/4)$. Then
\[
D_{\rm KL}(q\|p)=\tfrac12(1+\tfrac14-1-\log\tfrac14)=0.818147.
\]
Under $q$, $\E[(1-z)^2]/2=1/8$. Including the Gaussian normalization gives ELBO $=-1/8-\tfrac12\log(2\pi)-0.818147=-1.862086$. The exact marginal is $x\sim\mathcal N(0,2)$, so $\log p(1)=-\tfrac12\log(4\pi)-1/4=-1.515512$. The nonnegative gap is $\tfrac12\log2=0.346574$, equal to the divergence between $q$ and the exact posterior $\mathcal N(1/2,1/2)$. These are properties of the specified scalar model and candidate posterior, not measured training results.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small,align=center}]
\node[draw] (x) at (0,0) {$\vect x$};
\node[draw,fill=blue!10,text width=2.4cm] (enc) at (3,0) {Encoder\\$\vect\mu,\vect\sigma$};
\node[draw,text width=2.7cm] (z) at (7,0) {$\vect z=\vect\mu+\vect\sigma\odot\vect\epsilon$};
\node[draw,fill=orange!10,text width=2.2cm] (dec) at (11,0) {Decoder density\\$p_\theta(\vect x\mid\vect z)$};
\node[draw,text width=3cm] (kl) at (3,-1.8) {KL to prior $p(\vect z)$};
\node (noise) at (7,1.1) {$\vect\epsilon\sim\mathcal N(\vect0,\mat I)$};
\draw[->] (x)--(enc);\draw[->] (enc)--(z);\draw[->] (noise)--(z);\draw[->] (z)--(dec);\draw[->] (enc)--(kl);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{VAE inference and reconstruction with a separate KL term. The encoder describes a distribution; a sampled latent value enters the decoder. The likelihood specifies the reconstruction loss and its normalization.}
\end{figure}
The \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1312.6114}{auto-encoding variational Bayes paper} introduces this variational/reparameterized approach. A VAE is a probabilistic generative model; an ordinary deterministic autoencoder does not acquire a sampling distribution merely by having a bottleneck.

\section{Association Rule Learning}
\label{sec:association-rules}
% =======================

\subsection{Market Basket Analysis}

\begin{definition}[Association Rules]
Given transactions (sets of items), find rules of the form:
\[
X \Rightarrow Y
\]
meaning ``if $X$ is purchased, then $Y$ is likely to be purchased.''

\textbf{Example}: $\{\text{bread, butter}\} \Rightarrow \{\text{milk}\}$
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Key Metrics]
For nonempty disjoint itemsets $X,Y$, let $E_X$ be the event that a transaction contains every item in $X$ (and similarly for $Y$). Then $E_{X\cup Y}=E_X\cap E_Y$. For rule $X\Rightarrow Y$:

\textbf{1. Support}:
\[
\text{support}(X \Rightarrow Y) = P(E_X\cap E_Y) = \frac{\text{transactions containing } X \text{ and } Y}{\text{total transactions}}
\]
Measures how frequently the rule occurs.

\textbf{2. Confidence}:
\[
\text{confidence}(X \Rightarrow Y) = P(E_Y\mid E_X) = \frac{\text{support}(X \cup Y)}{\text{support}(X)}
\]
Measures reliability: if $X$ is purchased, how often is $Y$ also purchased?

\textbf{3. Lift}:
\[
\text{lift}(X \Rightarrow Y) = \frac{P(E_X\cap E_Y)}{P(E_X)P(E_Y)} = \frac{\text{confidence}(X \Rightarrow Y)}{\text{support}(Y)}
\]
Measures how much more likely $Y$ is given $X$ compared to $Y$ alone.
\begin{itemize}
\item Lift > 1: $X$ and $Y$ are positively correlated
\item Lift = 1: Independent
\item Lift < 1: Negatively correlated
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Market Basket]
Store with 1000 transactions:
\begin{itemize}
\item Bread purchased: 600 times
\item Milk purchased: 500 times
\item Bread AND Milk: 400 times
\end{itemize}

For rule $\{\text{bread}\} \Rightarrow \{\text{milk}\}$:
\begin{align}
\text{support} &= \frac{400}{1000} = 0.4 \\
\text{confidence} &= \frac{400}{600} = 0.67 \\
\text{lift} &= \frac{0.4}{0.6 \times 0.5} = 1.33
\end{align}
People who buy bread are 33\% more likely to buy milk than average.
\end{example}

\subsection{Apriori Algorithm}

\begin{theorem}[Apriori Principle]
If an itemset is frequent, then all of its subsets must also be frequent. Equivalently:
\[
\text{If }X\subseteq Y\text{ and }X\text{ is infrequent, then }Y\text{ is infrequent}
\]
This allows pruning the search space.
\end{theorem}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Apriori Algorithm}

\textbf{Input}: Transactions $\mathcal{T}$, minimum support threshold $\text{minsup}$

\textbf{Initialize}: $L_1 = \{\text{frequent 1-itemsets}\}$ (items with support $\geq$ minsup)

\textbf{For} $k = 2, 3, \ldots$ until $L_k = \emptyset$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Generate candidates}: $C_k = \text{generate}(L_{k-1})$
\begin{itemize}
\item Join pairs in $L_{k-1}$ that share $k-2$ items
\item Prune candidates whose $(k-1)$-subsets are not in $L_{k-1}$
\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Count support}: Scan database to count support of each candidate in $C_k$

\item \textbf{Filter}: $L_k = \{c \in C_k : \text{support}(c) \geq \text{minsup}\}$
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Output}: All frequent itemsets $L = \bigcup_k L_k$

\textbf{Generate rules}: For each frequent itemset $X$, use nonempty proper subsets $\varnothing\ne Y\subsetneq X$ to generate $(X\setminus Y)\Rightarrow Y$, retaining the confidence threshold. Confidence requires positive antecedent support; lift additionally requires positive consequent support.
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Apriori Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Can prune many of the $2^V$ itemsets over $V$ distinct items; worst-case candidate growth is still exponential
\item Guarantees finding all rules above thresholds
\item Simple and interpretable
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Multiple database scans (one per level)
\item Generates many candidates
\item Not efficient for very large databases or low support thresholds
\end{itemize}

\textbf{FP-Growth}: More efficient alternative
\begin{itemize}
\item Builds compressed data structure (FP-tree)
\item Only 2 database scans
\item No candidate generation
\item Faster than Apriori, especially for large dense datasets
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{A Complete Apriori Run and All Qualifying Rules}
For itemsets $A\subseteq B$, every transaction containing $B$ also contains $A$. The corresponding event inclusion implies $\operatorname{support}(B)\le\operatorname{support}(A)$, proving the Apriori pruning principle. This anti-monotonicity concerns support, not a universal ordering of confidence or lift across arbitrary rules.

\begin{example}[From five transactions to the final rule list]
Use the five transactions
\[
\{A,B,C\},\quad\{A,B\},\quad\{A,C\},\quad\{B,C\},\quad\{B\},
\]
with minimum support $0.4$ and minimum confidence $0.6$. Minimum support count is two. Single-item counts are $A:3,B:4,C:3$, so all three are frequent. The pair candidates $AB,AC,BC$ each have count two, hence all survive. Their join generates $ABC$; every pair subset is frequent, so it is counted rather than pruned before counting. Its count is one and it is rejected. There are no larger frequent itemsets.

The six candidate singleton-to-singleton rules from frequent pairs have the following metrics:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccc|c}
Rule & Support & Confidence & Lift & Qualifies?\\\hline
$A\Rightarrow B$&$2/5$&$2/3$&$5/6$&yes\\
$B\Rightarrow A$&$2/5$&$1/2$&$5/6$&no\\
$A\Rightarrow C$&$2/5$&$2/3$&$10/9$&yes\\
$C\Rightarrow A$&$2/5$&$2/3$&$10/9$&yes\\
$B\Rightarrow C$&$2/5$&$1/2$&$5/6$&no\\
$C\Rightarrow B$&$2/5$&$2/3$&$5/6$&yes
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Thus exactly four rules pass both thresholds. No rule with union $ABC$ can pass the support threshold, even if its confidence might be high. The rule $A\Rightarrow B$ has confidence $2/3$ yet lift below one, because $B$ occurs in $4/5$ of all transactions. High confidence alone can reflect a common consequent rather than positive association. Lift is symmetric under reversing this pair, while confidence is not.
\end{example}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\small,align=center}]
\node[draw,fill=orange!10] (abc) at (0,2.8) {$ABC$: count 1\\not frequent};
\node[draw,fill=blue!10] (ab) at (-3,1.4) {$AB$: count 2};
\node[draw,fill=blue!10] (ac) at (0,1.4) {$AC$: count 2};
\node[draw,fill=blue!10] (bc) at (3,1.4) {$BC$: count 2};
\node[draw] (a) at (-3,0) {$A$: count 3};\node[draw] (b) at (0,0) {$B$: count 4};\node[draw] (c) at (3,0) {$C$: count 3};
\draw[->] (a)--(ab);\draw[->] (b)--(ab);\draw[->] (a)--(ac);\draw[->] (c)--(ac);\draw[->] (b)--(bc);\draw[->] (c)--(bc);
\draw[->] (ab)--(abc);\draw[->] (ac)--(abc);\draw[->] (bc)--(abc);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{The complete candidate lattice for three items at minimum support count two. Arrows connect subsets to supersets. The triple is generated and fails its support test; it is not excluded by the pair-subset pruning rule because all three pairs are frequent.}
\end{figure}
A mined association is not a causal effect or a guaranteed recommendation benefit. Searching many itemsets creates many opportunities for unstable findings. Fix support/confidence choices using training or validation transactions, then evaluate frozen rules on independent or later transactions when the aim is future prediction. Repeated customers, sessions, and temporal availability require the same group/time safeguards as supervised learning.

\subsection{Applications}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Retail}: Product placement, cross-selling, promotions
\item \textbf{Web usage mining}: Page navigation patterns
\item \textbf{Bioinformatics}: Gene co-occurrence, protein interactions
\item \textbf{Healthcare}: Symptom associations, drug interactions
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Summary of Part 5}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Key Takeaways]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Clustering}: Group similar points. K-means for spherical clusters (fast but needs $K$). Hierarchical for dendrograms (intuitive but slow). DBSCAN for arbitrary shapes and noise handling.

\item \textbf{Cluster Evaluation}: Silhouette score, Davies-Bouldin index (intrinsic). ARI when ground truth available.

\item \textbf{PCA}: Linear dimensionality reduction via eigendecomposition. Maximizes variance, fast, but linear only. Use for preprocessing and visualization.

\item \textbf{LDA}: Supervised projection maximizing between/within-class scatter; fit it with training labels only and validate downstream benefit.

\item \textbf{t-SNE}: Fits local-affinity visualizations; standard coordinates do not specify a new-input mapping. Check sensitivity and neighborhood preservation before interpreting a layout.

\item \textbf{UMAP}: Neighbor-graph embedding that supports transforming new data. Runtime and preservation of local/global structure depend on data, parameters and implementation.

\item \textbf{Autoencoders}: Neural network-based non-linear reduction. Flexible but requires training.

\item \textbf{Association Rules}: Find patterns in transaction data. Apriori algorithm with support/confidence/lift metrics. Applications in retail and beyond.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Next Steps}: Part 6 develops evaluation and model selection; Part 7 applies those boundaries to complete pipelines and deployment. Representation-learning extensions in Section~\ref{sec:ae-applications} reuse the autoencoder definitions above.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item For K-means with data $\{(1,1), (1,2), (5,5), (5,6)\}$ and $K=2$, starting with centroids at $(1,1)$ and $(5,5)$, perform one complete iteration (assignment + update). What are the new centroids?

\item Show that the K-means objective $J$ decreases (or stays the same) in both the assignment step and the update step.

\item For hierarchical clustering with complete linkage, compute the dendrogram for points $\{(0,0), (1,0), (0,1), (5,5)\}$ using Euclidean distance.

\item Given covariance matrix $\mat{\Sigma} = \begin{bmatrix} 4 & 2 \\ 2 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$, find the principal components (eigenvectors) and explained variance (eigenvalues).

\item Explain why PCA requires centering the data (subtracting the mean). What happens if you don't center?

\item For transactions: $\{A,B,C\}, \{A,B\}, \{A,C\}, \{B,C\}, \{B\}$, find all rules with minimum support 0.4 and minimum confidence 0.6.

\item Compare computational complexity: K-means, hierarchical clustering (complete linkage), and DBSCAN. Which is fastest for $n=100,000$ points?

\item When would you use t-SNE vs PCA vs UMAP? Give specific scenarios for each.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

% =======================
% Part 6: Model Evaluation & Practical Aspects
% =======================
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 6: Model Evaluation \& Practical Aspects}
\section*{Part 6: Model Evaluation \& Practical Aspects}

\section{Model Evaluation Metrics}
\label{sec:metrics}

\subsection{Classification Metrics}

\subsubsection{Confusion Matrix}

\begin{definition}[Confusion Matrix]
For binary classification with classes \{Positive, Negative\}:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Predicted}} \\
\textbf{Actual} & Positive & Negative \\
\midrule
Positive & TP & FN \\
Negative & FP & TN \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

where:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{TP (True Positive)}: Correctly predicted positive
\item \textbf{TN (True Negative)}: Correctly predicted negative
\item \textbf{FP (False Positive)}: Incorrectly predicted positive (Type I error)
\item \textbf{FN (False Negative)}: Incorrectly predicted negative (Type II error)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Medical Diagnosis]
Disease detection model tested on 100 patients:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\toprule
& \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{Predicted}} \\
\textbf{Actual} & Disease & Healthy \\
\midrule
Disease & 40 & 10 \\
Healthy & 5 & 45 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

TP=40 (correct disease detection), TN=45 (correct healthy), FP=5 (false alarm), FN=10 (missed disease).
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Basic Metrics}

\begin{definition}[Classification Metrics]

\textbf{1. Accuracy}:
\[
\text{Accuracy} = \frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}
\]
Proportion of correct predictions overall.

\textbf{Limitation}: Misleading for imbalanced datasets (e.g., 95\% negative class $\to$ predicting all negative gives 95\% accuracy).

\textbf{2. Precision (Positive Predictive Value)}:
\[
\text{Precision} = \frac{TP}{TP + FP}
\]
Of all predicted positives, what fraction are actually positive? ``How precise are positive predictions?''

\textbf{3. Recall (Sensitivity, True Positive Rate)}:
\[
\text{Recall} = \frac{TP}{TP + FN}
\]
Of all actual positives, what fraction did we detect? ``How many positives did we recall?''

\textbf{4. Specificity (True Negative Rate)}:
\[
\text{Specificity} = \frac{TN}{TN + FP}
\]
Of all actual negatives, what fraction did we correctly identify?

\textbf{5. F1-Score} (Harmonic Mean of Precision and Recall):
\[
F_1 = 2 \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}} = \frac{2TP}{2TP + FP + FN}
\]
Balances precision and recall. Useful when both are important.

\textbf{6. F$_\beta$-Score} (Generalized):
\[
F_\beta = (1 + \beta^2) \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\beta^2 \cdot \text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}
\]
\begin{itemize}
\item $\beta = 1$: Equal weight (F1)
\item $\beta > 1$: More weight on recall
\item $\beta < 1$: More weight on precision
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Precision vs Recall Trade-off}:

\textbf{High Precision, Low Recall}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Conservative predictions
\item Few false alarms (FP), but miss many positives (FN)
\item Example: Spam filter that rarely flags legitimate email as spam but misses some spam
\end{itemize}

\textbf{High Recall, Low Precision}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Aggressive predictions
\item Catch most positives (low FN), but many false alarms (FP)
\item Example: Cancer screening that detects most cases but has many false positives
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to prioritize}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Precision}: When false positives are costly (e.g., predicting customer will buy expensive item, spam detection)
\item \textbf{Recall}: When false negatives are costly (e.g., disease detection, fraud detection)
\item \textbf{F1}: When both matter equally
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\begin{example}[Computing Metrics]
From medical diagnosis example (TP=40, TN=45, FP=5, FN=10):
\begin{align}
\text{Accuracy} &= \frac{40+45}{100} = 0.85 \\
\text{Precision} &= \frac{40}{40+5} = 0.889 \\
\text{Recall} &= \frac{40}{40+10} = 0.80 \\
\text{Specificity} &= \frac{45}{45+5} = 0.90 \\
F_1 &= 2 \cdot \frac{0.889 \cdot 0.80}{0.889 + 0.80} = 0.842
\end{align}
\end{example}

\subsubsection{ROC Curve and AUC}

\begin{definition}[ROC Curve]
The \textbf{Receiver Operating Characteristic (ROC)} curve plots:
\begin{itemize}
\item X-axis: False Positive Rate (FPR) = $\frac{FP}{FP + TN} = 1 - \text{Specificity}$
\item Y-axis: True Positive Rate (TPR) = $\frac{TP}{TP + FN} = \text{Recall}$
\end{itemize}
at various classification thresholds.

For a classifier outputting probability $p$, vary threshold $t$: predict positive if $p \geq t$.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=10cm,height=8cm,xlabel={False positive rate},ylabel={True positive rate},xmin=0,xmax=1,ymin=0,ymax=1,grid=major,legend pos=south east]
\addplot[gray,very thick] coordinates {(0,0)(0,1)(1,1)};
\addlegendentry{Perfect (AUC=1)}
\addplot[blue,thick,domain=0:1,samples=201] {1-(1-x)^9};
\addlegendentry{Good (AUC=0.9)}
\addplot[black,dashed,domain=0:1] {x};
\addlegendentry{Random (AUC=0.5)}
\addplot[orange!80!black,thick,domain=0:1,samples=201] {x^3};
\addlegendentry{Reversed ranking (AUC=0.25)}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Analytic ROC illustrations, not empirical estimates. Integrating $1-(1-x)^9$ gives $0.9$; integrating $x^3$ gives $0.25$.}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[AUC (Area Under the Curve)]
\textbf{AUC-ROC} is the area under the ROC curve:
\begin{itemize}
\item AUC = 1.0: All evaluated positives rank above all negatives; this does not establish probability calibration or performance on unseen populations
\item AUC = 0.5: Chance-level pairwise ranking; a diagonal ROC is one possible curve with this area
\item AUC < 0.5: Below chance-level ranking; check label/score orientation on validation data before considering reversal
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Interpretation}: $\mathrm{AUC}=P(s_+>s_-)+\tfrac12P(s_+=s_-)$ for independent positive and negative score draws; ties receive half credit.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Advantages of ROC-AUC}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Threshold-independent}: Evaluates all possible thresholds
\item \textbf{Class-proportion comparison}: For fixed class-conditional score distributions, ROC-AUC is invariant to prevalence; it can still conceal poor precision when positives are rare
\item \textbf{Single number summary}: Easy to compare models
\item \textbf{Probabilistic interpretation}: Ranking quality
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use ROC vs PR}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{ROC}: Balanced classes, care about both classes equally
\item \textbf{PR}: Imbalanced classes, focus on positive class (more informative)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Precision-Recall Curve}

\begin{definition}[PR Curve]
Plots Precision vs Recall at various thresholds.

Emphasizes positive-class precision and recall, often useful for rare positives; the appropriate metric still depends on the decision goal.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}
\begin{axis}[width=10cm,height=7cm,xlabel={Recall},ylabel={Precision},xmin=0,xmax=1,ymin=0,ymax=1,grid=major,legend pos=south west]
\addplot[blue,thick,domain=0:1,samples=101] {1-0.7*x};
\addlegendentry{Illustrative classifier}
\addplot[black,dashed,domain=0:1] {0.3};
\addlegendentry{Random baseline (prevalence=0.3)}
\end{axis}
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Schematic PR curve $P(R)=1-0.7R$, within $[0,1]$ and reaching prevalence $0.3$ at full recall. This analytic illustration is not an empirical curve or an AP estimate.}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[AP and mAP]
\textbf{Average Precision (AP)}: A non-interpolated, recall-weighted sum of precision at successive operating points:
\[
AP = \sum_{k=1}^n (R_k - R_{k-1}) P_k
\]
Here thresholds are traversed from high to low so $R_k$ is nondecreasing, with $R_0=0$; tied scores are processed together. AP is distinct from trapezoidal interpolation of a PR curve.

\textbf{Mean Average Precision (mAP)}: Average AP across multiple classes (used in object detection).
\end{definition}

% Batch 4: finite metrics, probabilities, decisions
\subsubsection{A Complete Ranking and Decision Calculation}
\label{sec:finite-metrics}
A score ranks examples; a probability estimates a conditional frequency; a
threshold specifies an action. These are three different evaluation questions.
Precision is undefined when $TP+FP=0$, recall when $TP+FN=0$, and ROC-AUC
requires both classes. Declare any software convention for these cases rather
than interpreting a substituted zero as a measured failure.

\begin{example}[Eight scores, including ties]
Use the following fixed teaching sample, with positive label 1:
\[
(y_i)=(1,0,1,0,1,0,0,1),\qquad
(s_i)=(.9,.8,.8,.6,.4,.4,.2,.1).
\]
Predict $\hat y_i=\indicator{s_i\ge t}$; tied scores enter together.
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrrrr}\toprule
$t$ & TP & FP & FN & TN & Precision & Recall\\\midrule
$+\infty$ &0&0&4&4&undefined&0\\
$.9$ &1&0&3&4&1&$.25$\\
$.8$ &2&1&2&3&$2/3$&$.50$\\
$.6$ &2&2&2&2&$1/2$&$.50$\\
$.4$ &3&3&1&1&$1/2$&$.75$\\
$.2$ &3&4&1&0&$3/7$&$.75$\\
$.1$ &4&4&0&0&$1/2$&1\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
There are $4\cdot4=16$ positive--negative pairs. Positives with scores
$.9,.8,.4,.1$ earn respectively $4,3.5,1.5,0$ wins (ties count half), giving
\[
\mathrm{AUC}=\frac9{16}=.5625,\qquad
\mathrm{AP}=\frac14\left(1+\frac23+\frac12+\frac12\right)=\frac23.
\]
Only recall increases contribute to AP. Trapezoidal interpolation of the same
PR operating points gives approximately $.699405$, a different quantity.
In Figure~\ref{fig:b4-rank}, precision 1 at zero predicted positives is only a
plotting endpoint convention, not an observed ratio.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/finite-roc-pr.pdf}
\caption{Exact empirical operating points for the eight rows. The shaded
right-endpoint rectangles integrate to AP; the dashed PR interpolation has a different area.}
\label{fig:b4-rank}\end{figure}

\begin{proposition}[Cost-based binary decision]
Let $p(x)=P(Y=1\mid X=x)$ be calibrated for the deployment population,
with costs $c_{FP}>0,c_{FN}>0$ and zero cost for correct decisions.
The conditional-risk derivation is in Section~\ref{sec:cost-sensitive}. Its rule is to predict positive when
\[
p\ge \frac{c_{FP}}{c_{FP}+c_{FN}}.
\]
This rule assumes these costs represent the decision and that there are no
additional capacity or abstention constraints.
\end{proposition}
\begin{example}[Costs and empirical thresholds]
For $c_{FP}=1,c_{FN}=4$, the probability threshold is $.2$. On the eight rows,
the mean empirical cost $(FP+4FN)/8$ is 1 at $.2$ and $.5$ at $.1$.
This finite sample need not favor the population-optimal threshold, especially
when its scores are uncalibrated. Select an empirical threshold on a decision
validation block, then freeze it before final testing. F1 is a harmonic mean
of precision and recall; it does not encode a fixed false-positive to
false-negative cost ratio. AUC of $.5$ means chance-level pairwise ranking on
the evaluated distribution; it does not prove the score was randomly generated.
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Proper Losses, Calibration, and Prevalence}
\label{sec:calibration-worked}
For binary probabilities $q_i$, use the sample means
\[
\mathrm{Brier}=\frac1n\sum_i(q_i-y_i)^2,\qquad
\mathrm{NLL}=-\frac1n\sum_i[y_i\log q_i+(1-y_i)\log(1-q_i)].
\]
This NLL has the same mean scaling as earlier cross-entropy objectives.
With true conditional probability $p$, the expected Brier loss is
$(q-p)^2+p(1-p)$, uniquely minimized at $q=p$. Expected NLL is binary
entropy $H(p)$ plus $D_{KL}(\mathrm{Bern}(p)\Vert\mathrm{Bern}(q))$;
nonnegative KL proves the same minimizer. Numerical clipping must be declared;
it changes predictions at the boundary, not the theoretical proper-loss result.

\begin{example}[Brier, reliability bins, and calibration fitting]
Treat the eight scores as candidate probabilities. The squared errors sum to
$2.42$, so Brier is $.3025$; mean NLL is $.850885$. Two fixed bins,
$[0,.5)$ and $[.5,1]$, each contain four rows. Their mean probabilities are
$.275,.775$, while both positive fractions are $.5$. Consequently
\[
\mathrm{ECE}=\sum_b\frac{n_b}{n}|\bar q_b-\bar y_b|=.25.
\]
ECE depends on bin choices and sample size. In a separate two-row case
$q=(.1,.9),y=(1,0)$, one bin gives ECE zero although Brier is $.81$.
A reliability plot should report bin counts and leave empty bins unestimated.

For a separate calibration sample with increasing scores $(.1,.4,.6,.9)$ and
labels $(0,1,0,1)$, isotonic least squares pools the violating middle pair:
$(0,.5,.5,1)$. The calibration-training Brier decreases from $.185$ to $.125$.
This is a fit result, not evidence of improved held-out calibration.
Alternatively fit $q=\sigma(as+b)$ (Platt-style) on a declared score $s$,
or $q=\sigma(z/T)$, $T>0$, on logits $z$. Positive temperature preserves
ranking but may change both calibration and decisions. On the eight rows,
$T=2$ lowers Brier to $.262479$ without changing AUC or AP; choosing $T$
on these evaluated labels would make that an in-sample comparison.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/calibration-examples.pdf}
\caption{Two distinct teaching samples: binned reliability for eight rows (left)
and pool-adjacent-violators fitting on four calibration rows (right).}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Precision changes with prevalence]
For prevalence $\pi=P(Y=1)$ and fixed class-conditional score distributions,
\[
P(Y=1\mid\hat Y=1)=\frac{\pi\,\mathrm{TPR}}
 {\pi\,\mathrm{TPR}+(1-\pi)\,\mathrm{FPR}}.
\]
At TPR $.8$, FPR $.1$, precision is $8/9$ for $\pi=.5$ but only
$8/107\approx .07477$ for $\pi=.01$. ROC-AUC can remain unchanged.
Neither a high AUC nor a probability calibrated in the old population promises
useful precision after a population shift. If only the class prior changes,
with $P(X\mid Y)$ fixed, posterior odds multiply by
$\{\pi_{new}/(1-\pi_{new})\}/\{\pi_{old}/(1-\pi_{old})\}$.
Old probability $.8$ and priors $.5\to .1$ then give new probability $4/13$.
This correction requires the label-shift assumption.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.88\linewidth]{figures/batch4/prevalence-precision.pdf}
\caption{Bayes' rule at fixed TPR and FPR. The horizontal axis is logarithmic;
the marked prevalences are $.01$ and $.5$.}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Multi-Class Metrics}

\begin{definition}[Multi-Class Extension]
For $K$ classes, confusion matrix is $K \times K$.

\textbf{Macro-averaging}: Compute metric for each class, then average (treats all classes equally).
\[
\text{Macro-F1} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^K F1_k
\]

\textbf{Micro-averaging}: Pool all TP, FP, FN across classes, then compute the metric; this is not a support-weighted average of classwise metrics.
\[
\text{Micro-Precision} = \frac{\sum_{k=1}^K TP_k}{\sum_{k=1}^K (TP_k + FP_k)}
\]

\textbf{Weighted-averaging}: Weight each class metric by its support (number of true instances).
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{When to use}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Macro}: When all classes are equally important (regardless of size)
\item \textbf{Micro}: Aggregate instance-level errors; for complete single-label classification it equals accuracy
\item \textbf{Weighted}: Average class metrics using true-class support; it need not lie between macro and micro
\end{itemize}

For imbalanced datasets, macro-averaging can reveal poor performance on minority classes that micro-averaging might hide.
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Regression Metrics}

\begin{definition}[Regression Metrics]
For predictions $\hat{y}_i$ and true values $y_i$:

\textbf{1. Mean Absolute Error (MAE)}:
\[
MAE = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n |y_i - \hat{y}_i|
\]
Average absolute difference, in the units of $y$; large residuals incur linear rather than quadratic cost.

\textbf{2. Mean Squared Error (MSE)}:
\[
MSE = \frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2
\]
Penalizes large errors heavily. Not in original units.

\textbf{3. Root Mean Squared Error (RMSE)}:
\[
RMSE = \sqrt{MSE} = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}
\]
Same scale as $y$. More interpretable than MSE.

\textbf{4. Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE)}:
\[
MAPE = \frac{100\%}{n}\sum_{i=1}^n \left|\frac{y_i - \hat{y}_i}{y_i}\right|
\]
Scale-independent. Undefined when $y_i = 0$.

\textbf{5. R\textsuperscript{2} (Coefficient of Determination)}:
\[
R^2 = 1 - \frac{SS_{\text{res}}}{SS_{\text{tot}}} = 1 - \frac{\sum_i(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}{\sum_i(y_i - \bar{y})^2}
\]
where $\bar{y} = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i y_i$.

\begin{itemize}
\item $R^2 = 1$: Perfect predictions
\item $R^2 = 0$: Model no better than predicting mean
\item $R^2<0$: Worse than predicting the evaluation-set mean; this can occur for linear or nonlinear models on held-out data
\end{itemize}

Measures proportion of variance explained by the model.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{MAE vs MSE/RMSE}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{MAE}: 
  \begin{itemize}
  \item Less sensitive to large residuals than squared error, but still unbounded
  \item All errors weighted equally
  \item Easier to interpret
  \end{itemize}
\item \textbf{MSE/RMSE}:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item Sensitive to outliers (large errors penalized heavily)
  \item MSE is differentiable everywhere; RMSE, a scaled residual-vector norm, is not differentiable at the zero residual vector
  \item Common in statistical theory
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When a linear residual penalty matches the goal}: Consider MAE; investigate measurement errors and the evaluation population separately

\textbf{When large errors are particularly bad}: Use MSE/RMSE

\textbf{R\textsuperscript{2} advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Invariant to a common affine rescaling of targets and predictions; different target distributions and baselines still limit comparisons
\item Intuitive (percentage of variance explained)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{R\textsuperscript{2} limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item For nested unregularized OLS fits with an intercept on the same training data, training $R^2$ cannot decrease as features are added; this guarantee does not extend to test $R^2$
\item Can be negative on held-out data for linear or nonlinear models; it is undefined by this formula when the evaluation targets are constant
\item Doesn't indicate if model is appropriate
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsection{Aggregation, Estimation Targets, and Uncertainty}
\label{sec:evaluation-uncertainty}
\begin{example}[Multi-class aggregation calculated completely]
Rows are true classes and columns predicted classes in
\[
C=\begin{pmatrix}8&1&1\\1&1&0\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Supports are $(10,2,2)$, predicted counts $(9,3,2)$, and per-class F1 values
are $(16/19,2/5,1/2)$. Thus macro-F1 is $.580702$, support-weighted F1
is $.730075$, and accuracy is $10/14=.714286$.
In single-label classification over all classes, every incorrect prediction
adds one pooled FP and one pooled FN. Hence micro-precision, micro-recall,
and micro-F1 all equal accuracy. Weighted F1 need not lie between macro-F1
and micro-F1, as this example shows. Macro-F1 is the average of class F1s,
not the harmonic mean of separately macro-averaged precision and recall.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}[Why squared and absolute errors estimate different targets]
For a constant prediction $a$ and finite second moment,
$\E(Y-a)^2=\Var(Y)+(a-\E Y)^2$, so squared error is minimized at the mean.
For absolute error, a subgradient of $\E|Y-a|$ includes zero exactly when
$P(Y\le a)\ge1/2$ and $P(Y\ge a)\ge1/2$: any median minimizes it.
These are conditional means and medians when predicting from $X=x$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{example}[A rare large response]
Let $P(Y=0)=.8$, $P(Y=10)=.2$. The mean is 2 and the median is 0.
Predictions 0 and 2 have expected squared losses 20 and 16, but expected
absolute losses 2 and 3.2. MAE penalizes large residuals linearly rather than
quadratically; it is still unbounded. MAPE is undefined at a zero target and
can be dominated by targets near zero. Report its denominator convention.
On a separate test set $y=(0,1,2)$, predicting zero gives SSE 5 and the
constant test-mean baseline gives SST 2, hence $R^2=1-5/2=-1.5$.
The test mean defines this evaluation denominator; it is not a predictor
that was fitted with test labels.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/mean-median-loss.pdf}
\caption{The same response distribution yields different optimal constant
predictions for squared and absolute loss. Curves are exact expectations.}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Intervals Must Match the Sampling Unit}
\begin{example}[Wilson interval and a paired comparison]
For $k$ correct predictions in $n$ independent Bernoulli trials, invert the
score-test inequality $(\hat p-p)^2\le z^2p(1-p)/n$. The Wilson interval is
\[
\frac{\hat p+z^2/(2n)\ \pm\ z\sqrt{\hat p(1-\hat p)/n+z^2/(4n^2)}}
 {1+z^2/n}.
\]
With $k=85,n=100,z=1.96$, this is $[.76716,.90694]$, an approximate
95\% interval under those assumptions. Repeated observations of the same
device do not supply 100 independent trials merely because there are 100 rows.

Suppose two classifiers evaluated on the same eight independent cases have
4 and 7 correct predictions: three cases improve and none worsen.
The exact two-sided McNemar/binomial test conditional on these three discordant
cases has $p=2(1/2)^3=.25$. A visible accuracy difference can have considerable
uncertainty. Paired bootstrap comparisons resample the same case indices for
both models; group bootstrap resamples whole independent groups. Neither
method justifies treating overlapping CV fold scores as independent replicates.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.89\linewidth]{figures/batch4/wilson-intervals.pdf}
\caption{The same $.85$ accuracy has different Wilson intervals at three
independent sample sizes. This figure assumes independent trials.}
\end{figure}
For unequal validation fold sizes $n_k$, a pooled mean loss is
$\sum_k n_k\bar\ell_k/\sum_k n_k$, whereas an unweighted fold mean estimates
an average over folds. They coincide for equal sizes. For nonlinear metrics,
even size weighting need not reproduce the metric of pooled predictions:
compute pooled F1 from pooled confusion counts. Repeated searches, dependence,
and temporal shifts create additional uncertainty. A bootstrap with a frozen
predictor measures evaluation-sample uncertainty conditional on that predictor;
it does not include variability from retraining and model selection.

\subsubsection{A Finite-Sample Prediction Interval}
\begin{example}[Split conformal, including the small-sample edge case]
Fit a regression model on training data and use a separate exchangeable
calibration sample of size $m=5$. Absolute residual scores are
$(.1,.2,.2,.4,.8)$. For miscoverage $\alpha=.2$, use order statistic
$k=\lceil(m+1)(1-\alpha)\rceil=5$, hence $q=.8$ and prediction interval
$[\hat f(x)-q,\hat f(x)+q]$. A prediction 3 gives $[2.2,3.8]$.
Exchangeability makes the test score's rank uniform in the absence of ties;
conservative handling of ties yields marginal coverage at least $1-\alpha$.
It is not conditional coverage for every input or group.
If $\alpha=.1$, then $k=6>m$: the valid general convention is $q=+\infty$,
not clipping the index to 5 and claiming 90\% coverage.
An arbitrary future time block is not exchangeable with past residuals, so
this guarantee does not automatically apply to the device workflow below.
See \href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2107.07511}{Angelopoulos and Bates' conformal tutorial}.
\end{example}

\section{Model Selection and Hyperparameter Tuning}
\label{sec:evaluation-protocol}
% =======================

\subsection{Cross-Validation}

\subsubsection{Splitting Units and Fitting the Whole Procedure}
\label{sec:cv-protocol}
The protocol introduced in Section~\ref{sec:learning-protocol} evaluates a \emph{procedure}: every learned transformation, model fit, hyperparameter search, and threshold choice that will be applied to new data. Splitting rows is appropriate only when rows are the independent units relevant to deployment. Use group-disjoint folds for new patients/users/devices, and forward temporal splits for future prediction. A stratified random split preserves class proportions but does not enforce either requirement.

\begin{example}[A group split and an availability check]
Suppose patients A, B, C, D each have five visits. For prediction on new patients, a valid holdout might use A/B/C for training and D for testing, keeping all five D visits outside training. Randomly placing one D visit in the test set and four in training measures a different scenario. For forecasting at time $t$, a seven-day summary must use data available by $t$, not days $t+1$ through $t+7$. If the target covers the next seven days, adjacent training and validation targets may overlap; choose a gap or purge the overlapping training labels according to their actual end times. A fixed gap is not a substitute for checking these windows.
\end{example}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{An executable split--search--refit--test example}
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python,showstringspaces=false]
from sklearn.datasets import load_iris
from sklearn.model_selection import (
    train_test_split, StratifiedKFold, GridSearchCV
)
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

X, y = load_iris(return_X_y=True)
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.2, stratify=y, random_state=42
)
procedure = Pipeline([
    ("scale", StandardScaler()),
    ("model", LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000))
])
folds = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=3, shuffle=True, random_state=42)
search = GridSearchCV(
    procedure, {"model__C": [0.1, 1.0, 10.0]},
    cv=folds, scoring="accuracy", refit=True
)
search.fit(X_train, y_train)
# Each CV fit learns its own scaler. refit=True then fits
# the selected whole pipeline on all 120 non-test examples.
print(search.best_params_)
print(search.score(X_test, y_test))  # one final test evaluation
\end{lstlisting}
This demonstration has 9 candidate/fold fits and one final refit. Do not use the printed test score to revise the grid or procedure. Iris rows are treated as independent for this example; replace the splitter and supply group/time information when the application requires it. The numerical value of $C$ follows the estimator's documented convention, not the custom ridge $\lambda$ convention in Part 1.
\end{implementationbox}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={align=center,font=\small}]
\node[draw,fill=blue!10,text width=10.7cm] (a) at (0,0) {One CV round: first separate training fold and validation fold};
\node[draw,below=7mm of a,text width=10.7cm] (b) {Fit scaler on training fold; transform training and validation with it};
\node[draw,below=7mm of b,text width=10.7cm] (c) {Fit model on transformed training fold; score transformed validation fold};
\draw[->] (a)--(b);\draw[->] (b)--(c);
\node[draw,dashed,below=9mm of c,fill=orange!10,text width=10.7cm] {Leakage: fit scaler on all non-test data first, then cross-validate only the model};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Fold-local fitting keeps validation-fold statistics outside training. The dashed bottom workflow is invalid for estimating the complete fitted procedure, even if the scaler never reads labels.}
\end{figure}

The \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/common_pitfalls.html}{scikit-learn leakage guide} explains why a pipeline is the safe unit of cross-validation. Its \href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/modules/cross_validation.html}{cross-validation documentation} describes stratified, group-aware, and temporal alternatives. A splitter encodes the intended evaluation population; a pipeline enforces the fitting boundary after those splits.

\subsubsection{K-Fold Cross-Validation}

\begin{definition}[K-Fold Cross-Validation]
For $2\le K\le n$, validation folds form a partition of the current fitting
sample, and each fit excludes its validation fold. Ordinary K-fold sizes
differ by at most one; the complete fitting algorithm is given below.
Choosing $K$ and reporting aggregate scores must respect the sampling units
and metric conventions in Section~\ref{sec:evaluation-uncertainty}.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=0.8]
\foreach \r in {1,...,5} {
  \foreach \c in {1,...,5} {
    \ifnum\r=\c
      \fill[orange!35] ({1.5*(\c-1)},{1-\r}) rectangle ++(1.4,0.6);
      \node at ({1.5*(\c-1)+0.7},{1.3-\r}) {Val};
    \else
      \fill[blue!20] ({1.5*(\c-1)},{1-\r}) rectangle ++(1.4,0.6);
      \node at ({1.5*(\c-1)+0.7},{1.3-\r}) {Train};
    \fi
  }
  \node[anchor=west] at (7.7,{1.3-\r}) {Round \r};
}
\foreach \c in {1,...,5} \node at ({1.5*(\c-1)+0.7},1.2) {Fold \c};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Complete 5-fold cross-validation: each column is held out exactly once. All learned preprocessing is fitted only on the four training folds in that round.}
\end{figure}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{K-Fold Cross-Validation}

\textbf{Input}: Data $\mathcal{D}$, model/hyperparameters, $K$

\begin{enumerate}
\item Split $\mathcal{D}$ into $K$ approximately equal-sized folds: $\{\mathcal{D}_1, \ldots, \mathcal{D}_K\}$

\item \textbf{For} $k = 1$ to $K$:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}} = \mathcal{D} \setminus \mathcal{D}_k$
\item $\mathcal{D}_{\text{val}} = \mathcal{D}_k$
\item Fit learned preprocessing on $\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}$ only; transform both folds with those fitted statistics
\item Train model on the transformed $\mathcal{D}_{\text{train}}$
\item Evaluate on $\mathcal{D}_{\text{val}}$, record score $M_k$
\end{itemize}

\item Compute average score: $\bar{M} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^K M_k$

\item (Optional) Compute standard deviation: $\sigma_M = \sqrt{\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^K (M_k - \bar{M})^2}$
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Output}: Mean score $\bar{M}$ and std $\sigma_M$
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Why Cross-Validation?}

\textbf{Problem with single train/val split}:
\begin{itemize}
\item High variance (depends on random split)
\item Wastes data (validation set not used for training)
\item May not be representative
\end{itemize}

\textbf{CV advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Lower variance estimate
\item Uses all data for both training and validation
\item Fold scores describe split-to-split variation, but their standard deviation is not a confidence interval: training-fold overlap induces dependence
\item More reliable for model selection
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Choosing $K$}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Small $K$ (e.g., 3)}: Fewer fits and less training data per fit; the error estimate may be more pessimistic
\item \textbf{Large $K$ (e.g., 10)}: More fits and more training data per fit; uncertainty depends on model stability and correlations between folds
\item \textbf{$K=n$ (LOOCV)}: Trains on $n-1$ samples per fit; often costly, with highly overlapping training sets. Its variance is not universally larger or smaller than other CV schemes
\item \textbf{Common choice}: $K = 5$ or $10$ (good trade-off)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Stratified K-Fold}

\begin{definition}[Stratified K-Fold]
For classification, ensure each fold has approximately the same class distribution as the full dataset.

Particularly important for:
\begin{itemize}
\item Imbalanced datasets
\item Small datasets
\item Multi-class problems
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Stratified Split]
Dataset: 100 examples, 80 class A, 20 class B (80/20 split).

\textbf{Regular 5-fold}: Some folds might have 70/30 or 90/10 (random variation).

\textbf{Stratified 5-fold}: Each fold has exactly 16 class A, 4 class B (80/20 maintained).
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Time Series Cross-Validation}

\begin{definition}[Time Series Split]
For temporal data, cannot randomly shuffle (violates temporal order).

\textbf{Forward chaining}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Fold 1: Train on $[1:n_1]$, validate on $[n_1+1:n_2]$
\item Fold 2: Train on $[1:n_2]$, validate on $[n_2+1:n_3]$
\item Fold 3: Train on $[1:n_3]$, validate on $[n_3+1:n_4]$
\item ...
\end{itemize}

Train on the past and validate on future observations. This ordering alone does not prevent leakage: feature windows, preprocessing, label availability and any necessary train-validation gap must also respect the forecast time.
\end{definition}

\subsection{A Protocol That Includes Calibration and Decisions}
\label{sec:four-block-protocol}
The training/validation/test terminology of Part 0 describes information roles.
A validation role can require several disjoint blocks when several operations
learn from labels. The following conservative example separates four roles:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{TRAIN}: search hyperparameters with fold-local preprocessing,
then refit the selected base pipeline on TRAIN.
\item \textbf{CALIBRATION}: fit a probability map to the frozen base scores.
\item \textbf{POLICY}: choose the cost threshold of the frozen calibrated model.
\item \textbf{TEST}: report the complete frozen procedure and prespecified baselines.
\end{enumerate}
Do not refit the base model after fitting its calibrator and threshold: that
changes their score distribution. If refitting on additional non-test data is
required, do it before allocating fresh calibration and policy data, or design
a suitable cross-fitting protocol. A calibrator is a learned model; a selected
threshold is a learned decision rule. Neither belongs on final test labels.

\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,b/.style={draw,rounded corners,text width=2.7cm,minimum height=1.2cm},>=Latex]
\node[b,fill=blue!10] (a) at (0,0) {TRAIN\\search + refit base};
\node[b,fill=green!10] (b) at (3.8,0) {CALIBRATION\\fit score map};
\node[b,fill=orange!15] (c) at (7.6,0) {POLICY\\choose threshold};
\node[b,fill=gray!12] (d) at (3.8,-2) {TEST\\report frozen system};
\draw[->,thick] (a)--(b);\draw[->,thick] (b)--(c);
\draw[->,thick] (c.south)|-(d.east);
\node at (3.8,1.1) {Groups disjoint; event and label-availability times checked};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Information flows forward. Every learned operation has a declared
fitting block; final test results have no return arrow into selection.}
\label{fig:b4-protocol}\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Groups, time, and label availability together]
The runnable device example uses 260 synthetic devices, three visits each.
Device $g$ has event times $10g,10g+1,10g+2$ and labels available two time
units later. Predeclared blocks contain devices 0--119, 120--159, 160--199,
and 200--259: 360, 120, 120, and 180 rows respectively. This evaluates
future \emph{unseen devices}; it is a different target from future visits to
known devices. Device identity and times are split metadata, not predictors.

Within TRAIN, three expanding splits each validate on 20 complete devices
and exclude a one-device gap. Exact sizes and boundaries are:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{rrrrr}\toprule
Fold & Train rows & Validation rows & Last training label ready & First validation event\\\midrule
1&177&60&584&600\\2&237&60&784&800\\3&297&60&984&1000\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
All training labels are available before the validation origin and device IDs
are disjoint. The gap is measured in devices in this construction; a library
row gap is not automatically a duration or a group gap. If a forecast label
uses a horizon of two days, require its entire label window to end before the
validation origin. At origin day 5, labels ending at day $t+2$ are available
strictly before day 5 only for integer $t\le2$. Use availability timestamps,
not just row ordering. For rolling deployment to known devices, allow their
available past histories but never future observations; the split target must
match the deployment question.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[What the nested audit actually estimates]
Six $(C,\text{class weight})$ settings and three forward folds cost
$6\cdot3+1=19$ base-model fits including the final TRAIN refit. The calibrator
adds one fit; evaluating nine policy thresholds adds no base-model fits.
A separate audit confined to TRAIN uses three outer folds and two inner
folds: $3(6\cdot2+1)=39$ base fits. Its held-out outer mean log losses are
$.480678,.476870,.607009$, with mean $.521519$. Each outer search happens
to choose $C=10$ and no class weighting. The main search selects the same
setting and has the same mean score; nesting does not guarantee a numerically
worse score on every dataset. This audit estimates \emph{base-pipeline
hyperparameter selection}, not the full calibration-and-policy procedure.
Auditing the latter requires fitting all those stages inside each outer
training portion. The final 180-row TEST evaluates the full frozen system.
\end{example}

\subsection{Hyperparameter Tuning}

\subsubsection{Grid Search}

\begin{definition}[Grid Search]
Exhaustively search over a specified hyperparameter grid.

\textbf{Example}: For SVM with RBF kernel:
\begin{itemize}
\item $C \in \{0.1, 1, 10, 100\}$
\item $\gamma \in \{0.001, 0.01, 0.1, 1\}$
\end{itemize}
Test all $4 \times 4 = 16$ combinations.
\end{definition}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Grid Search with Cross-Validation}

\textbf{Input}: Training data $\mathcal{D}$, model, hyperparameter grid, $K$ for CV

\begin{enumerate}
\item Generate all combinations of hyperparameters from grid

\item \textbf{For} each combination $\theta$:
\begin{itemize}
\item Run $K$-fold CV with hyperparameters $\theta$
\item Record mean CV score $\bar{M}(\theta)$
\end{itemize}

\item Select best: $\theta^* = \argmax_\theta \bar{M}(\theta)$

\item Refit the selected base procedure on its designated fitting block with $\theta^*$. If calibration or threshold selection is needed, retain their separate non-test blocks. Freeze every stage before evaluating on the untouched test set
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Output}: Best hyperparameters $\theta^*$, trained model
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Grid Search Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Simple, easy to implement
\item Guaranteed to find best combination in grid
\item Parallelizable (each combination independent)
\item Reproducible
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Curse of dimensionality}: $n$ parameters with $k$ values each $\to$ $k^n$ combinations
\item Wastes computation on unpromising regions
\item Grid may miss optimal values between grid points
\item Expensive for large grids or slow models
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Best practices}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Start with coarse grid, then refine around best values
\item Use logarithmic spacing for parameters spanning orders of magnitude
\item Limit grid size (test 3-5 values per parameter max)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Random Search}

\begin{definition}[Random Search]
Sample hyperparameter combinations randomly from specified distributions.

Instead of grid, define distributions:
\begin{itemize}
\item $C \sim \text{LogUniform}(10^{-3}, 10^3)$
\item $\gamma \sim \text{LogUniform}(10^{-4}, 10^1)$
\end{itemize}

Sample $n$ random combinations, evaluate each.
\end{definition}

\begin{intuition}[Random Search Coverage]
Random search is often more efficient than grid search because:
\begin{itemize}
\item Some hyperparameters matter more than others
\item Random search explores more unique values for important parameters
\item May cover relevant coordinates more finely under a suitable search distribution; no universal performance ordering follows
\end{itemize}

For $n$ trials with independent continuous parameter distributions (discrete values can repeat):
\begin{itemize}
\item Grid search with 2 parameters: $\sqrt{n} \times \sqrt{n}$ grid
\item Random search: Almost surely $n$ distinct sampled values per continuous parameter; no such guarantee holds for discrete parameters
\end{itemize}
\end{intuition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small]
\pgfmathsetseed{20261119}
\foreach \offset/\kind in {0/Grid,6/Random} {
  \begin{scope}[shift={(\offset,0)}]
    \draw[->] (0,0)--(4.2,0);
    \draw[->] (0,0)--(0,4.2);
    \node at (2,-0.5) {Important parameter};
    \node[rotate=90] at (-0.5,2) {Less important parameter};
    \node at (2,4.7) {\kind\ search};
  \end{scope}
}
\foreach \x in {0.5,1.5,2.5,3.5}
  \foreach \y in {0.5,1.5,2.5,3.5} \fill[blue] (\x,\y) circle (2pt);
\foreach \i in {1,...,16} {
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\x}{6.2+3.6*rnd}
  \pgfmathsetmacro{\y}{0.2+3.6*rnd}
  \fill[orange!80!black] (\x,\y) circle (2pt);
}
\node at (2,-1.1) {4 distinct values per axis};
\node at (8,-1.1) {16 continuous samples per axis};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Grid versus random search with 16 evaluations. Independent continuous sampling explores more distinct values along each axis. The schematic random draw uses a fixed seed for reproducible rendering.}
\end{figure}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Random Search in Practice}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.model_selection import RandomizedSearchCV
from scipy.stats import loguniform, randint

# Define distributions
param_distributions = {
    'C': loguniform(1e-3, 1e3),
    'gamma': loguniform(1e-4, 1e1),
    'kernel': ['rbf', 'poly']
}

# Random search with 100 iterations
random_search = RandomizedSearchCV(
    estimator=SVC(),
    param_distributions=param_distributions,
    n_iter=100,  # number of random samples
    cv=5,
    random_state=42,
    n_jobs=-1  # parallel
)

random_search.fit(X_train, y_train)
best_params = random_search.best_params_
\end{lstlisting}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsubsection{Bayesian Optimization}

\begin{definition}[Bayesian Optimization]
Intelligent search that uses past evaluations to guide future trials.

\textbf{Key idea}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Build probabilistic model (surrogate) of $f(\theta)$ where $\theta$ are hyperparameters
\item Use model to select promising $\theta$ (acquisition function)
\item Evaluate $f(\theta)$, update model
\item Repeat
\end{enumerate}

Common surrogate: Gaussian Process (GP)

Common acquisition functions:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Expected Improvement (EI)}: Balance exploration/exploitation
\item \textbf{Upper Confidence Bound (UCB)}: $\mu(\theta) + \kappa\sigma(\theta)$
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Bayesian Optimization vs Random/Grid}:

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Can improve sample efficiency when the surrogate and acquisition assumptions suit the objective
\item Learns from past trials
\item Handles expensive black-box functions well
\item Can incorporate prior knowledge
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Disadvantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item More complex to implement
\item Overhead in building surrogate model
\item Less parallelizable
\item Can get stuck in local optima
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Expensive evaluations (training takes hours)
\item Limited budget (can only try 10-50 configurations)
\item Continuous hyperparameters
\item Black-box optimization
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Popular libraries}: Optuna, Hyperopt, Scikit-Optimize, Ray Tune
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Nested Cross-Validation}

\begin{definition}[Nested CV]
To estimate the performance of the entire hyperparameter-selection procedure using held-out outer folds:

\textbf{Outer loop} (performance estimation):
\begin{itemize}
\item $K_{\text{outer}}$-fold CV
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Inner loop} (hyperparameter selection):
\begin{itemize}
\item For each outer fold, run $K_{\text{inner}}$-fold CV on training portion
\item Select best hyperparameters
\item Refit the selected entire procedure on the outer training portion, then evaluate on the untouched outer fold
\end{itemize}

This separates tuning from evaluation, reducing the selection optimism caused by reusing validation scores. Each outer model uses less data than the final all-non-test-data fit, so the result is not an exactly unbiased estimate of that final model's risk.
