Learned preprocessing must be fitted inside the inner folds and refitted on the outer training portion; outer scores must not guide further tuning without a new evaluation boundary.
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small]
\node at (5.5,3.2) {\textbf{Outer split: performance evaluation}};
\filldraw[fill=blue!20] (0,2)--(8,2)--(8,2.8)--(0,2.8)--cycle;
\filldraw[fill=orange!25] (8,2)--(11,2)--(11,2.8)--(8,2.8)--cycle;
\node at (4,2.4) {Outer training set};
\node at (9.5,2.4) {Outer test set};
\draw[->,thick] (4,2)--(4,1.5);
\node at (4,1.2) {\textbf{Inner split: hyperparameter tuning}};
\filldraw[fill=blue!20] (0,0)--(5.5,0)--(5.5,0.8)--(0,0.8)--cycle;
\filldraw[fill=orange!25] (5.5,0)--(8,0)--(8,0.8)--(5.5,0.8)--cycle;
\node at (2.75,0.4) {Inner training};
\node at (6.75,0.4) {Inner validation};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{One outer and one inner split of nested CV. Inner folds partition only the outer training set. After choosing hyperparameters, refit on the full outer training set before evaluating on the untouched outer test set; repeat over outer folds.}
\end{figure}

% =======================
\subsection{Search Budgets and Acquisition Functions}
\begin{example}[Random-search coverage and minimization EI]
If each independent random configuration has probability $q$ of landing in
a prespecified useful region, $B$ trials hit it with probability
$1-(1-q)^B$. For $q=.05$, this is $.6415$ at $B=20$ and $.9539$ at $B=60$.
This describes coverage under the chosen search distribution, not guaranteed
superiority to a grid or guaranteed held-out performance.

For minimizing validation loss, assume a surrogate predicts
$F(\theta)\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2)$ and the current best loss is $f_*$. With
improvement margin $\xi\ge0$, integrate the positive part
$I=(f_*-\xi-F)_+$ to obtain
\[
\mathrm{EI}(\theta)=(f_*-\xi-\mu)\Phi(z)+\sigma\phi(z),\qquad
z=\frac{f_*-\xi-\mu}{\sigma}.
\]
For $\sigma=0$ use $(f_*-\xi-\mu)_+$. At $f_*=.2,\xi=0$, assumed
predictions $N(.18,.03^2)$ and $N(.22,.05^2)$ yield EI approximately
$.024534$ and $.011522$: a worse predicted mean can still have positive EI.
Maximize EI while minimizing the underlying loss. An optimistic confidence
bound for minimization is a \emph{lower} bound $\mu-\kappa\sigma$; the
previous upper-bound form applies to reward maximization. Surrogate quality,
noise, and budget determine whether this search is useful.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.9\linewidth]{figures/batch4/expected-improvement.pdf}
\caption{Assumed normal surrogate predictions, not a fitted GP or an observed
training experiment. Shaded density lies below the current best loss.}
\end{figure}

\section{Data Preprocessing}
\label{sec:preprocessing-leakage}
Apply the splitting and fold-local fitting rules in Sections~\ref{sec:learning-protocol} and~\ref{sec:cv-protocol} to every learned preprocessing step below. Fit statistics on the current training fold, transform its validation fold without refitting, and repeat from scratch in the next round.
% =======================

\subsection{Feature Scaling}

\subsubsection{Standardization (Z-score Normalization)}

\begin{definition}[Standardization]
Transform features to have mean 0 and standard deviation 1:
\[
x_{\text{scaled}} = \frac{x - \mu}{\sigma}
\]
where $\mu = \frac{1}{n}\sum_i x_i$ and $\sigma = \sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_i (x_i - \mu)^2}$.

For a nonconstant training feature, these fitted statistics give training empirical mean zero and population-form empirical variance one. They do not force held-out data to have mean zero or variance one, and do not make a non-Gaussian distribution Gaussian. A truly Gaussian population becomes exactly $\mathcal N(0,1)$ only when transformed using its true population mean and standard deviation. Constant features need a separate convention.
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Min-Max Normalization]
Scale features to a fixed range, typically $[0, 1]$:
\[
x_{\text{scaled}} = \frac{x - x_{\min}}{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}
\]

Fit the minimum and maximum on training data only, and require $x_{\max}>x_{\min}$ (constant features need a separate convention). New observations outside the training range can transform outside $[0,1]$ unless explicitly clipped.

For range $[a,b]$:
\[
x_{\text{scaled}} = a + (b - a) \cdot \frac{x - x_{\min}}{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{When to use which}:

\textbf{Standardization}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Features have different units/scales
\item Many ML algorithms (SVM, logistic regression, neural networks)
\item Mean and standard deviation are meaningful summaries; they are sensitive to outliers, so heavy contamination may call for robust scaling
\item Preserves outlier information (can go beyond $[0,1]$)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Min-Max}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Need bounded range (e.g., $[0,1]$)
\item Neural networks (especially with bounded activations like sigmoid)
\item Image data (pixel values)
\item Zero is preserved only when it coincides with the chosen lower reference for the transform
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When NOT to scale}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Exact threshold-based trees depend on feature order, so strictly monotone feature transformations preserve available partitions; numerical and approximate split procedures can differ
\item Features already on same scale
\item Interpretability matters (keep original units)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Critical: Fit on Training, Transform on Test}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler

# Correct way
scaler = StandardScaler()
X_train_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(X_train)
X_test_scaled = scaler.transform(X_test)  # Use training statistics

# Wrong way (data leakage!)
# scaler.fit(X_test)  # Never fit on test data!
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Why?} Under ordinary inductive evaluation, learned statistics exclude test inputs. A declared descriptive/transductive analysis has a different scope, as discussed in Section~\ref{sec:unsupervised-protocol}.
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Encoding Categorical Variables}

\subsubsection{Label Encoding}

\begin{definition}[Label Encoding]
Map categories to integers:
\[
\text{Red} \to 0, \quad \text{Green} \to 1, \quad \text{Blue} \to 2
\]

\textbf{Issue}: Introduces artificial ordering ($\text{Blue} > \text{Green} > \text{Red}$).

\textbf{When to use}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Ordinal variables (Low $<$ Medium $<$ High)
\item For feature columns, use an ordinal encoder when an ordering is meaningful. Arbitrary integer codes impose an artificial order on numerical-threshold trees; native categorical split support is implementation-specific
\item Target variable in classification
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{One-Hot Encoding}

\begin{definition}[One-Hot Encoding]
Create binary column for each category:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{c|ccc}
Original & Red & Green & Blue \\
\hline
Red & 1 & 0 & 0 \\
Green & 0 & 1 & 0 \\
Blue & 0 & 0 & 1 \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

For $K$ categories, use $K$ binary features, or a $K-1$ reference encoding when needed for identifiability with an unpenalized intercept. Dropping a category is not compulsory for every regularized or tree-based model.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{One-Hot Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item No artificial ordering
\item Works with linear models
\item Standard approach for neural networks
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{High cardinality}: Many categories $\to$ many features
\item \textbf{Sparse matrices}: Most values are 0
\item \textbf{Curse of dimensionality}: $K$ categories = $K$ new dimensions
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Alternatives for high cardinality}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Target encoding}: Replace a category with a training-estimated target statistic; use out-of-fold or ordered estimates to avoid leaking a row's own target
\item \textbf{Frequency encoding}: Replace with category frequency
\item \textbf{Embedding layers}: Neural network learns dense representations (NLP, RecSys)
\item \textbf{Hashing trick}: Hash categories to fixed number of buckets
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Handling Missing Data}

\begin{definition}[Types of Missingness]

\textbf{1. MCAR (Missing Completely At Random)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Missingness independent of observed and unobserved data
\item Example: Responses lost by a failure independent of response content and participant attributes
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. MAR (Missing At Random)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Conditional on observed data, missingness does not further depend on the missing values
\item Example: Older people less likely to report income (age is observed)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. MNAR (Missing Not At Random)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Missingness depends on unobserved data
\item Example: High earners don't report income (income itself causes missingness)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{definition}[Handling Strategies]

\textbf{1. Deletion}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Listwise}: Remove entire row if any feature missing
\item \textbf{Pairwise}: Use available data for each analysis
\item \textbf{Assessment}: Complete-case validity depends on the missingness mechanism and analysis; there is no universal 5\% safety rule
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Imputation}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Mean/Median/Mode}: Simple, fast, but ignores relationships
\item \textbf{Forward/Backward fill}: Respect the prediction time. Backward fill uses future observations and is inappropriate when those observations would be unavailable at forecasting time
\item \textbf{KNN imputation}: Use $k$ nearest neighbors' values
\item \textbf{Model-based}: Regression, EM algorithm, MICE
\item \textbf{Indicator variable}: Add binary flag for missingness (preserves information)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Model-Specific}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Some algorithms handle missing values natively (XGBoost, LightGBM)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Imputation Best Practices}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer, KNNImputer

# Simple imputation
imputer = SimpleImputer(strategy='mean')  # or 'median', 'most_frequent'
X_train_imputed = imputer.fit_transform(X_train)
X_test_imputed = imputer.transform(X_test)

# KNN imputation (more sophisticated)
knn_imputer = KNNImputer(n_neighbors=5)
X_train_imputed = knn_imputer.fit_transform(X_train)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Remember}: Always fit imputer on training data only!
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Outlier Detection and Treatment}

\begin{definition}[Outlier Detection Methods]

\textbf{1. Statistical}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Z-score}: $|z| > 3$ (assuming Gaussian)
\item \textbf{IQR method}: Below $Q_1 - 1.5 \cdot IQR$ or above $Q_3 + 1.5 \cdot IQR$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Distance-based}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{DBSCAN}: Points marked as noise
\item \textbf{KNN}: Points with large distance to $k$-th neighbor
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Model-based}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Isolation Forest}: Isolates outliers in random trees
\item \textbf{One-Class SVM}: Learns boundary around normal data
\item \textbf{Local Outlier Factor (LOF)}: Compares local density
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Handling Outliers}:

\textbf{1. Investigate first}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Data error (typo, measurement error)? \ensuremath{\to} Correct or remove
\item Valid extreme value? \ensuremath{\to} Keep (important information)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Treatment options}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Keep}: If valid and informative
\item \textbf{Remove}: Only under a declared error/eligibility rule defining the target population; do not remove held-out rows after inspecting their labels or prediction errors
\item \textbf{Cap/Winsorize}: Replace with threshold (e.g., 95th percentile)
\item \textbf{Transform}: Log, square root to reduce impact
\item \textbf{Separate model}: Build model specifically for outliers
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Model robustness}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Robustness depends on the perturbation}: Trees are insensitive to strictly monotone feature rescaling, but squared-error leaf means can be sensitive to extreme targets; Huber loss limits large-residual influence
\item \textbf{Sensitive models}: Linear regression, KNN, SVM (need preprocessing)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\section{Feature Engineering}
\label{sec:feature-engineering}
% =======================

\subsection{Feature Extraction vs Selection}

\begin{definition}[Feature Engineering]

\textbf{Feature Extraction}: Create new features from existing ones
\begin{itemize}
\item PCA: Linear combinations
\item Polynomial features: $x_1 \cdot x_2$, $x_1^2$
\item Domain-specific transformations
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Feature Selection}: Choose subset of existing features
\begin{itemize}
\item Remove irrelevant or redundant features
\item Improve model performance and interpretability
\item Reduce overfitting and training time
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Feature Selection Methods}

\subsubsection{Filter Methods}

\begin{definition}[Filter Methods]
Select features based on statistical measures, independent of model.

\textbf{1. Correlation}:
Remove features highly correlated with each other (redundant).

\textbf{2. Mutual Information}:
\[
MI(X, Y) = \sum_{x,y} P(x,y) \log\frac{P(x,y)}{P(x)P(y)}
\]
This is the discrete-variable formula; continuous variables require the corresponding density integral and an appropriate estimator. It measures dependence, including nonlinear dependence, rather than a causal effect.

\textbf{3. Chi-Square Test}:
For categorical features and classification:
\[
\chi^2 = \sum_{i,j} \frac{(O_{ij} - E_{ij})^2}{E_{ij}}
\]
Tests independence between feature and target.

\textbf{4. ANOVA F-test}:
For continuous features and categorical target. Tests if feature means differ across classes.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Filter Methods}:

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Fast (no model training)
\item Model-agnostic
\item Good for preprocessing
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Ignores feature interactions
\item Doesn't consider model performance
\item May select redundant features
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Wrapper Methods}

\begin{definition}[Wrapper Methods]
Use model performance to guide feature selection.

\textbf{1. Forward Selection}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Start with no features
\item Add one feature at a time (choose best improvement)
\item Stop when no improvement
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{2. Backward Elimination}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Start with all features
\item Remove one feature at a time (remove worst)
\item Stop when removal hurts performance
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{3. Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE)}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Train model on all features
\item Rank features by importance
\item Remove least important
\item Repeat until desired number of features
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Recursive Feature Elimination (RFE)}

\textbf{Input}: Features $\vect{X}$, target $\vect{y}$, model, target feature count $k$

\textbf{Initialize}: Current feature set $\mathcal{F} = \{\text{all features}\}$

\textbf{While} $|\mathcal{F}| > k$:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Train model on features in $\mathcal{F}$
\item Compute feature importances (e.g., coefficients, Gini importance)
\item Remove the least important feature from $\mathcal{F}$; for coefficient-based ranking account for feature scale and use magnitude rather than signed coefficient
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Output}: Selected features $\mathcal{F}$. For performance evaluation, repeat feature selection within each training fold; do not select using held-out labels.
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Wrapper Methods}:

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Sequential wrappers can use validation performance; ordinary RFE ranks fitted-model importances, while RFECV uses CV to select the retained count
\item Can capture feature interactions
\item Optimizes for specific model
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Computationally expensive (many model trainings)
\item Risk of overfitting (especially with small data)
\item Model-specific (selected features may not work for other models)
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{Embedded Methods}

\begin{definition}[Embedded Methods]
Feature selection built into model training.

\textbf{1. L1 Regularization (Lasso)}:
Penalty $\lambda\sum_j |w_j|$ drives some weights to exactly zero.

\textbf{2. Tree-based Feature Importance}:
Random Forest, XGBoost provide importance scores. Select top $k$ features.

\textbf{3. ElasticNet}:
Combines L1 (sparsity) and L2 (grouping) penalties.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Embedded Methods}:

\textbf{Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Faster than wrapper methods
\item Less overfitting risk
\item Integrated with training
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Best Practice}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Start with filter methods (fast preprocessing)
\item Use embedded methods if model supports (L1, tree importance)
\item Use wrapper methods for critical applications with budget
\end{enumerate}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Domain-Specific Feature Engineering}

\begin{example}[Common Transformations]

\textbf{1. Time Features} (from datetime):
\begin{itemize}
\item Hour, day of week, month, year
\item Is weekend? Is holiday?
\item Time since event
\item Cyclic encoding: $\sin(2\pi \cdot \text{hour}/24)$, $\cos(2\pi \cdot \text{hour}/24)$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Text Features}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Bag of words, TF-IDF
\item N-grams
\item Length, number of words, capitals
\item Sentiment scores
\item Word embeddings (Word2Vec, GloVe)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Geospatial}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Distance to landmark
\item Cluster of locations
\item Urban vs rural classification
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Interaction Features}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Products: $x_1 \cdot x_2$
\item Ratios: $x_1 / x_2$
\item Differences: $x_1 - x_2$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{5. Aggregations} (for grouped data):
\begin{itemize}
\item Mean, median, std per group
\item Count, min, max per group
\item Time-based: rolling averages, lag features
\end{itemize}
\end{example}

\subsection{Preprocessing and Feature Availability: An Auditable Example}
\label{sec:feature-availability}
\begin{example}[Fit statistics on precisely the current training block]
For training numeric values $(1,\mathrm{missing},3)$, the fitted median is 2.
After imputation the mean is 2 and the population standard deviation used by
\texttt{StandardScaler} is $\sqrt{2/3}$. A held-out value 5 transforms to
$3/\sqrt{2/3}$; it does not change that mean or standard deviation.
A held-out missing value transforms to zero after median imputation.

For training categories blue and red, one-hot columns are fixed by training.
With \texttt{handle\_unknown='ignore'}, unseen green maps to an all-zero
vector. That is a declared fallback, not a learned effect for green. Dropping
a reference category can make its representation indistinguishable from an
unknown category. Missing categories need their own explicit policy.
The runnable example permits missing numerics but requires a nonempty category;
it retains all request rows and their order.
\end{example}

Let $R$ denote an observation indicator. MAR means the conditional law of $R$
given all data depends only on the observed portion, equivalently an appropriate
conditional independence from the missing values given observed values.
It does not mean marginal independence from every unobserved variable.
An independent random server failure could produce MCAR; a failure associated
with device load may not. Neither a low missing fraction nor MAR by itself
licenses unbiased complete-case analysis. A missingness mechanism generally
cannot be identified from the observed sample alone.

\begin{example}[Selection and temporal joins can leak before a Pipeline]
Suppose an identifier appears in both folds, or a feature table contains
``number of failures in the next week.'' A perfectly fold-local scaler cannot
repair either issue. At prediction time $t$, a joined record must have both
its event time and its availability time no later than $t$. Use an as-of join
against a versioned history, not the latest corrected table.

For a current-outcome task, a within-device rolling average of past outcomes
requires sorting, grouping, and a lag before rolling. In pandas, for outcomes
that were available by the next visit, one possible expression is
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
history = history.sort_values(['device_id', 'event_time'])
history['past_mean'] = history.groupby('device_id')['target'].transform(
    lambda s: s.shift(1).rolling(3, min_periods=1).mean())
\end{lstlisting}
With delayed labels, the lag alone is insufficient: filter by label-availability
time for each query. In the future-unseen-device protocol, previous training
devices' outcomes cannot be used as the new device's history. Keep split IDs,
feature timestamps, and availability checks outside the model's public inputs.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[A univariate selector can discard the whole signal]
Let $X_1,X_2$ be independent fair bits and $Y=X_1\mathbin{\mathrm{XOR}}X_2$.
Each single feature has $I(X_j;Y)=0$, but the pair determines $Y$, so
$I((X_1,X_2);Y)=H(Y)=\log2$. An aggressive univariate filter removes both
useful features. Feature selection must be tuned inside the training folds;
filter methods do not inherently prevent leakage or preserve interactions.
The scikit-learn chi-squared selector expects nonnegative feature values
(such as counts), not arbitrary standardized negative measurements.
Target encoding needs training-row out-of-fold estimates as well as a map
fitted on the fold's training data for held-out transformation. Learn
winsorization cutoffs and imputation parameters there too. Do not remove test
rows because their outcomes or prediction errors look inconvenient: that changes
the evaluation population after seeing results.
\end{example}

\subsection{Summary of Part 6}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Key Takeaways]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Classification Metrics}: Confusion matrix, precision, recall, F1. ROC-AUC measures ranking; PR highlights positive-class performance, especially for rare positives. Neither curve alone specifies the best decision threshold.

\item \textbf{Regression Metrics}: MAE (linear residual penalty), RMSE (penalizes large errors), R\textsuperscript{2} (variance explained). Choose based on error characteristics.

\item \textbf{Cross-Validation}: K-fold for robust evaluation. Stratified for classification. Time series split for temporal data. Nested CV for hyperparameter tuning.

\item \textbf{Hyperparameter Tuning}: Grid search (exhaustive), random search (efficient), Bayesian optimization (sample-efficient for expensive evaluations).

\item \textbf{Feature Scaling}: Standardization (most ML algorithms), Min-Max (bounded activations). Always fit on training, transform on test.

\item \textbf{Categorical Encoding}: Use ordinal encoding for ordered feature categories, one-hot or configured categorical models for nominal features. Target encoding must be fitted within the training/validation scheme.

\item \textbf{Missing Data}: Understand missingness type (MCAR/MAR/MNAR). Imputation strategies from simple (mean) to complex (KNN, model-based).

\item \textbf{Feature Selection}: Filter (fast, model-agnostic), wrapper (model-specific, expensive), embedded (integrated). Use based on computational budget and goals.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Next Steps}: Part 7 covers practical tools and deployment: WEKA, scikit-learn pipelines, and model serving strategies.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Given confusion matrix with TP=85, TN=40, FP=10, FN=15, compute accuracy, precision, recall, F1, and specificity.

\item Explain why accuracy is misleading for a dataset with 95\% negative class, 5\% positive class. What metrics would you use instead?

\item For a cancer detection model, would you prioritize precision or recall? Justify your answer and suggest an appropriate threshold strategy.

\item Implement 5-fold cross-validation by hand for a dataset with 100 examples. Show which examples go in which fold.

\item Why must we fit the scaler on training data only? What happens if we fit on all data (train + test)?

\item Design one-hot encoding for categorical variable \{Red, Green, Blue, Red, Green\}. How many columns? Should you use $K$ or $K-1$ columns?

\item Compare computational complexity: Grid search with 4 parameters (5 values each) vs random search with 100 iterations. Which tries more unique combinations for each parameter?

\item For time series data, why can't we use regular k-fold cross-validation? Design a proper time series CV scheme for 120 monthly observations.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

% =======================
% Part 7: Tools, Pipelines, and Deployment
% =======================
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 7: Tools, Pipelines, and Deployment}
\section*{Part 7: Tools, Pipelines, and Deployment}

\section{WEKA: Practical Guide}
\label{sec:weka}

\subsection{Introduction to WEKA}

\begin{definition}[WEKA]
\textbf{WEKA (Waikato Environment for Knowledge Analysis)} is a collection of machine learning algorithms for data mining tasks. Developed at University of Waikato, New Zealand.

\textbf{Key Features}:
\begin{itemize}
\item GUI-based interface (no coding required)
\item Extensive algorithm library (classification, regression, clustering, etc.)
\item Built-in visualization tools
\item Java-based (cross-platform)
\item Free and open-source
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to use WEKA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Quick prototyping and experimentation
\item Teaching and learning ML concepts
\item Comparing multiple algorithms easily
\item Small to medium datasets
\item When GUI is preferred over coding
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{WEKA Explorer Interface}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{WEKA Explorer has 6 main tabs}:

\textbf{1. Preprocess}: Load data, view statistics, apply filters
\begin{itemize}
\item Import ARFF, CSV, JDBC databases
\item View attribute statistics and distributions
\item Apply filters (discretization, normalization, attribute selection)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Classify}: Train classification/regression models
\begin{itemize}
\item Choose algorithm from tree structure
\item Configure hyperparameters
\item Select test options (cross-validation, percentage split, etc.)
\item View results: accuracy, confusion matrix, ROC
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Cluster}: Perform clustering
\begin{itemize}
\item K-means, EM, DBSCAN, hierarchical
\item Evaluate clusters
\item Visualize assignments
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Associate}: Find association rules
\begin{itemize}
\item Apriori, FP-Growth
\item Set minimum support and confidence
\end{itemize}

\textbf{5. Select attributes}: Feature selection
\begin{itemize}
\item Various search and evaluation methods
\item Rank features by importance
\end{itemize}

\textbf{6. Visualize}: Data exploration
\begin{itemize}
\item Scatter plots, histograms
\item Class distributions
\item Interactive exploration
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Data Format: ARFF}

\begin{definition}[ARFF Format]
ARFF (Attribute-Relation File Format) is WEKA's native format.

\textbf{Structure}:
\begin{lstlisting}
@relation weather

@attribute outlook {sunny, overcast, rainy}
@attribute temperature numeric
@attribute humidity numeric
@attribute windy {TRUE, FALSE}
@attribute play {yes, no}

@data
sunny,85,85,FALSE,no
sunny,80,90,TRUE,no
overcast,83,86,FALSE,yes
rainy,70,96,FALSE,yes
...
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Components}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{@relation}: Dataset name
\item \texttt{@attribute}: Feature name and type (numeric, nominal, string, date)
\item \texttt{@data}: Actual data rows
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Workflow Example: Classification}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{WEKA Classification Workflow}

\textbf{Step 1: Load Data}
\begin{itemize}
\item Open Explorer $\to$ Preprocess tab
\item Click ``Open file...'' $\to$ Select ARFF or CSV
\item Review attributes in the table
\item Check class distribution (click on class attribute)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 2: Preprocess (Optional)}
\begin{itemize}
\item Configure the desired learned preprocessing, such as \texttt{unsupervised.attribute.Normalize}.
\item For cross-validation, place the filter inside \texttt{meta.FilteredClassifier} and choose the base classifier there. Do not fit a learned filter to the full dataset before CV.
\item For a supplied test set, learn the filter only on the training set and apply that fitted filter to test instances.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 3: Select Algorithm}
\begin{itemize}
\item Click ``Classify'' tab
\item Click ``Choose'' button
\item Choose a base model such as \texttt{trees.J48}; with learned preprocessing, select it as the classifier inside \texttt{meta.FilteredClassifier}
\item Click on algorithm name to configure parameters
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 4: Choose Test Method}
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Cross-validation}: Select folds (default 10)
\item \textbf{Percentage split}: e.g., 66\% train, 34\% test
\item \textbf{Use training set}: Evaluate on training (overfitting check)
\item \textbf{Supplied test set}: Load separate test file
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 5: Run and Evaluate}
\begin{itemize}
\item Click ``Start'' button
\item View results in output area:
\begin{itemize}
\item Summary statistics
\item Confusion matrix
\item Detailed accuracy by class
\end{itemize}
\item Right-click on result $\to$ Visualize for plots
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Step 6: Compare Models}
\begin{itemize}
\item Run multiple algorithms (results stack in result list)
\item Use paired exported evaluations or Experimenter with matched splits for comparisons; menu availability depends on the interface/version
\item Export results: Right-click $\to$ Save result buffer
\end{itemize}
\end{algorithmbox}

\subsection{Common WEKA Algorithms}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Category} & \textbf{Algorithms in WEKA} \\
\midrule
\textbf{Trees} & J48 (C4.5), RandomForest, REPTree \\
\textbf{Rules} & JRip, PART, DecisionTable \\
\textbf{Functions} & Logistic, MultilayerPerceptron, SMO (SVM) \\
\textbf{Lazy} & IBk (KNN), LWL \\
\textbf{Bayes} & NaiveBayes, BayesNet \\
\textbf{Meta} & AdaBoostM1, Bagging, Stacking, Vote \\
\textbf{Clustering} & SimpleKMeans, EM, DBSCAN, HierarchicalClusterer \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Common algorithms in WEKA by category}
\end{table}

\subsection{Visualizing Results}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{WEKA Visualization Tools}:

\textbf{1. ROC Curve}:
\begin{itemize}
\item After classification: Right-click result $\to$ Visualize threshold curve
\item Choose class and metric (TPR vs FPR for ROC)
\item Compare multiple classifiers on same plot
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Classification Errors}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Right-click result $\to$ Visualize classifier errors
\item Shows 2D plot of predictions
\item Misclassified instances highlighted
\item Interactive: hover for details
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Tree Visualization}:
\begin{itemize}
\item For a drawable single-tree classifier such as J48, right-click its result and select ``Visualize tree''; a whole forest is not one such tree
\item Shows decision rules graphically
\item Can save as PNG/PDF
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Cost/Benefit Analysis}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Define cost matrix for different types of errors
\item WEKA computes expected cost
\item Useful for imbalanced datasets
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Advantages and Limitations}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{WEKA Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Easy to use}: GUI-based, no programming required
\item \textbf{Comprehensive}: 100+ algorithms built-in
\item \textbf{Educational}: Excellent for learning ML
\item \textbf{Visualization}: Good built-in plots
\item \textbf{Reproducible}: Can save configurations
\end{itemize}

\textbf{WEKA Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Integration requirements}: WEKA also has Java APIs and command-line interfaces; production suitability depends on the application and integration, not on GUI availability
\item \textbf{Scalability}: Limited to datasets that fit in memory
\item \textbf{Packages}: Available methods depend on installed packages and Java dependencies; verify the exact estimator rather than assuming every extension is bundled
\item \textbf{Automation}: Reproducible automation uses its command-line or Java interfaces rather than manual GUI steps
\item \textbf{Customization}: Limited compared to programming libraries
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Best Use Cases}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Academic research and teaching
\item Quick baseline comparisons
\item Data exploration
\item Proof of concept
\end{itemize}

\textbf{When to move beyond WEKA}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Datasets whose feature representation or computation exceeds available memory/time; row count alone is insufficient
\item Production deployment
\item Need for latest algorithms
\item Complex preprocessing pipelines
\item Integration with other systems
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsection{Reproducible WEKA Evaluation with the Same Split Boundaries}
\label{sec:weka-forward}
The repository workflow exports complete ARFF files, not excerpts containing
ellipsis. Predictors are two numeric signals and nominal device type; the
last attribute is the nominal class $\{0,1\}$. Numeric missing values use
\texttt{?}. The allowed category schema $\{alpha,beta,gamma\}$ is specified
in advance; gamma is permitted even if absent from training. IDs, times, and
split names are excluded from model attributes.

\begin{example}[A supplied validation fold with a training-only filter]
After running \texttt{scripts/batch4\_workflow.py}, use WEKA 3.8.6 and its
runtime dependencies on the Java classpath. The following command, with
\texttt{weka-lib/*} pointing to those JARs, was tested with Java 21:
\begin{lstlisting}[basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize]
java -Djava.awt.headless=true -cp 'weka-lib/*' \
  weka.classifiers.meta.FilteredClassifier \
  -t output/batch4-demo/fold-1-train.arff \
  -T output/batch4-demo/fold-1-valid.arff -c last \
  -F weka.filters.unsupervised.attribute.Standardize \
  -W weka.classifiers.functions.Logistic -- -R 1.0E-8 -M 200
\end{lstlisting}
\texttt{-T} supplies the already separated validation file. Do not add
\texttt{-no-cv} to this command: this WEKA version rejects that combination.
The filter learns on the 177 training rows, not on all 360 TRAIN rows.
The 60 validation rows produce the confusion matrix
\[
\begin{array}{c|rr}
 &\hat y=0&\hat y=1\\\hline
y=0&35&9\\y=1&6&10
\end{array},\qquad \mathrm{accuracy}=45/60=.75.
\]
Repeat with fold-2 and fold-3 files for the same forward boundaries, preserving
all candidates' paired folds. WEKA's built-in randomized CV does not enforce
this grouped temporal protocol. This is a separate WEKA logistic baseline,
with its own regularization, preprocessing, and uncalibrated $.5$ decision.
It is not the frozen calibrated Python system: do not attach the Python
calibrator or threshold to a different classifier's scores. Any WEKA model
chosen for deployment needs its own calibration and policy stages.
\end{example}

\section{ML Pipelines in Python}
\label{sec:pipelines}
% =======================

\subsection{Introduction to Scikit-learn}

\begin{definition}[Scikit-learn]
Scikit-learn is Python's premier machine learning library, offering:
\begin{itemize}
\item Consistent API across algorithms
\item Comprehensive algorithm coverage
\item Excellent documentation
\item Integration with NumPy, Pandas, Matplotlib
\item Production-ready code
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Core API}: All estimators follow the same pattern:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
model = Estimator()          # Initialize
model.fit(X_train, y_train)  # Train
predictions = model.predict(X_test)  # Predict
score = model.score(X_test, y_test)  # Evaluate
\end{lstlisting}
\end{definition}

\subsection{The Pipeline Concept}

\begin{definition}[Pipeline]
A \textbf{Pipeline} chains multiple processing steps into a single estimator:
\[
\text{Data} \xrightarrow{\text{Transform 1}} \xrightarrow{\text{Transform 2}} \xrightarrow{\cdots} \xrightarrow{\text{Model}}
\]

\textbf{Benefits}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Convenience}: Single \texttt{fit()}/\texttt{predict()} call
\item \textbf{Enforces fitting boundaries}: When the whole Pipeline is fitted within valid splits, its transformers learn only from that training block; upstream feature/time/group leakage still needs separate checks
\item \textbf{Hyperparameter tuning}: Tune entire pipeline with GridSearchCV
\item \textbf{Reproducibility}: Easy to save and load entire workflow
\item \textbf{Code cleanliness}: Organized preprocessing + modeling
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    box/.style={rectangle, draw, minimum width=25mm, minimum height=10mm, align=center},
    data/.style={fill=blue!20},
    transform/.style={fill=orange!20},
    model/.style={fill=green!20}
]
\node[box, data] (data) at (0,0) {Raw\\Data};
\node[box, transform] (scale) at (3,0) {Scaler};
\node[box, transform] (pca) at (6,0) {PCA};
\node[box, model] (lr) at (9,0) {Logistic\\Regression};
\node[box, data] (pred) at (12,0) {Predictions};

\draw[->, thick] (data) -- (scale);
\draw[->, thick] (scale) -- (pca);
\draw[->, thick] (pca) -- (lr);
\draw[->, thick] (lr) -- (pred);

\draw[decorate, decoration={brace, amplitude=10pt, mirror}] 
    (2.5,-1) -- (9.5,-1) node[midway, below, yshift=-10pt] {Pipeline};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Pipeline chains preprocessing and modeling steps}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Building Pipelines}
The short snippets below are independent API illustrations: each assumes its
own previously split data with the displayed numeric or named-column schema.
They are not one script that changes the meaning of \texttt{X\_train} midway.
The generated-data implementation in Section~\ref{sec:device-workflow} is the
complete executable workflow. A short fragment's external data or metric
variables must be supplied consistently for that fragment.


\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Basic Pipeline Example}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.decomposition import PCA
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

# X_train/X_test: numeric matrices with at least two features;
# training sample size is at least two. Splits are already fixed.
# Create pipeline
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),      # Step 1: Scale
    ('pca', PCA(n_components=2)),     # Step 2: Reduce dim
    ('classifier', LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000)) # Step 3: Classify
])

# Use like any other estimator
pipeline.fit(X_train, y_train)
predictions = pipeline.predict(X_test)
accuracy = pipeline.score(X_test, y_test)

print(f"Accuracy: {accuracy:.3f}")
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Key Points}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Each step is a tuple: \texttt{('name', transformer)}
\item Intermediate steps must implement \texttt{fit()} and \texttt{transform()}; a separate \texttt{fit\_transform()} can be inherited or optionally implemented
\item Last step must implement \texttt{fit()}; prediction pipelines need \texttt{predict()}, while a transformation-only pipeline may end in a transformer
\item Access steps: \texttt{pipeline.named\_steps['scaler']}
\end{itemize}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Column Transformer}

\begin{definition}[ColumnTransformer]
Apply different transformations to different columns:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder

# Define transformations for different column types
preprocessor = ColumnTransformer([
    ('num', StandardScaler(), ['age', 'income']),
    ('cat', OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown='ignore'), ['gender', 'city'])
])

# Use in pipeline
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('preprocessor', preprocessor),
    ('classifier', RandomForestClassifier(random_state=42))
])
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Benefits}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Handle mixed data types (numerical + categorical)
\item Apply appropriate transformation to each feature type
\item Cleaner than manual column splitting
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Pipeline with GridSearchCV}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Hyperparameter Tuning with Pipeline}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.svm import SVC

# Create pipeline
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),
    ('classifier', SVC())
])

# Define parameter grid (use double underscore)
param_grid = [
    {'classifier__kernel': ['rbf'],
     'classifier__C': [0.1, 1, 10],
     'classifier__gamma': [0.001, 0.01, 0.1]},
    {'classifier__kernel': ['linear'],
     'classifier__C': [0.1, 1, 10]}
]

# Grid search on entire pipeline
grid_search = GridSearchCV(
    pipeline, 
    param_grid, 
    cv=5, 
    scoring='accuracy',
    n_jobs=1
)

grid_search.fit(X_train, y_train)

print(f"Best params: {grid_search.best_params_}")
print(f"Best CV score: {grid_search.best_score_:.3f}")

# Use best model
best_model = grid_search.best_estimator_
test_score = best_model.score(X_test, y_test)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Naming convention}: Use \texttt{step\_name\_\_parameter\_name} to reference pipeline parameters.
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Custom Transformers}

\begin{definition}[Custom Transformer]
Create your own transformer by inheriting from \texttt{BaseEstimator} and \texttt{TransformerMixin}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import numpy as np
from sklearn.base import BaseEstimator, TransformerMixin
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestRegressor

class LogTransformer(TransformerMixin, BaseEstimator):
    def __init__(self, features):
        self.features = features
    
    def fit(self, X, y=None):
        return self  # Nothing to learn
    
    def transform(self, X):
        X_copy = X.copy()
        values = X_copy.loc[:, self.features].to_numpy(dtype=float)
        if not np.isfinite(values).all() or np.any(values <= -1):
            raise ValueError('log1p requires finite values greater than -1')
        X_copy[self.features] = np.log1p(values)
        return X_copy

# Use in pipeline
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('log', LogTransformer(['income', 'age'])),
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),
    ('model', RandomForestRegressor(random_state=42))
])
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Requirements}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Implement \texttt{fit()} (even if it just returns \texttt{self})
\item Implement \texttt{transform()}
\item Optionally implement \texttt{fit\_transform()} (inherited from mixin)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Model Persistence}

\subsubsection{Saving and Loading Models}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Method 1: Joblib (Recommended)}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import joblib

# Save model
joblib.dump(pipeline, 'model_pipeline.joblib')

# Load model
loaded_pipeline = joblib.load('model_pipeline.joblib')

# Use loaded model
predictions = loaded_pipeline.predict(X_new)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Method 2: Pickle}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import pickle

# Save
with open('model.pkl', 'wb') as f:
    pickle.dump(pipeline, f)

# Load
with open('model.pkl', 'rb') as f:
    loaded_model = pickle.load(f)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Joblib vs Pickle}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Joblib}: Array-oriented persistence with optional compression; performance depends on data and settings
\item \textbf{Pickle}: Supports many Python objects; referenced definitions and compatible dependencies must be available when loading
\item Cross-version scikit-learn estimator loading is unsupported; recreate the recorded environment. Both formats can execute code when loading, so use only trusted artifacts
\end{itemize}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsubsection{Versioning and Reproducibility}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Best Practices for Model Persistence}:

\textbf{1. Save Metadata}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import json
import sklearn
from datetime import datetime

# Schema example: metrics refer to this same frozen selected procedure.
selected = grid_search.best_estimator_
metadata = {
    'model_type': type(selected.steps[-1][1]).__name__,
    'sklearn_version': sklearn.__version__,
    'training_date': datetime.now().isoformat(),
    'features': list(X_train.columns),
    'performance': {
        'train_accuracy': train_acc,
        'test_accuracy': test_acc,
        'cv_score': cv_score
    },
    'hyperparameters': grid_search.best_params_
}

with open('model_metadata.json', 'w') as f:
    json.dump(metadata, f, indent=2)
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{2. Version Control}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Use Git for code
\item Use DVC (Data Version Control) for models and data
\item Tag model versions: \texttt{v1.0.0}, \texttt{v1.1.0}
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Environment Management}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
# Save environment
pip freeze > requirements.txt

# Or use conda
conda env export > environment.yml
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{4. Directory Structure}:
\dirtree{%
.1 project/.
.2 models/.
.3 model\_v1.0.0.joblib.
.3 model\_v1.0.0\_metadata.json.
.2 data/.
.3 train.csv.
.3 test.csv.
.2 notebooks/.
.2 src/.
.3 requirements.txt.
}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Complete Pipeline Example}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{End-to-End Pipeline with All Features}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.compose import ColumnTransformer
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler, OneHotEncoder
from sklearn.impute import SimpleImputer
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.model_selection import cross_val_score
import pandas as pd
import joblib

# Define column types
numeric_features = ['age', 'income', 'credit_score']
categorical_features = ['gender', 'education', 'employment']

# Numeric pipeline
numeric_transformer = Pipeline([
    ('imputer', SimpleImputer(strategy='median')),
    ('scaler', StandardScaler())
])

# Categorical pipeline
categorical_transformer = Pipeline([
    ('imputer', SimpleImputer(strategy='most_frequent')),
    ('onehot', OneHotEncoder(handle_unknown='ignore'))
])

# Combine with ColumnTransformer
preprocessor = ColumnTransformer([
    ('num', numeric_transformer, numeric_features),
    ('cat', categorical_transformer, categorical_features)
])

# Full pipeline
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('preprocessor', preprocessor),
    ('classifier', RandomForestClassifier(random_state=42))
])

# Cross-validation
cv_scores = cross_val_score(
    pipeline, X_train, y_train, 
    cv=5, scoring='accuracy'
)
print(f"CV Accuracy: {cv_scores.mean():.3f} (fold SD {cv_scores.std():.3f})")

# Train final model
pipeline.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Evaluate
test_score = pipeline.score(X_test, y_test)
print(f"Test Accuracy: {test_score:.3f}")

# Save
joblib.dump(pipeline, 'credit_model_pipeline.joblib')

# Later: Load and predict
loaded_pipeline = joblib.load('credit_model_pipeline.joblib')
new_predictions = loaded_pipeline.predict(X_new)
\end{lstlisting}
\end{implementationbox}

% =======================
\subsection{One Complete, Executed Device-Alert Workflow}
\label{sec:device-workflow}
The preceding credit example illustrates a conventional pipeline. This section
provides a complete additional implementation with generated data, declared
partitions, calibration, a decision rule, persistence, serving, and export.
All reported values below come from the synthetic run with seed 20261119;
they are not a claim about a real device population.

\begin{example}[Training through the final frozen report]
Use Python 3.12 with the direct dependency pins in
\texttt{scripts/requirements-batch4.txt}. For example, create a virtual
environment and install those packages before running from the repository root:
\begin{lstlisting}[basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize]
python3.12 -m venv .venv
.venv/bin/python -m pip install -r scripts/requirements-batch4.txt
\end{lstlisting}
Activate that environment, then run:
\begin{lstlisting}[basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize]
python scripts/batch4_workflow.py --output output/batch4-demo
python scripts/batch4_export.py
uvicorn --app-dir scripts batch4_serving:app --host 127.0.0.1 --port 8000
\end{lstlisting}
The last line starts a local demonstration service; validation uses an in-process
client, so no network listener is necessary. The first script generates 780
rows, applies Section~\ref{sec:four-block-protocol}, and saves the complete
bundle. Its actual selection sequence is:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from batch4_workflow import (
    make_data, partition, fit_before_test, evaluate_frozen)

data = make_data()  # deterministic synthetic records
parts = partition(data)
bundle, selection = fit_before_test(data)
# fit_before_test uses TRAIN, CALIBRATION and POLICY labels only.
metrics, probability, prediction = evaluate_frozen(bundle, parts['test'])
\end{lstlisting}
Here the module lives in \texttt{scripts}; use \texttt{PYTHONPATH=scripts}
when executing this excerpt from the root. The complete script, rather than
this excerpt, writes all artifacts and audit tables.

Numeric preprocessing is median imputation followed by standardization;
nominal preprocessing is one-hot encoding with unknown categories ignored.
Every search clone fits these statistics inside its current training fold.
Six logistic settings select $C=10$ with no class weighting using mean
validation NLL $.521519$. Logistic regularization follows the estimator's
own $C$ convention; $C$ is not silently equated with an earlier mean-loss
$\lambda$. If transferring a regularization setting between sum and mean
objectives, convert the loss/penalty scaling explicitly.

The base pipeline is refitted on TRAIN only. For base probability $p_0$ define
$s=\log\{\tilde p_0/(1-\tilde p_0)\}$ with
$\tilde p_0=\mathrm{clip}(p_0,10^{-7},1-10^{-7})$.
A one-feature logistic calibrator fitted on 120 CALIBRATION rows gives
\[
q=\sigma(.7864064865\,s+.1497350775).
\]
On 120 POLICY rows, search the predeclared grid $.1,.2,\ldots,.9$ for mean
cost $(FP+4FN)/120$, breaking ties toward the higher threshold.
Threshold $.2$ wins with FP 54, FN 1, cost $58/120=.483333$.
Thresholds $.1$ and $.3$ give costs $65/120$ and $60/120$.
No model, calibrator, or threshold is fitted after this selection.

The frozen TEST result on 180 rows from 60 devices is:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lr@{\qquad}lr}\toprule
TN / FP &22 / 93& FN / TP&2 / 63\\
Accuracy &$.472222$ & ROC-AUC &$.698997$\\
Average precision &$.536225$ & Brier &$.213298$\\
Mean NLL &$.611886$ & Mean cost &$.561111$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
The low accuracy is retained in the report. With these declared costs the
fixed always-negative and always-positive baselines cost $260/180=1.444444$
and $115/180=.638889$. The chosen decision prioritizes recall at a substantial
false-positive burden; these synthetic results do not establish deployment
readiness. No threshold is changed in response to TEST.

A 500-draw percentile bootstrap samples whole test devices with all three
visits retained. Conditional on this frozen predictor, the AUC interval is
$[.618689,.785074]$ and the mean-cost interval is $[.477778,.661111]$.
All draws contain both classes. This excludes training/search uncertainty
and does not resolve future population shift.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/workflow-selection-test.pdf}
\caption{Different information roles: POLICY chooses the threshold (left);
TEST reports the frozen probability model (right). Test reliability points
show row counts in five fixed bins, not independent device counts.}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Artifacts and Reproducibility}
\texttt{output/batch4-demo} contains the base pipeline, calibrator, class order,
clipping constant, threshold, and schema version in \texttt{device\_alert.joblib};
metadata records source/data hashes and dependency versions. Complete split CSVs,
ARFF files, forward-fold exports, test predictions, and a JSON report accompany
it. Test rows do not enter the model identifier or decision-data hash. Changing
only test labels must not change a fitted parameter, threshold, or model ID.
A saved file hash detects accidental file changes relative to a trusted manifest;
it does not authenticate an artifact obtained from an untrusted source.
Both pickle and joblib can execute code while loading: load only trusted,
operator-selected artifacts. Record and recreate the training environment;
cross-version estimator loading is not supported merely because deserialization
happens to succeed. See the official
\href{https://scikit-learn.org/stable/model_persistence.html}{persistence guide}.

\subsubsection{An Input Contract Shared by Offline and Online Prediction}
\label{sec:device-contract}
The complete service is \texttt{scripts/batch4\_serving.py}. Its contract is:
\begin{itemize}
\item Exactly \texttt{signal\_a}, \texttt{signal\_b}, and \texttt{device\_type}
per row. Both numeric keys are required but may be null; non-null values must
be finite numbers. Strings masquerading as numbers, booleans, infinities,
extra keys, missing keys, and blank categories are rejected.
\item Unknown nonempty category strings are permitted by the trained all-zero
one-hot fallback. This differs from silently inventing a new fitted category.
\item A request has 1--256 rows. Responses preserve row order and identify model
version, schema version, threshold, positive label 1, probabilities, and decisions.
No request can cause a fitting operation or choose an artifact path.
\end{itemize}
A valid request includes a null and an unseen category:
\begin{lstlisting}
{"rows":[{"signal_a":0.2,"signal_b":null,"device_type":"gamma"}]}
\end{lstlisting}
The app factory verifies the artifact's feature list, class ordering, and
estimator runtime version before exposing readiness. Separate liveness and
readiness endpoints distinguish a running process from a loaded model.
The provided service logs model ID, batch size, and latency rather than raw
feature values. Before a broader deployment, specify authentication, resource
limits, concurrency, and service-level requirements for that environment.
The earlier Flask/FastAPI excerpts illustrate smaller credit-model interfaces;
they are separate schemas, not interchangeable endpoints for this artifact.

\subsubsection{Export Is a Whole-System Parity Question}
\begin{example}[ONNX graph plus explicit postprocessing]
\texttt{scripts/batch4\_export.py} converts the full \emph{base} pipeline,
including imputation, scaling, category encoding, and logistic head, at opset 21.
Numeric inputs are float32 tensors of shape $(B,1)$; the category is a string
tensor of that shape. The probability output's column 1 corresponds to class 1.
The graph's raw label is not the calibrated system's decision.

\texttt{onnx\_postprocess.json} stores clipping, calibration coefficients, class
index, threshold, and model ID. The adapter applies the same clipped-logit
calibration formula and $q\ge .2$ rule as Python. On all 180 fixed test rows
plus three null/unseen-category/saturated-score cases, maximum calibrated
probability difference is $4.08\times10^{-7}$ (rounded upward); all decisions
match, using absolute and relative tolerances $2\times10^{-6}$.
This finite parity check is not a proof for all possible inputs. A different
converter, dtype, or quantized graph needs its own parity assessment, especially
near the threshold. Exporting a classifier alone would omit the policy.
\end{example}

A local ASGI container recipe can copy the frozen artifact and use an explicit
startup command:
\begin{lstlisting}[basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize]
FROM python:3.12-slim
WORKDIR /app
COPY output/batch4-demo/requirements-runtime.txt requirements.txt
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
COPY scripts/batch4_workflow.py scripts/batch4_serving.py /app/scripts/
COPY output/batch4-demo/device_alert.joblib /app/model.joblib
ENV MODEL_PATH=/app/model.joblib
EXPOSE 8000
CMD ["uvicorn", "--app-dir", "scripts", "batch4_serving:app", "--host", "0.0.0.0", "--port", "8000"]
\end{lstlisting}
The generated requirements pin direct runtime packages; a deployment release
should additionally lock transitive dependencies and the base image digest.
This recipe has been reviewed but not built here because Docker is unavailable.
The TensorFlow export excerpt is an optional framework-specific example and
was not executed in this environment. The executed export evidence is ONNX.

\section{Model Deployment}
\label{sec:deployment}
% =======================

\subsection{Deployment Considerations}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Key Questions Before Deployment}:

\textbf{1. Latency Requirements}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Real-time ($<$ 100ms): Lightweight models, caching
\item Near real-time (seconds): Benchmark the complete preprocessing and inference path under expected load
\item Batch processing (hours): Choose a model whose memory and compute fit the processing window
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Scale}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Queries per second (QPS)?
\item Peak vs average load?
\item Need for horizontal scaling?
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Infrastructure}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Cloud (AWS, GCP, Azure) vs on-premise?
\item Serverless (Lambda) vs containers (Docker) vs VMs?
\item GPU required?
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Monitoring}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Performance metrics (latency, throughput)
\item Model metrics (accuracy, drift detection)
\item System metrics (CPU, memory, errors)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{5. Updates}:
\begin{itemize}
\item How often to retrain?
\item Blue-green deployment vs canary?
\item Rollback strategy?
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Model Export Formats}

\subsubsection{ONNX (Open Neural Network Exchange)}

\begin{definition}[ONNX]
ONNX is an open format for representing ML models, enabling interoperability across frameworks.

\textbf{Benefits}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Train in one framework (PyTorch, scikit-learn), deploy in another
\item Optimized inference engines (ONNX Runtime)
\item Hardware acceleration support
\item Language-agnostic (use from Python, C++, Java, etc.)
\end{itemize}

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
# This separate excerpt assumes a supported, already fitted
# all-numeric sklearn_model and matching numeric X_test.
import numpy as np
# Convert scikit-learn to ONNX
from skl2onnx import convert_sklearn
from skl2onnx.common.data_types import FloatTensorType

# Define input type
initial_type = [('float_input', FloatTensorType([None, n_features]))]

# Convert
onnx_model = convert_sklearn(
    sklearn_model, 
    initial_types=initial_type, target_opset=21
)

# Save
with open("model.onnx", "wb") as f:
    f.write(onnx_model.SerializeToString())

# Load and inference with ONNX Runtime
import onnxruntime as rt

sess = rt.InferenceSession("model.onnx")
input_name = sess.get_inputs()[0].name
pred = sess.run(None, {input_name: X_test.astype(np.float32)})
\end{lstlisting}
The returned list contains the graph's named outputs; inspect their names,
shapes, and class ordering rather than assuming every entry is a label array.
For the mixed-column pipeline and calibrated decision rule, use the executed
adapter in Section~\ref{sec:device-workflow} and its explicit postprocessing.

\end{definition}

\subsubsection{TensorFlow SavedModel}

\begin{definition}[TensorFlow SavedModel]
TensorFlow SavedModel is an inference/export format. In Keras 3, use the native \texttt{.keras} format with \texttt{save()} and \texttt{load\_model()}. Use \texttt{model.export()} for SavedModel. A SavedModel is loaded using TensorFlow APIs or wrapped with \texttt{TFSMLayer}, not passed to Keras 3 \texttt{load\_model()}. See the \href{https://keras.io/guides/migrating_to_keras_3/}{Keras 3 migration guide}.

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import tensorflow as tf

# Keras 3: save a reloadable Keras model
model.save('model.keras')
loaded_model = tf.keras.models.load_model('model.keras')
predictions = loaded_model.predict(X_test)

# Export an inference artifact in TensorFlow SavedModel format
model.export('saved_model/my_model', format='tf_saved_model')
serving_model = tf.saved_model.load('saved_model/my_model')
served_predictions = serving_model.serve(tf.convert_to_tensor(X_test))
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Benefits}:
\begin{itemize}
\item SavedModel preserves exported computation and weights for inference; the native Keras format also preserves Keras configuration and optimizer state when saved
\item TensorFlow Serving integration
\item TensorFlow Lite for mobile/edge
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Format Comparison}

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{@{}llll@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Format} & \textbf{Framework} & \textbf{Use Case} & \textbf{Pros} \\
\midrule
Pickle/Joblib & Any Python & Python-only & Simple, native \\
ONNX & Cross-framework & Production & Optimized, portable \\
SavedModel & TensorFlow & TF ecosystem & Complete, TF Serving \\
PMML & Traditional ML & Enterprise & Standardized, legacy \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Model export format comparison}
\end{table}

\subsection{Serving Models via REST API}

\subsubsection{Flask API}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Simple Flask API for Model Serving}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from flask import Flask, request, jsonify
import joblib
import os
import pandas as pd

app = Flask(__name__)
# Same artifact and named-column contract as the complete pipeline example.
model = joblib.load(os.environ.get(
    'MODEL_PATH', 'credit_model_pipeline.joblib'))

def prepare_rows(rows):
    if not isinstance(rows, list) or not rows:
        raise ValueError('rows must be a nonempty list of objects')
    expected = list(model.feature_names_in_)
    if any(not isinstance(row, dict) or set(row) != set(expected)
           for row in rows):
        raise ValueError(f'each row must have exactly these keys: {expected}')
    return pd.DataFrame(rows).loc[:, expected]

@app.route('/predict', methods=['POST'])
def predict():
    data = request.get_json()
    if not isinstance(data, dict):
        return jsonify({'error': 'expected a JSON object'}), 400
    try:
        features = prepare_rows(data.get('rows'))
        return jsonify({
            'predictions': model.predict(features).tolist(),
            'probabilities': model.predict_proba(features).tolist(),
            'classes': model.classes_.tolist()
        })
    except (ValueError, TypeError) as exc:
        return jsonify({'error': str(exc)}), 400

@app.route('/health', methods=['GET'])
def health():
    return jsonify({'status': 'healthy'})

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(host='0.0.0.0', port=5000)  # Local development server
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Test with curl}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
curl -X POST http://localhost:5000/predict \
  -H 'Content-Type: application/json' \
  -d '{"rows": [{"age": 25, "income": 50000, "credit_score": 700,
                "gender": "female", "education": "college",
                "employment": "employed"}]}'
\end{lstlisting}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsubsection{FastAPI (Modern Alternative)}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{FastAPI for High-Performance Serving}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
from fastapi import FastAPI, HTTPException
from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
import joblib
import os
import pandas as pd

class PredictionRequest(BaseModel):
    rows: list[dict[str, float | str | None]] = Field(min_length=1)

app = FastAPI(title='ML Model API')
model = joblib.load(os.environ.get(
    'MODEL_PATH', 'credit_model_pipeline.joblib'))

@app.post('/predict')
def predict(request: PredictionRequest):
    # A regular def lets FastAPI run blocking model work in a thread pool.
    expected = list(model.feature_names_in_)
    if any(set(row) != set(expected) for row in request.rows):
        raise HTTPException(400, f'each row must have these keys: {expected}')
    try:
        features = pd.DataFrame(request.rows).loc[:, expected]
        return {
            'predictions': model.predict(features).tolist(),
            'probabilities': model.predict_proba(features).tolist(),
            'classes': model.classes_.tolist()
        }
    except (ValueError, TypeError) as exc:
        raise HTTPException(400, detail=str(exc)) from exc

@app.get('/health')
def health():
    return {'status': 'healthy'}

# Development: uvicorn app:app --reload
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{FastAPI Advantages}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Automatic API documentation (Swagger UI at \texttt{/docs})
\item Type validation with Pydantic
\item Supports asynchronous I/O; synchronous model computation must not block an async event loop
\item Throughput depends on the server, workers and model cost; framework choice alone does not determine it
\end{itemize}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Containerization with Docker}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Dockerfile for ML API}:

\begin{lstlisting}
FROM python:3.12-slim

WORKDIR /app

# Copy requirements and install
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt

# Copy model and code
COPY credit_model_pipeline.joblib .
COPY app.py .

# Expose port
EXPOSE 5000

# Run app
CMD ["python", "app.py"]
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Build and Run}:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash]
# Build image
docker build -t ml-model-api .

# Run container
docker run -p 5000:5000 ml-model-api

# Test
curl http://localhost:5000/health
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Docker Compose for the Flask Example}: Place the saved artifact in \texttt{./models/}. Redis is an optional additional service; the API below does not use it until caching code is added.
\begin{lstlisting}
services:
  api:
    build: .
    ports:
      - "5000:5000"
    environment:
      - MODEL_PATH=/models/credit_model_pipeline.joblib
    volumes:
      - ./models:/models
  
  redis:
    image: redis:alpine
    ports:
      - "6379:6379"
\end{lstlisting}
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Cloud Deployment}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Cloud Deployment Options}:

\textbf{1. Serverless (AWS Lambda, Google Cloud Functions)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pros}: No server management, auto-scaling, pay per use
\item \textbf{Cons}: Cold starts, limited execution time, limited memory
\item \textbf{Best for}: Low to medium traffic, event-driven, cost-sensitive
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Containers (AWS ECS, GKE, Azure Container Instances)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pros}: Consistent environments, easy scaling, orchestration
\item \textbf{Cons}: More complex setup, need to manage infrastructure
\item \textbf{Best for}: Medium to high traffic, microservices
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Managed ML Services}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Managed training/serving platforms; verify the provider's current interfaces, supported runtimes, and service configuration
\item \textbf{Pros}: Built-in monitoring, auto-scaling, A/B testing, versioning
\item \textbf{Cons}: Vendor lock-in, can be expensive
\item \textbf{Best for}: Enterprise ML, need full MLOps
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Kubernetes}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pros}: Highly scalable, flexible, cloud-agnostic
\item \textbf{Cons}: Steep learning curve, operational overhead
\item \textbf{Best for}: Large scale, multi-model serving, complex workflows
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Model Monitoring}

\begin{definition}[Model Monitoring]
Continuous tracking of model performance and data quality in production.

\textbf{Key Metrics to Monitor}:

\textbf{1. Performance Metrics}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Prediction accuracy/error
\item Latency (p50, p95, p99)
\item Throughput (requests per second)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Data Quality}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Missing values
\item Out-of-range values
\item Data type mismatches
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Data Drift}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Feature distribution changes
\item Target distribution changes
\item KL divergence, KS test
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Concept Drift}:
\begin{itemize}
\item $P(y|X)$ changes over time
\item Predictive performance may change; outcome measurements are needed to assess it
\item Investigate the cause and validate an appropriate response before retraining or changing the policy
\end{itemize}

\textbf{5. System Metrics}:
\begin{itemize}
\item CPU/memory usage
\item Error rates
\item Uptime
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Simple Logging for Monitoring}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import logging
import time
from datetime import datetime, timezone

logging.basicConfig(level=logging.INFO)
logger = logging.getLogger(__name__)

# Replace the Flask /predict handler above; do not register both handlers.
# Reuse app, model, prepare_rows, request and jsonify from that example.
@app.route('/predict', methods=['POST'])
def predict():
    start = time.perf_counter()
    data = request.get_json()
    if not isinstance(data, dict):
        return jsonify({'error': 'expected a JSON object'}), 400
    try:
        features = prepare_rows(data.get('rows'))
        predictions = model.predict(features).tolist()
        probabilities = model.predict_proba(features).tolist()
        logger.info({
            'timestamp': datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(),
            'batch_size': len(features),
            'latency_ms': (time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000
        })
        return jsonify({'predictions': predictions,
                        'probabilities': probabilities,
                        'classes': model.classes_.tolist()})
    except (ValueError, TypeError) as exc:
        return jsonify({'error': str(exc)}), 400
\end{lstlisting}

\textbf{Advanced}: Use dedicated tools like Prometheus, Grafana, ELK stack, or cloud-native solutions (CloudWatch, Stackdriver).
\end{implementationbox}

\subsection{Best Practices Summary}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Deployment Best Practices]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Versioning}: Tag models, save metadata, track lineage

\item \textbf{Testing}: Unit tests, integration tests, load tests before production

\item \textbf{Monitoring}: Log predictions, track performance, detect drift

\item \textbf{Gradual Rollout}: Canary deployment (5\% $\to$ 50\% $\to$ 100\%)

\item \textbf{Rollback Plan}: Keep previous model version, quick rollback capability

\item \textbf{Security}:
\begin{itemize}
\item API authentication (API keys, OAuth)
\item Rate limiting
\item Input validation
\item HTTPS only
\end{itemize}

\item \textbf{Documentation}: API docs, model cards, usage examples

\item \textbf{Automation}: CI/CD pipeline for model updates

\item \textbf{Cost Optimization}: Right-size resources, use caching, batch predictions when possible

\item \textbf{Feedback Loop}: Collect ground truth labels, retrain periodically
\end{enumerate}
\end{tcolorbox}

\subsection{Monitoring, Delayed Labels, and a Reversible Release}
\label{sec:release-monitoring}
\begin{example}[An input alarm is not a performance measurement]
For fixed categorical bins, reference frequencies are $p=(.5,.5)$ and current
frequencies $q=(.8,.2)$. Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
D_{KL}(q\Vert p)&=.8\log1.6+.2\log.4=.192745,\\
\mathrm{PSI}&=\sum_j(q_j-p_j)\log(q_j/p_j)=.3\log4=.415888.
\end{aligned}
\]
The direction of KL is declared. With empty bins, define a fixed smoothing
rule and normalize before computing these quantities; a plug-in zero is not
an innocuous finite value. Thresholds for alerts need baseline variability,
sample sizes, and investigation procedures, not universal PSI cutoffs.

Input drift concerns $P(X)$. Performance can remain unchanged after it; conversely,
changing $Y=X$ to $Y=1-X$ for a fair binary $X$ leaves $P(X)$ unchanged while
reversing a formerly perfect classifier. Thus input tests alone cannot detect
all changes in $P(Y\mid X)$. Delayed outcome metrics join each prediction's
ID, model ID, prediction time, threshold, and feature snapshot to a label
\emph{available} by the monitoring cutoff. Report unresolved-label counts;
if resolved labels are selective, their metric is for the observed subset.
Never treat missing labels as negatives or overwrite old predictions with a
new model before computing historical performance.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.84\linewidth]{figures/batch4/drift-counts.pdf}
\caption{A specified categorical frequency change. No labels appear in this
calculation, so it cannot establish a change in predictive performance.}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Stable assignment and a prespecified canary decision]
Assign traffic consistently at the independent entity level using
\texttt{SHA256(salt:entity)} mapped to buckets 0--9999. Buckets below 1000
form an approximately 10\% canary; finite realized fractions need not be
exactly 10\%. Python's process-randomized \texttt{hash()} is unsuitable for
persistent assignment. Version the salt and retain assignment IDs. Repeated
visits by one device stay in one arm.

For a separate illustrative experiment with 1000 independent entities in each
arm, old and new error counts 120 and 90 give difference
$\hat p_{new}-\hat p_{old}=-.03$, standard error
$\sqrt{.12\cdot.88/1000+.09\cdot.91/1000}=.013693$, and approximate
95\% interval $[-.05684,-.00316]$. These are invented release numbers,
not measurements of the device example. Predeclare the horizon, minimum
sample size, primary metric, and latency/error/fairness guardrails. Repeated
optional peeking invalidates a fixed-horizon interval; dependent observations
need an appropriate cluster or sequential analysis.

A release version contains the preprocessing, base predictor, calibrator,
threshold, input schema, and runtime together. Rollback restores this entire
version. An input alarm initiates investigation; retraining uses a newly
versioned training window and a fresh evaluation boundary. Repeatedly choosing
new versions on the old final test turns that test into validation data.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,b/.style={draw,text width=2.6cm,minimum height=1.1cm},>=Latex]
\node[b,fill=blue!10] (a) at (0,0) {Freeze artifacts\\and contract};
\node[b,fill=green!10] (b) at (3.7,0) {Offline parity\\and readiness};
\node[b,fill=orange!10] (c) at (7.4,0) {Canary with\\stable assignment};
\node[b] (d) at (7.4,-1.9) {Delayed labels\\and guardrails};
\node[b,fill=green!10] (e) at (3.7,-1.9) {Expand release\\or restore old version};
\draw[->,thick] (a)--(b);\draw[->,thick] (b)--(c);
\draw[->,thick] (c)--(d);\draw[->,thick] (d)--(e);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{A complete release loop. Monitoring is tied to the version that
actually generated each prediction; a failed gate restores a complete version.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Summary of Part 7}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=yellow!10!white,colframe=orange!75!black,title=Key Takeaways]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{WEKA}: Offers a GUI, command-line tools and Java APIs. Useful for learning and baseline comparisons; put learned filters inside cross-validation.

\item \textbf{Scikit-learn Pipelines}: Chain preprocessing and modeling. Fit the complete pipeline inside correctly constructed folds to enforce preprocessing boundaries; audit upstream feature availability separately. Use ColumnTransformer for mixed data types.

\item \textbf{Model Persistence}: Use joblib for scikit-learn models. Save metadata (version, hyperparameters, performance). Version control models.

\item \textbf{Export Formats}: ONNX for cross-framework compatibility, SavedModel for TensorFlow ecosystem, pickle/joblib for Python-only.

\item \textbf{API Serving}: Flask for simple APIs, FastAPI for high performance. Include health checks and error handling.

\item \textbf{Containerization}: Docker ensures consistent environments. Docker Compose for multi-service setups.

\item \textbf{Cloud Deployment}: Choose based on scale (serverless $\to$ containers $\to$ Kubernetes). Managed services for full MLOps.

\item \textbf{Monitoring}: Track performance, data drift, system metrics. Log predictions for debugging and retraining.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Next Steps}: Part 8 covers advanced topics: reinforcement learning, representation learning, ethics in ML, and modern trends like foundation models and AutoML.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Design a WEKA workflow to compare Decision Tree (J48), Random Forest, and SVM on the Iris dataset. What test method would you use and why?

\item Create a scikit-learn pipeline that handles both numeric (scaling) and categorical (one-hot encoding) features, then trains a Random Forest. Include proper error handling.

\item Explain why we must fit transformers only on training data. Give an example of what goes wrong if we fit on test data.

\item Design a Flask API that serves a sentiment analysis model. Include endpoints for single prediction and batch prediction. Add appropriate error handling.

\item Write a Dockerfile for deploying a scikit-learn model as a Flask API. What base image would you use? Why?

\item Compare serverless (AWS Lambda) vs container (Docker on ECS) deployment for: (a) Low-traffic app (100 requests/day), (b) High-traffic app (10,000 requests/second).

\item Design a monitoring system for a production model. What metrics would you track? How would you detect when the model needs retraining?

\item Implement a simple A/B test framework where 10\% of traffic goes to a new model version, 90\% to the old version. How would you decide when to fully switch?
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}

% =======================
% Part 8: Advanced Topics and Modern Directions
% =======================
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Part 8: Advanced Topics and Modern Directions}
\section*{Part 8: Advanced Topics and Modern Directions}
The advanced objectives below reuse the notation and loss conventions of
Section~\ref{sec:notation-loss}. They change the learning problem, not the
need for a valid evaluation boundary: policy training uses fresh evaluation
episodes, representation fitting respects source-level splits, and federated
or retrieved data need their own client/time/availability rules. Part 5 holds
the core autoencoder derivation; this part develops its uses and extensions.


\section{Reinforcement Learning}
\label{sec:reinforcement-learning}

\subsection{Introduction to Reinforcement Learning}

\begin{definition}[Reinforcement Learning]
\textbf{Reinforcement Learning (RL)} is learning by interacting with an environment to maximize cumulative reward.

\textbf{Key difference from supervised learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item No labeled examples---agent must discover good actions through trial and error
\item Delayed rewards---actions may have long-term consequences
\item Sequential decision-making---current action affects future states
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Core Components}

\begin{definition}[RL Framework]
An RL system consists of:

\textbf{1. Agent}: The learner/decision maker
