\textbf{2. Environment}: Everything the agent interacts with

\textbf{3. State} ($s_t$): Current situation of the agent

\textbf{4. Action} ($a_t$): Choice made by the agent

\textbf{5. Reward} ($r_t$): Immediate feedback from environment

\textbf{6. Policy} ($\pi$): Agent's strategy for selecting actions
\[
\pi(a|s) = P(\text{action } a | \text{state } s)
\]

\textbf{7. Value Function} ($V^\pi(s)$): Expected cumulative reward from state $s$ under policy $\pi$
\[
V^\pi(s) = \E_\pi\left[\sum_{t=0}^\infty \gamma^t r_t \mid s_0 = s\right]
\]
For bounded rewards in a continuing task, take $0\leq\gamma<1$ so this sum is finite. The episodic case can use $\gamma=1$ when the return has finite expectation.

\textbf{8. Q-Function} ($Q^\pi(s,a)$): Expected cumulative reward from taking action $a$ in state $s$
\[
Q^\pi(s,a) = \E_\pi\left[\sum_{t=0}^\infty \gamma^t r_t \mid s_0 = s, a_0 = a\right]
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    box/.style={rectangle, draw, minimum width=20mm, minimum height=12mm, align=center},
    agent/.style={fill=blue!20},
    env/.style={fill=green!20}
]
\node[box, agent] (agent) at (0,0) {Agent};
\node[box, env] (env) at (5,0) {Environment};

\draw[->, thick] (agent.north) -- ++(0,1) -| (env.north);
\node at (2.5,1.4) {Action $a_t$};
\draw[->, thick] (env.south) -- ++(0,-1) -| (agent.south);
\node at (2.5,-1.4) {State $s_{t+1}$, reward $r_t$};
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Agent-environment interaction: the agent sends an action; the environment returns the next state and reward on a separate path.}
\end{figure}

\begin{intuition}
Think of learning to play a video game:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{State}: Current game screen
\item \textbf{Action}: Button presses (up, down, left, right, jump)
\item \textbf{Reward}: Points gained, lives lost
\item \textbf{Policy}: Strategy for playing (e.g., ``when enemy approaches, jump'')
\item \textbf{Goal}: Maximize total score (cumulative reward)
\end{itemize}

The agent explores different actions, observes rewards, and learns which actions lead to high scores.
\end{intuition}

\subsection{The Exploration-Exploitation Dilemma}

\begin{definition}[Exploration vs Exploitation]
\textbf{Exploitation}: Choose the action with the largest estimated long-term return, not necessarily the largest immediate reward

\textbf{Exploration}: Try new actions to discover potentially better options

\textbf{Dilemma}: How to balance?
\begin{itemize}
\item Pure exploitation: May miss better strategies
\item Pure exploration: Wastes time on known bad actions
\end{itemize}

\textbf{$\epsilon$-Greedy Strategy}:
\[
a_t = \begin{cases}
\argmax_a Q(s_t, a) & \text{with probability } 1-\epsilon \text{ (exploit)} \\
\text{random action} & \text{with probability } \epsilon \text{ (explore)}
\end{cases}
\]
Common: Start with high $\epsilon$ (e.g., 1.0), decay over time to 0.01.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Q-Learning}

\begin{definition}[Q-Learning]
Model-free algorithm to learn optimal Q-function (and thus optimal policy).

\textbf{Bellman Optimality Equation}:
\[
Q^*(s,a) = \E_{s' \sim P(\cdot|s,a)}\left[r(s,a) + \gamma \max_{a'} Q^*(s', a')\right]
\]
The optimal Q-value equals expected immediate reward plus discounted max future Q-value. In deterministic environments, this simplifies to $Q^*(s,a) = r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q^*(s', a')$.

For a terminal next state, use a future-value term of zero.

\textbf{Q-Learning Update Rule}:
\[
Q(s_t, a_t) \leftarrow Q(s_t, a_t) + \alpha\left[r_t + \gamma \max_{a} Q(s_{t+1}, a) - Q(s_t, a_t)\right]
\]
where $\alpha$ is the learning rate. Set $Q(s_{\rm terminal},a)=0$ for every action; the terminal state is not used for another action selection.

\textbf{Intuition}: Adjust Q-value toward a sampled, bootstrapped TD target; the TD error is target minus current estimate.
\end{definition}

\begin{algorithmbox}
\textbf{Q-Learning Algorithm}

\textbf{Initialize}: Q-table $Q(s,a) = 0$ for all states $s$ and actions $a$

\textbf{For} each episode:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Initialize state $s$
\item \textbf{While} not terminal:
\begin{itemize}
\item Choose action $a$ using $\epsilon$-greedy policy based on $Q(s, \cdot)$
\item Take action $a$, observe reward $r$ and next state $s'$
\item Update: 
\[
Q(s,a) \leftarrow Q(s,a) + \alpha\left[r + \gamma \max_{a'} Q(s',a') - Q(s,a)\right]
\]
\item $s \leftarrow s'$
\end{itemize}
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Output}: Learned Q-table and its greedy policy. Convergence to $Q^*$ requires assumptions including sufficient exploration and suitable diminishing learning rates; a finite run is not guaranteed optimal.
\end{algorithmbox}

\begin{example}[GridWorld Navigation]
Agent in $4 \times 4$ grid, goal at top-right corner.

\textbf{States}: Grid positions
\textbf{Actions}: $\{\text{up, down, left, right}\}$
\textbf{Rewards}: $-1$ per step, $+10$ at goal, $-10$ for obstacles

With suitable exploration and learning rates, Q-learning can learn a policy maximizing the stated discounted rewards. A finite run need not find a shortest path, and shortest length is not equivalent to every reward objective.
\end{example}

\subsection{Deep Q-Networks (DQN)}

\begin{definition}[DQN]
Extend Q-learning to high-dimensional state spaces (e.g., images) using neural networks.

Instead of Q-table, use neural network $Q(s, a; \theta)$ parameterized by $\theta$.

\textbf{Key innovations}:

\textbf{1. Experience Replay}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Store transitions $(s_t,a_t,r_t,s_{t+1},d_t)$, where $d_t=1$ denotes an actual terminal state. A time-limit truncation is handled according to the task definition, not automatically as termination
\item Sample random mini-batches for training
\item Reduces the influence of consecutive-sample correlation; replay samples can remain dependent
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Target Network}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Use separate network $Q(s, a; \theta^-)$ for target computation
\item Update $\theta^-$ periodically (copy from $\theta$)
\item Stabilizes training (target doesn't move every update)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Loss Function}:
\[
\Loss(\theta)=\E_{(s,a,r,s',d)\sim\text{Buffer}}
\left[\left(r+\gamma(1-d)\max_{a'}Q(s',a';\theta^-)-Q(s,a;\theta)\right)^2\right]
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{DQN Breakthroughs}:

\textbf{Success}: DQN (2013-2015) achieved human-level performance on Atari games using only pixel input.

\textbf{Why important}:
\begin{itemize}
\item First deep RL success on complex tasks
\item Showed RL can work with raw sensory input
\item Sparked modern deep RL research
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Extensions}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Double DQN}: Addresses overestimation bias
\item \textbf{Dueling DQN}: Separate value and advantage streams
\item \textbf{Prioritized Experience Replay}: Sample important transitions more
\item \textbf{Rainbow}: Combines multiple improvements
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Sample inefficient (needs millions of frames)
\item Discrete actions only
\item Can be unstable
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Policy Gradient Methods}

\begin{definition}[Policy Gradient]
Instead of learning Q-function, directly learn policy $\pi(a|s; \theta)$.

\textbf{Objective}: Maximize expected cumulative reward
\[
J(\theta)=\E_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^t r_t\right]
\]

\textbf{Policy Gradient Theorem}:
\[
\nabla_\theta J(\theta)=\E_{\tau\sim\pi_\theta}\left[\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\gamma^t\nabla_\theta\log\pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)G_t\right]
\]
Here $G_t=\sum_{k=t}^{T-1}\gamma^{k-t}r_k$ is reward-to-go for a finite horizon $T$. Assume differentiability, parameter-independent environment dynamics, and conditions permitting differentiation under the expectation. The outer $\gamma^t$ is required for this start-state discounted objective; for $\gamma=1$ both expressions reduce to the undiscounted episodic case.

\textbf{Intuition}: Increase probability of actions that led to high returns.
\end{definition}

\textbf{Modern Algorithms}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{REINFORCE}: Basic policy gradient
\item \textbf{Actor-Critic}: Combines policy gradient with value function
\item \textbf{A3C}: Asynchronous actor-critic
\item \textbf{PPO (Proximal Policy Optimization)}: Uses a constrained or clipped surrogate update; stability and performance depend on implementation and tuning
\item \textbf{SAC (Soft Actor-Critic)}: Maximum entropy RL
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Bellman Equations, Targets, and a Complete Small MDP}
\label{sec:rl-worked}
For bounded rewards and $0\le\gamma<1$, define
$(TV)(s)=\max_a\E[r+\gamma V(s')\mid s,a]$, with terminal value zero.
The inequality $|\max_a u_a-\max_a v_a|\le\max_a|u_a-v_a|$ gives
$\|TV-TW\|_\infty\le\gamma\|V-W\|_\infty$.
The contraction has a unique fixed point $V^*$ and exact value iteration obeys
$\|V_k-V^*\|_\infty\le\gamma^k\|V_0-V^*\|_\infty$.
This statement concerns exact expectations on a fixed MDP, not automatic
convergence of an arbitrary neural approximation.

\begin{example}[Two states and an absorbing terminal state]
In state A, stop terminates with reward 1; go moves to B with reward 0.
In B, stop terminates with reward 2; loop stays in B with reward $.5$.
With $\gamma=.9$, looping forever in B has value $.5/(1-.9)=5>2$,
so $V^*(B)=5$, $V^*(A)=.9\cdot5=4.5$. Action values are
\[
\begin{array}{c|rr} &\text{stop}&\text{go/loop}\\\hline
 A&1&4.5\\B&2&5\end{array}.
\]
Starting from $V_0=(0,0)$, simultaneous updates give
$V_1=(1,2)$, $V_2=(1.8,2.3)$, $V_3=(2.07,2.57)$, converging to $(4.5,5)$.
The finite initial preference for stopping at B eventually changes as future
loop rewards propagate through the backups.

For sampled Q-learning, start every Q-value at zero and use $\alpha=.5$.
A sequence of observed transitions gives these updated entries:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lll}\toprule
Transition & TD target & Updated Q entry\\\midrule
B loop to B & $.5$ & $Q(B,loop)=.25$\\
B stop & $2$ & $Q(B,stop)=1$\\
A go to B & $.9\max(.25,1)=.9$ & $Q(A,go)=.45$\\
B loop to B & $.5+.9\max(.25,1)=1.4$ & $Q(B,loop)=.825$\\
A stop & $1$ & $Q(A,stop)=.5$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
These are bootstrap targets, not complete observed Monte Carlo returns.
This short trace is not a convergence proof. In the finite tabular discounted
setting, standard sufficient conditions include infinite visits to each
state--action pair and per-pair step sizes satisfying
$\sum_t\alpha_t=\infty$, $\sum_t\alpha_t^2<\infty$, with bounded noise.
A fixed $.5$ step size used for demonstration does not meet the second condition.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/bellman-contraction.pdf}
\caption{Exact value iteration for the stated MDP. The log-scale right panel
compares actual error to the contraction bound $5(.9)^k$.}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[A DQN batch with terminal masking and mean-loss scaling]
For two replay transitions, let rewards be $(2,.5)$, terminal indicators $(1,0)$,
and target-network next-state maxima $(100,3)$. With $\gamma=.9$ the detached
TD targets are $(2,3.2)$, not $(92,3.2)$. If current selected-action outputs
are $(1,4)$, the \emph{mean squared} loss is
\[
L=\tfrac12[(1-2)^2+(4-3.2)^2]=.82,\quad
\nabla_{(Q_1,Q_2)}L=(-1,.8).
\]
Here $1/2$ is $1/B$ for batch size two, not the extra half convention for
squared loss. In general $\partial L/\partial Q_i=2(Q_i-y_i)/B$.
Targets are held constant during the online update. A target-network copy and
random replay reduce some feedback and serial correlation; they do not make
samples independent or guarantee stable nonlinear learning.

For a separate Double-DQN next state, online action scores $(5,4)$ choose the
first action and target-network values $(1,3)$ evaluate it. At reward zero,
Double-DQN target is $.9\cdot1=.9$, versus ordinary target $.9\cdot3=2.7$.
Selection and evaluation are separated; this does not promise lower error for
every transition. A true termination masks bootstrap value. A time-limit
truncation may require bootstrapping from the final observation when the
underlying task continues; using the observation after an automatic reset is
incorrect. Follow the task semantics and
\href{https://gymnasium.farama.org/tutorials/gymnasium_basics/handling_time_limits/}{Gymnasium's time-limit guidance}.
\end{example}

\subsubsection{Deriving the Policy Gradient and Its Baseline}
Write a trajectory likelihood as its initial-state density times policy terms
and environment transition densities. If the environment does not depend on
$\theta$, then $\nabla\log P_\theta(\tau)=\sum_t\nabla\log\pi_\theta(a_t\mid s_t)$.
Differentiating the expected discounted return gives the score-function
estimator. Rewards before action $a_t$ have zero expected product with its
score conditional on the past; removing them yields the reward-to-go formula
above, including its outer $\gamma^t$. Similarly,
\[
\E_{a\sim\pi_\theta(\cdot\mid s)}
 [b(s)\nabla\log\pi_\theta(a\mid s)]
 =b(s)\nabla\sum_a\pi_\theta(a\mid s)=0.
\]
A state-dependent baseline therefore preserves the expected gradient.
Treat a fitted critic's advantage as constant in the actor loss; the critic
has a separate fitting objective. An arbitrary action-dependent baseline
does not satisfy this argument without an appropriate correction.

\begin{example}[A bandit gradient and variance reduction]
Choose action 1 with $p=\sigma(\theta)$; rewards are 2 for action 1 and 0
otherwise. Then $J=2p$ and $J'=2p(1-p)$. At $\theta=0$, $J'=1/2$.
The unbiased sample estimator $r(a)(a-p)$ equals 1 or 0 with equal probability,
so variance is $.25$. With baseline 1 it becomes $(r(a)-1)(a-p)=.5$ for
either action, giving zero variance in this special case. One exact gradient
ascent step with $\eta=.1$ gives $\theta=.05$ and $J=1.024995$.

To check discount placement, let two independent Bernoulli actions have this
same probability, rewards $r_0=a_0$, $r_1=2a_0+a_1$, and $\gamma=.7$.
Then $J=(1+3\gamma)p$, with derivative $.775$ at $\theta=0$.
The correct score terms have expectations $.6$ and $.175$; omitting the outer
$\gamma$ from the second gives $.6+.25=.85$, the wrong gradient.
For evaluation, freeze the policy and use fresh episodes, initial conditions,
and multiple seeds. Replay transitions used for training are not an independent
policy-evaluation set; multiple steps within one episode are not independent
experimental replicates.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/policy-gradient-baseline.pdf}
\caption{Exact expected reward and derivative for the bandit, plus two unbiased
estimators' variances at $\theta=0$. Zero baseline variance is specific to this example.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Applications of RL}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Game Playing}: AlphaGo, OpenAI Five (Dota 2), AlphaStar (StarCraft)
\item \textbf{Robotics}: Manipulation, locomotion, drone control
\item \textbf{Autonomous Vehicles}: Path planning, decision making
\item \textbf{Recommendation Systems}: Sequential recommendations
\item \textbf{Resource Management}: Data center cooling, traffic light control
\item \textbf{Finance}: Trading, portfolio management
\item \textbf{Healthcare}: Treatment optimization, drug dosing
\end{itemize}

% =======================
\section{Representation Learning}
\label{sec:representation-learning}
% =======================

\subsection{Introduction to Representation Learning}

\begin{definition}[Representation Learning]
Learning good representations (features) of data automatically, rather than hand-crafting them.

\textbf{Goal}: Discover abstractions that make it easier to extract useful information for downstream tasks.

\textbf{Good representations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Capture relevant factors of variation
\item Disentangle underlying causes
\item Robust to noise
\item Transfer across tasks
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsection{Using Autoencoders as Learned Representations}
\label{sec:ae-applications}
The encoder/decoder definition, architecture diagram, reconstruction objective,
linear-PCA equivalence, and VAE derivation are collected in
Sections~\ref{sec:autoencoder-basics}--\ref{sec:vae-bound}.
Here the question is how the learned code is used and constrained.
A denoising target remains the clean training input; generating new data
requires a specified generative model, such as the VAE, rather than only a
small deterministic bottleneck.

\subsubsection{Sparse and Contractive Extensions}
Let $z_i=f_{enc}(x_i)$ and $\hat x_i=f_{dec}(z_i)$. An activation-sparse
objective with the same example-mean convention is
\[
\Loss_{sparse}=\frac1n\sum_i\left[\|x_i-\hat x_i\|_2^2
 +\lambda\|z_i\|_1\right].
\]
This penalizes latent activations, unlike Lasso's penalty on model weights.
A contractive extension instead adds sensitivity of the encoder:
\[
\Loss_{contractive}=\frac1n\sum_i\left[\|x_i-\hat x_i\|_2^2
 +\lambda\|J_{enc}(x_i)\|_F^2\right],
\quad J_{enc}\in\R^{k\times d}.
\]
The Jacobian uses the output-by-input convention of Part 0. Both losses sum
coordinates and average observations. Decoder capacity and constraints matter:
for a freely rescalable linear encoder/decoder, shrinking the code and
inversely scaling the decoder can reduce an activation penalty while leaving
reconstruction unchanged. A sparsity term alone does not identify a unique
representation or its semantics.

\subsubsection{Downstream Use and Anomaly Decisions}
For a linear probe, freeze the fitted encoder and its inference state, fit
only the head with training labels, and select settings on validation data.
Fine-tuning is a different procedure that updates the encoder as well.
A low reconstruction loss need not imply useful class separation; the PCA/LDA
comparison in Section~\ref{sec:lda-worked} illustrates this objective mismatch.

For reconstruction-based anomaly detection, declare the reference population,
score, and threshold-selection block. High reconstruction error means poor
reconstruction under this model; it does not establish a harmful or causal
anomaly. An overcomplete model can reconstruct unusual inputs well. Fit
preprocessing and the encoder on permitted training records, select any
threshold using appropriate validation data, and evaluate the frozen decision
using the splitting units and costs in Sections~\ref{sec:cv-protocol}
and~\ref{sec:cost-sensitive}. The device workflow in
Section~\ref{sec:device-workflow} shows how to separate fitting and decision
selection, though its score is a class probability rather than reconstruction
error.

\subsection{Word Embeddings}

\subsubsection{Motivation}

\begin{definition}[Word Representation Problem]
How to represent words for machine learning?

\textbf{One-hot encoding}:
\[
\text{``cat''} = [0, 0, 1, 0, \ldots, 0], \quad \text{``dog''} = [0, 0, 0, 1, \ldots, 0]
\]

\textbf{Problems}:
\begin{itemize}
\item High-dimensional ($|V|$ for vocabulary size)
\item Sparse (only one 1)
\item No semantic similarity (all words equally distant)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Solution}: Dense, low-dimensional embeddings that capture semantics.
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Word2Vec}

\begin{definition}[Word2Vec]
Learn word embeddings by predicting context words.

\textbf{Two architectures}:

\textbf{1. CBOW (Continuous Bag of Words)}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Input: Context words
\item Output: Target (center) word
\item Predict word from its context
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Skip-gram}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Input: Target word
\item Output: Context words
\item Predict context from word
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Training objective} (Skip-gram):
\[
\min\ -\frac{1}{|\mathcal P|}\sum_{(t,u)\in\mathcal P}\log P(w_u\mid w_t),\quad
\mathcal P=\{(t,u):1\le t,u\le T,\ 0<|u-t|\le c\}
\]
where $c$ is the context window size and only valid in-document pairs enter the mean; no out-of-range boundary tokens are included.

\textbf{Probability}: Use separate input vectors $\vect{v}_w$ and output/context vectors $\vect{u}_w$:
\[
P(w_O\mid w_I)=\frac{\exp(\vect{u}_{w_O}^\top\vect{v}_{w_I})}{\sum_{w\in V}\exp(\vect{u}_w^\top\vect{v}_{w_I})}
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Word2Vec Properties}:

\textbf{Semantic similarity}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Distributional training can place words with similar contexts nearby; this is an empirical tendency
\item $\text{similarity}(\text{``cat''}, \text{``dog''}) > \text{similarity}(\text{``cat''}, \text{``car''})$
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Analogies}:
\[
\vect{v}_{\text{king}} - \vect{v}_{\text{man}} + \vect{v}_{\text{woman}} \approx \vect{v}_{\text{queen}}
\]
Such analogy relations can occur empirically; their quality depends on the learned corpus representation.

\textbf{Composition}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Adding word vectors is a simple composition heuristic; it need not represent a non-compositional phrase such as ``New York'' accurately
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Training efficiency}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Negative sampling: Instead of full softmax over $|V|$, sample few negative examples
\item Hierarchical softmax: Use tree structure for faster computation
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsubsection{GloVe}

\begin{definition}[GloVe (Global Vectors)]
Learn embeddings by factorizing word co-occurrence matrix.

\textbf{Objective}:
\[
J = \sum_{\substack{1\le i,j\le |V|\\X_{ij}>0}} f(X_{ij})(\vect{w}_i^\top \tilde{\vect{w}}_j + b_i + \tilde{b}_j - \log X_{ij})^2
\]
where:
\begin{itemize}
\item $X_{ij}$: Number of times word $j$ appears in context of word $i$
\item $f(X_{ij})$: Weighting function (down-weight rare co-occurrences)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Advantage}: Uses global corpus statistics (Word2Vec uses local context).
\end{definition}

\subsection{Contrastive Learning}

\begin{definition}[Contrastive Learning]
Learn representations by contrasting similar and dissimilar examples.

\textbf{Core idea}: Pull similar examples together, push dissimilar ones apart in embedding space.

\textbf{SimCLR (Simple Framework for Contrastive Learning)}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Data augmentation}: Create two views of each image (crop, color jitter, etc.)
\item \textbf{Encode}: Pass through neural network $f(\cdot)$ to get embeddings
\item \textbf{Project}: Apply projection head $g(\cdot)$ to get $\vect{z}$
\item \textbf{Contrastive loss}:
\[
\ell_{i,j} = -\log \frac{\exp(\text{sim}(\vect{z}_i, \vect{z}_j)/\tau)}{\sum_{k=1}^{2N} \mathbbm{1}_{k \neq i} \exp(\text{sim}(\vect{z}_i, \vect{z}_k)/\tau)}
\]
where $\text{sim}(\vect{u}, \vect{v}) = \vect{u}^\top\vect{v} / (\norm{\vect{u}}\norm{\vect{v}})$ (cosine similarity).
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Why Contrastive Learning is Powerful}:

\textbf{Self-supervised}: No labels needed! Use data augmentation to create positive pairs.

\textbf{State-of-the-art results}:
\begin{itemize}
\item SimCLR: Competitive with supervised learning on ImageNet
\item MoCo (Momentum Contrast): Efficient contrastive learning
\item CLIP: Contrastive learning of aligned image and text representations; some image-generation systems use such representations, but architectures differ
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Applications}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Image classification (pretrain on unlabeled data)
\item Few-shot learning (good representations with few labels)
\item Transfer learning (transfer to downstream tasks)
\item Multi-modal learning (vision + language)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Recent methods}:
\begin{itemize}
\item BYOL (Bootstrap Your Own Latent): No negative pairs needed
\item SimSiam: Even simpler, still effective
\item SwAV: Clustering-based contrastive learning
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

% =======================
\subsection{Embedding Objectives and Their Gradients}
\label{sec:embeddings-worked}
The autoencoder and VAE derivations in Part 5 continue to apply here; a
representation is evaluated by a declared downstream protocol. A linear probe
freezes the fitted encoder, learns a linear head on training labels, and uses
validation for selection. Frozen weights also require a declared inference
mode for dropout and batch-normalization buffers. Fit vocabularies, whitening,
and normalization on permitted training data. Augmented views of one source
example must stay in the same split. Using test inputs for representation
training is a transductive protocol and must be declared; it is different
from a test set withheld from all fitting.

\begin{example}[A complete skip-gram softmax step]
Use input vector $v=(1,0)^\top$ and context matrix
\[
U=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\\log2&1\\\log3&-1\end{pmatrix}.
\]
For target context 3, $p=\mathrm{softmax}(Uv)=(1/6,1/3,1/2)^\top$ and
$\ell=-\log p_3=\log2$. With $e_3=(0,0,1)^\top$,
\[
\nabla_v\ell=U^\top(p-e_3)=(-.318257,.833333)^\top,\qquad
\nabla_U\ell=(p-e_3)v^\top.
\]
The three rows of the latter are $(1/6,0),(1/3,0),(-1/2,0)$.
Simultaneously updating both parameter sets with $\eta=.1$, using gradients
at the old parameters, gives
\[
v'=(1.031826,-.083333)^\top,\quad
U'=\begin{pmatrix}-.016667&0\\.659814&1\\1.148612&-1\end{pmatrix},
\]
and loss $.580898$. These coordinates are constructed teaching parameters,
not pretrained word meanings. A minibatch loss averages over its valid
center--context pairs; each pair contributes once to the gradient before
that mean scaling.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/word2vec-step.pdf}
\caption{Constructed input/context vectors and the target-3 probability increase
after one simultaneous softmax gradient step.}
\end{figure}

\begin{example}[Negative sampling is a different objective]
For one positive score $u_+^\top v$ and $K$ sampled negatives, minimize
\[
\ell_{NS}=-\log\sigma(u_+^\top v)
 -\sum_{k=1}^K\log\sigma(-u_k^\top v).
\]
The negative terms are summed within a pair; average pairs over the minibatch.
The positive score derivative is $\sigma(z_+)-1$ and each negative score
derivative is $\sigma(z_k)$. With scalar $v=1,u_+=\log3,u_-=0,K=1$,
loss is $\log(8/3)=.980829$ and derivatives are
$\partial_{u_+}\ell=-.25$, $\partial_{u_-}\ell=.5$,
$\partial_v\ell=-(\log3)/4$.
Negative sampling depends on its sampling distribution and $K$; it is not
an unbiased estimator of the full-softmax loss or gradient.

For GloVe, include only $X_{ij}>0$ in the log-count objective and commonly
use $f(x)=\min\{(x/x_{max})^\alpha,1\}$. For one toy term with
$X_{ij}=2,x_{max}=4,\alpha=1$ and predicted log-count $s=0$, its contribution
is $.5(0-\log2)^2=.240227$, with derivative $-\log2$ with respect to $s$.
These toy weighting parameters are not claimed as paper defaults. Word-vector
analogies and similarity patterns depend on corpus, preprocessing, and
training; they are empirical patterns rather than algebraic semantic laws.
\end{example}

\subsection{A Fully Specified Contrastive Batch}
\begin{example}[Four views, self masking, and temperature]
Take $N=2$ original examples, two views each, and normalized embeddings
$z_1=z_2=(1,0)$, $z_3=z_4=(0,1)$. Positive pairs are $(1,2)$ and $(3,4)$.
With $\tau=1$, every anchor's denominator is $e+2$, since its self term
is excluded but its positive is included. Each positive probability is
$e/(e+2)=.576117$, and
\[
L=\frac1{2N}\sum_{i=1}^{2N}\ell_{i,j(i)}
 =\log(e+2)-1=.551445.
\]
If all four embeddings collapse to the same unit vector, each probability
is $1/3$ and loss is $\log3=1.098612$. This comparison shows why this
objective disfavors collapse on this batch; it is not a global optimization
or downstream-generalization guarantee.

For row logits $a_{ik}=z_i^\top z_k/\tau$, the derivative is
$(p_{ik}-\indicator{k=j(i)})/(2N)$ for $k\ne i$.
Backpropagation through dot products adds a factor $1/\tau$ and includes
both anchor and key roles, because each embedding occurs in other rows.
For unnormalized network output $h$, $z=h/\|h\|$ contributes Jacobian
$(I-zz^\top)/\|h\|$; ignoring this changes the gradient.
A separate two-image/two-text CLIP-style batch with matched logits 1 and
mismatched logits 0 has row and column cross-entropies
$\log(1+e^{-1})=.313262$. Its symmetric loss is the average of the two
\emph{mean} cross-entropies, not their unscaled sum.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/contrastive-views.pdf}
\caption{Four-view probabilities, with diagonal masked to zero, and the same
geometry's loss versus temperature. The dashed curve is collapsed geometry.}
\end{figure}

\section{Ethics and Fairness in Machine Learning}
\label{sec:fairness}
% =======================

\subsection{Bias and Discrimination}

\begin{definition}[Types of Bias in ML]

\textbf{1. Data Bias}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Historical bias}: Data reflects past discrimination
\item \textbf{Sampling bias}: Training data not representative of population
\item \textbf{Measurement bias}: Features measured differently for different groups
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Algorithmic Bias}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Model amplifies existing biases
\item Optimization objective doesn't account for fairness
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Interaction Bias}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Feedback loops: Biased predictions reinforce themselves
\item Example: Predictive policing $\to$ more arrests in certain areas $\to$ model predicts more crime there
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[Audit mechanisms before assigning a fairness conclusion]
A hiring model trained on historical selections may reproduce a selective
label process; an outcome available only for hired people is not an unbiased
label for all applicants. A credit feature can proxy group membership even if
a group column is removed. In any such application, specify the measured
outcome, affected population, subgroup denominators, error rates, and uncertainty.
The historical \href{https://proceedings.mlr.press/v81/buolamwini18a.html}{Gender Shades study}
audited commercial gender classification by skin type and gender; its scope
should not be silently generalized to every facial identification system.
The rate calculation below uses synthetic counts so its claims can be checked
without relying on a disputed universal description of a deployed product.
\end{example}

\subsection{Fairness Definitions}

\begin{definition}[Fairness Metrics]

\textbf{1. Demographic Parity (Statistical Parity)}:
\[
P(\hat{Y} = 1 | A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | A = 1)
\]
Equal positive prediction rates across groups $A$ (e.g., race, gender).

\textbf{2. Equalized Odds}:
\[
P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = y, A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = y, A = 1) \quad \text{for } y \in \{0, 1\}
\]
Equal TPR and FPR across groups.

\textbf{3. Equal Opportunity}:
\[
P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 1, A = 0) = P(\hat{Y} = 1 | Y = 1, A = 1)
\]
Equal TPR (recall) across groups. This is a weaker condition implied by equalized odds, since it does not also constrain FPR.

\textbf{4. Predictive Parity}:
\[
P(Y = 1 | \hat{Y} = 1, A = 0) = P(Y = 1 | \hat{Y} = 1, A = 1)
\]
Equal precision across groups.

\textbf{5. Individual Fairness}:
Similar individuals should receive similar predictions (harder to formalize).
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Fairness Trade-offs}:

\textbf{Compatibility depends on conditions}: For groups with different base rates, calibration/predictive parity and equalized error rates are generally incompatible for imperfect, nondegenerate predictors. State the exact fairness definitions and assumptions; there is no unconditional claim that every collection of fairness criteria conflicts.

\textbf{Accuracy vs Fairness}: Often a trade-off
\begin{itemize}
\item Adding constraints restricts a fixed empirical optimization problem; it cannot improve that problem's optimum objective under exact optimization
\item Held-out accuracy and held-out constraint satisfaction remain empirical questions; an empirical constraint does not guarantee population fairness
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Which fairness metric to use?}
\begin{itemize}
\item Depends on context and stakeholders
\item Consider the actual decision, harms, outcome validity, and affected groups
\item No single rate criterion is automatically appropriate for every application in a domain
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Mitigation strategies}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Pre-processing}: Transform data to remove bias
\item \textbf{In-processing}: Add fairness constraints to training
\item \textbf{Post-processing}: Adjust predictions to satisfy fairness
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Fairness Rates, Base Rates, and Their Compatibility}
\label{sec:fairness-worked}
\begin{example}[Equalized odds does not imply equal selection or precision]
Use two synthetic groups of 100 people each:
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{lrrrrrr}\toprule
Group&TP&FN&FP&TN&Prevalence&Selection rate\\\midrule
0&16&4&8&72&$.2$&$.24$\\1&40&10&5&45&$.5$&$.45$\\\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
Both groups have TPR $.8$ and FPR $.1$, so equalized odds and equal
opportunity hold on this sample. Group precisions are $16/24=2/3$ and
$40/45=8/9$; demographic parity and predictive parity do not hold.
The second group's counts coincide with the earlier binary-metric teaching
example, now used as one group in a distinct comparison.

For group prevalence $\pi_g$, common TPR $t$ and FPR $f$ imply
\[
P(\hat Y=1\mid g)=f+\pi_g(t-f),\qquad
P(Y=1\mid\hat Y=1,g)=\frac{\pi_g t}{\pi_g t+(1-\pi_g)f}.
\]
When prevalences differ, the first rates agree only if $t=f$.
For $t>0,f>0$ the precision expression is strictly increasing in prevalence,
so predictive parity cannot also hold. Degenerate cases, such as $f=0$ with
nonzero positive predictions, are exceptions. This is a precise conditional
incompatibility, not a statement that all fairness definitions always conflict.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.94\linewidth]{figures/batch4/fairness-base-rates.pdf}
\caption{The same TPR and FPR coexist with different selection rates and
precision in two fully specified synthetic groups.}
\end{figure}
Treat the outcome definition and observation process as part of the audit.
An outcome recorded only for selected applicants can make apparent error rates
unrepresentative of all applicants. Report group denominators, unavailable
outcomes, uncertainty, and relevant intersections; a rate based on two cases is
not as precise as the same rate based on thousands. Choose criteria with the
people affected and the decision context. Fit any thresholds or constraints on
training/validation data and report the frozen policy on held-out groups;
repeatedly adjusting it on test-group disparities is another selection loop.

\subsection{Explainable AI (XAI)}

\begin{definition}[Explainability]
The ability to explain or present ML predictions in understandable terms to humans.

\textbf{Why important?}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Trust: Users need to trust model decisions
\item Debugging: Understand why model fails
\item Governance: Document the applicable decision-review and transparency requirements; mathematical attribution alone does not establish compliance
\item Fairness: Detect and fix bias
\item Safety: Critical applications (healthcare, autonomous vehicles)
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{LIME (Local Interpretable Model-agnostic Explanations)}

\begin{definition}[LIME]
Explain individual predictions by approximating the model locally with an interpretable model.

\textbf{Algorithm}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Select instance $\vect{x}$ to explain
\item Perturb $\vect{x}$ to create neighborhood samples
\item Get predictions from black-box model on perturbed samples
\item Fit simple interpretable model (e.g., linear regression) weighted by proximity to $\vect{x}$
\item Explain prediction using interpretable model's coefficients
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Intuition}: Fit a simple model locally around the prediction to understand what features matter.
\end{definition}

\begin{example}[LIME for Image Classification]
Model predicts ``husky'' for an image.

LIME highlights image regions:
\begin{itemize}
\item Green regions: Support prediction (dog's face, fur)
\item Red regions: Against prediction (background)
\end{itemize}

Reveals: Model uses dog features (good) or background snow (bad---spurious correlation)?
\end{example}

\subsubsection{SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations)}

\begin{definition}[SHAP]
Uses Shapley values from cooperative game theory to assign each feature an importance value for a prediction.

\textbf{Shapley value}: Fair distribution of ``payout'' (prediction) among ``players'' (features).

\textbf{Properties}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Local accuracy}: Explanation matches prediction
\item \textbf{Consistency}: If feature contributes more, importance increases
\item \textbf{Missingness}: A feature absent from the simplified explanation representation has zero attribution
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Computation}:
\[
\phi_i = \sum_{S \subseteq F \setminus \{i\}} \frac{|S|!(|F|-|S|-1)!}{|F|!}[v_{\vect{x}}(S\cup\{i\})-v_{\vect{x}}(S)]
\]
Here $v_{\vect{x}}(S)$ is the expected model output when features in $S$ are fixed to the instance and other features are integrated over a declared background distribution. Conditional and interventional definitions differ for dependent features. The baseline is $v_{\vect{x}}(\emptyset)$; a model-unused feature need not receive zero under a conditional game with correlated features.

\textbf{Advantages over LIME}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Theoretical foundation (game theory)
\item Consistent across models
\item Can aggregate for global explanations
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{implementationbox}
\textbf{Using SHAP in Python}:

\begin{lstlisting}[language=Python]
import shap
import numpy as np
from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier

# Independent example: X_train and X_test contain numeric model features.
model = RandomForestClassifier(random_state=42)
model.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Declare a tree-path-dependent background and use the Explanation API.
explainer = shap.TreeExplainer(
    model, feature_perturbation='tree_path_dependent')
explanations = explainer(X_test)

# Forest classifiers have an output axis for classes; select one explicitly.
class_index = 1 if len(model.classes_) > 1 else 0
if explanations.values.ndim == 3:
    explanations = explanations[:, :, class_index]

# Verify the selected baseline and attributions reconstruct its probability.
np.testing.assert_allclose(
    explanations.base_values + explanations.values.sum(axis=1),
    model.predict_proba(X_test)[:, class_index], atol=1e-6)

shap.plots.beeswarm(explanations)       # Global, selected class
shap.plots.waterfall(explanations[0])   # First instance, same class
\end{lstlisting}
This example defines its reference distribution using training tree-path counts. Multi-output models require an explicit output selection; see the \href{https://shap.readthedocs.io/en/stable/generated/shap.TreeExplainer.html}{TreeExplainer documentation}. Do not mix one class's attribution values with another class's baseline. An interventional explanation with an explicit background dataset defines a different reference game.
\end{implementationbox}

\subsubsection{Other Interpretability Methods}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Attention visualization}: Displays attention weights; these weights alone are not a validated explanation of a token's causal contribution to the prediction
\item \textbf{Saliency Maps}: Gradient-based methods for images (highlight important pixels)
\item \textbf{Partial Dependence Plots (PDP)}: Show relationship between feature and prediction
\item \textbf{Counterfactual Explanations}: ``What would need to change for different prediction?''
\item \textbf{Anchors}: Find sufficient conditions for prediction (``If age > 50 AND income > 100K, then approve'')
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Local Explanations with an Explicit Reference Game}
\label{sec:explanations-worked}
\begin{example}[A weighted local surrogate need not interpolate the query]
For perturbed scalar inputs $z=(0,1,2)$ around query 1, take weights $(1,2,1)$
and black-box output $f(z)=z^2$. A LIME-style linear surrogate
$g(z)=\beta_0+\beta z$ minimizes
\[
\frac1S\sum_iw_i[f(z_i)-\beta_0-\beta z_i]^2+\lambda\beta^2,
\qquad S=\sum_iw_i=4.
\]
With design matrix $Z$ containing an intercept, $W=\mathrm{diag}(w_i)$ and
$P=\mathrm{diag}(0,1)$, the normal equations give
$\theta=(Z^\top WZ+S\lambda P)^{-1}Z^\top Wf$ when invertible.
For $\lambda=0$, weighted means are $\bar z=1,\bar f=1.5$,
weighted variance numerator 2 and covariance numerator 4, so
$\beta=2,\beta_0=-.5$. Weighted mean squared residual is $.25$ and
$g(1)=1.5\ne f(1)=1$. For $\lambda=.5$, the solution is
$\beta=1,\beta_0=.5$, objective $1.25$; using the unregularized slope in
that penalized objective would give $2.25$. The weight normalization changes
the effective regularization, just as mean/sum loss scaling does elsewhere.
Results depend on perturbations, locality weights, and output scale.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Compute all coalitions and both Shapley contributions]
For $f(x_1,x_2)=x_1+2x_2+3x_1x_2$, query $(1,1)$, and a fixed-zero
replacement reference, coalition values are
$v(\varnothing)=0,v(\{1\})=1,v(\{2\})=2,v(\{1,2\})=6$.
Averaging the two feature orderings yields
\[
\phi_1=\tfrac12[(1-0)+(6-2)]=2.5,\quad
\phi_2=\tfrac12[(2-0)+(6-1)]=3.5.
\]
They sum to $f(1,1)-v(\varnothing)=6$. If the explained output is instead
$\sigma(f)$, use coalition values $.5,\sigma(1),\sigma(2),\sigma(6)$;
contributions become $.173894$ and $.323633$, summing to $\sigma(6)-.5$.
Applying a sigmoid separately to raw-output contributions is incorrect.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/local-explanations.pdf}
\caption{Separate examples: a weighted LIME-style fit that misses the query
(left), and raw-output SHAP contributions in the two-feature coalition game (right).}
\end{figure}
\begin{example}[Conditional versus interventional reference distributions]
Let $X_1=X_2$ be a fair Bernoulli variable and the model be $f(x)=x_1$.
At query $(1,1)$, the conditional game
$v(S)=\E[f(X)\mid X_S=x_S]$ has baseline $.5$, both singleton values 1,
and full value 1. It assigns $(\phi_1,\phi_2)=(.25,.25)$ even though the
model does not use feature 2. In an interventional replacement game, missing
features are drawn from their marginal background law: singleton values are
1 and $.5$, so contributions are $(.5,0)$. This game evaluates off-support
combinations $(0,1)$ and $(1,0)$, making the specified reference important.
Neither allocation establishes a causal effect or certifies fairness.
Document background data, coalition semantics, explained class, and output
scale. Using held-out explanations to redesign a model also consumes the
held-out data as selection information.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.83\linewidth]{figures/batch4/conditional-attribution.pdf}
\caption{The same model and query can assign different contributions when the
coalition reference game changes. Both allocations sum to $.5$.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Responsible AI Practices}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=red!5!white,colframe=red!75!black,title=Guidelines for Ethical ML]
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Diverse Teams}: Include diverse perspectives in development

\item \textbf{Data Auditing}: Check training data for bias, representativeness

\item \textbf{Fairness Testing}: Evaluate model across demographic groups

\item \textbf{Transparency}: Document data sources, model architecture, limitations

\item \textbf{Human Oversight}: Keep humans in the loop for critical decisions

\item \textbf{Regular Audits}: Monitor model performance and fairness in production

\item \textbf{Right to Appeal}: Allow users to contest automated decisions

\item \textbf{Privacy}: Control data access and retention; distinguish data locality in federated learning from a formally accounted differential-privacy guarantee

\item \textbf{Impact Assessment}: Consider societal impact before deployment

\item \textbf{Continuous Learning}: Stay updated on fairness research and regulations
\end{enumerate}
\end{tcolorbox}

% =======================
\section{Modern Trends}
\label{sec:modern-trends}
% =======================

\subsection{Transfer Learning and Fine-Tuning}

\begin{definition}[Transfer Learning]
Use knowledge learned from one task (source) to improve learning on a different but related task (target).

\textbf{Why transfer?}
\begin{itemize}
\item Limited labeled data for target task
\item Training from scratch is expensive
\item Pretrained models capture general features
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Typical workflow}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Pretrain}: Train model on large dataset (e.g., ImageNet)
\item \textbf{Transfer}: Use pretrained weights as initialization
\item \textbf{Fine-tune}: Train on target task with small learning rate
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Transfer Learning Strategies}:

\textbf{1. Feature Extraction} (Frozen Pretrained Model):
\begin{itemize}
\item Freeze all pretrained layers
\item Add new layers on top
\item Train only new layers
\item Fast, works when target data is small and similar to source
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Fine-Tuning All Layers}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Unfreeze all layers
\item Train entire network with small learning rate
\item Can improve or degrade target performance; validate the chosen strategy and regularization
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Fine-Tuning Top Layers Only}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Freeze early layers (general features)
\item Unfreeze later layers (task-specific features)
\item Balance between feature extraction and full fine-tuning
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Success stories}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Computer Vision}: ResNet, EfficientNet pretrained on ImageNet
\item \textbf{NLP}: BERT, GPT pretrained on massive text corpora
\item \textbf{Speech}: Wav2Vec2 pretrained on unlabeled audio
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Low-Rank Adaptation with Dimensions and Gradients}
\label{sec:lora-worked}
For a frozen linear map $W_0\in\R^{d_{out}\times d_{in}}$, LoRA parameterizes
\[
Wx=W_0x+\frac\alpha r BAx,\qquad
A\in\R^{r\times d_{in}},\quad B\in\R^{d_{out}\times r}.
\]
Only $A,B$ are trained in this map. Rank of $BA$ is at most $r$; the original
map need not itself be low rank. The trainable count is $r(d_{in}+d_{out})$
instead of $d_{in}d_{out}$, excluding biases and other trained components.
For output loss gradient $g=\nabla_{Wx}\ell$,
\[
\nabla_B\ell=\frac\alpha r g(Ax)^\top,\qquad
\nabla_A\ell=\frac\alpha r B^\top g x^\top.
\]
Minibatch gradients average these expressions with the same loss normalization
as the base task. See the original
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2106.09685}{LoRA paper} for the parameterization.

\begin{example}[A rank-one update calculated end to end]
Take $r=1,\alpha=2$ and
\[
W_0=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&-1\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix},\quad
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
B=\begin{pmatrix}.1\\-.2\end{pmatrix}.
\]
Then $\Delta W=\begin{pmatrix}.2&.4&0\\-.4&-.8&0\end{pmatrix}$.
At $x=(1,1,1)^\top$, base output is $(0,2)^\top$ and adapted output is
$(.6,.8)^\top$. Five adapter parameters replace six full-map trainable
parameters in this deliberately small case. At dimension $4096\times4096$
and rank 8, counts become 65,536 versus 16,777,216, a factor of 256 for this
map's weights only. It is not a factor-256 reduction in total training memory,
which includes activations and optimizer state for other components.

Initializing $B=0$ and $A\ne0$ preserves the original map while allowing a
nonzero first gradient for $B$; the first gradient for $A$ is zero.
Initializing both to zero makes both gradients zero for this isolated map.
Choose rank, learning rate, and which layers to adapt using validation only.
Record pretrained weight provenance, tokenizer, normalization, batch-normalization
buffer behavior, and train/evaluation modes. Freezing gradients does not by
itself freeze mutable normalization statistics. Transfer can hurt when source
and target differ; full fine-tuning has no universal performance advantage.
\end{example}

\subsection{Foundation Models}

\begin{definition}[Foundation Models]
Large-scale models trained on broad data that can be adapted to a wide range of downstream tasks.

\textbf{Characteristics}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Trained on massive diverse datasets (web-scale)
\item Often large, but no fixed parameter-count threshold defines a foundation model
\item Capabilities beyond any one downstream training task, requiring task-specific evaluation
\item General-purpose (adapt to many tasks)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Paradigm shift}: 
\[
\text{Traditional: Task-specific data} \to \text{Task-specific model}
\]
\[
\text{Foundation: General data} \to \text{Foundation model} \to \text{Many tasks}
\]
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers)}

\begin{definition}[BERT]
Pretrained Transformer encoder (2018, Google).

\textbf{Pretraining tasks}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Masked Language Modeling (MLM)}: Select 15\% of positions and predict their originals; original BERT uses an 80/10/10 corruption mixture within that selection
\[
\text{Input: } \text{The [MASK] is on the table}
\]
\[
\text{Predict: } \text{cat}
\]
\item \textbf{Next Sentence Prediction (NSP)}: Predict if sentence B follows sentence A
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Impact}:
\begin{itemize}
\item State-of-the-art on many NLP tasks
\item Spawned many variants (RoBERTa, ALBERT, DistilBERT)
\item Showed power of bidirectional context
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer)}

\begin{definition}[GPT Family]
Autoregressive Transformer decoders (OpenAI).

\textbf{GPT-1} (2018): 117M parameters
\textbf{GPT-2} (2019): 1.5B parameters
\textbf{GPT-3} (2020): 175B parameters
\textbf{GPT-4} (2023): Multimodal; parameter count is not disclosed in the \href{https://cdn.openai.com/papers/gpt-4.pdf}{official technical report}. Do not treat an external estimate as a verified specification.

\textbf{Training}: Predict next token given previous tokens
\[
P(w_t | w_1, \ldots, w_{t-1})
\]

\textbf{Historical observation}: Larger next-token models have demonstrated varied task capabilities; scaling and this objective alone do not guarantee a particular ability
\begin{itemize}
\item Few-shot learning: Learn from few examples in prompt
\item In-context learning: Adapt to task from prompt alone
\item Chain-of-thought reasoning: Generate step-by-step solutions
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{GPT-3 Capabilities}:

\textbf{Zero-shot}: No examples, just task description
\begin{verbatim}
Translate to French: Hello, how are you?
Output: Bonjour, comment allez-vous?
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Few-shot}: Provide examples in prompt
\begin{verbatim}
Q: What is 2+2? A: 4
Q: What is 5+3? A: 8
Q: What is 7+9? A: 16
\end{verbatim}

\textbf{Illustrative capabilities to evaluate explicitly} (performance depends on model, prompting, and scoring):
\begin{itemize}
\item Arithmetic
\item Code generation
\item Common sense reasoning
\item Instruction following
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Limitations}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Hallucinations (generates plausible but false information)
\item Bias from training data
\item No grounding in real world
\item Expensive to run
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Language Objectives, Token Averaging, and Retrieval Boundaries}
\label{sec:language-objectives}
For a causal language model with a declared start context,
$P_\theta(w_{1:T})=\prod_{t=1}^T P_\theta(w_t\mid w_{<t})$.
Its token-mean loss on a masked/padded batch is
\[
L_{AR}=-\frac{\sum_{b,t}m_{bt}\log P_\theta(w_{bt}\mid w_{b,<t})}
 {\sum_{b,t}m_{bt}},
\]
where $m$ selects valid prediction targets, not padding. The causal attention
mask prevents seeing future tokens. This token mean differs from averaging
each sequence's mean equally when sequence lengths differ.
For masked language modeling, sum NLL only over the selected positions and
divide by their count. Bidirectional context is permitted for this separate
objective. In original BERT, 15\% of positions were selected; of those,
80\% used a mask token, 10\% a random token, and 10\% the unchanged token.
This is not ``replace every selected position by MASK.'' See
\href{https://aclanthology.org/N19-1423/}{Devlin et al. (2019)}.

\begin{example}[Likelihood, mean NLL, and perplexity]
For three causal targets with true-token probabilities $.8,.5,.25$, sequence
likelihood is $.1$, total NLL is $\log10$, mean NLL is $\log10/3=.767528$,
and token perplexity is $\exp(L)=10^{1/3}=2.154435$.
A separate selected MLM target with true-token probability $.6$ contributes
$-\log.6=.510826$. It does not define a normalized autoregressive likelihood
for the whole uncorrupted sentence. Compare perplexities only under compatible
tokenization, target masking, context, and evaluation data.

Split by source document, entity, and time before constructing windows.
Overlapping train/test windows from the same document can leak near-identical
continuations. Fix tokenizer fitting, deduplication, normalization, prompt
templates, and model selection before final evaluation. If the pretrained
corpus is unknown, explicitly record that benchmark contamination cannot be
excluded; a held-out downstream split alone cannot prove its absence.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Retrieval-augmented prediction and an index snapshot]
For a simplified latent-document model,
\[
P(y\mid x)=\sum_{d\in\mathcal D}P(d\mid x)P(y\mid x,d).
\]
With two documents, retrieval weights $.6,.4$ and answer likelihoods $.8,.3$,
the mixture likelihood is $.6$, giving NLL $.510826$.
A real top-$k$ retrieval approximation must declare whether weights are
renormalized over the retrieved set; omitted mass changes this formula.
Retrieval quality, generated-answer correctness, and support by retrieved
evidence are distinct evaluation targets.

Version the index and permit only documents available and authorized at the
prediction time. Keep benchmark answer keys and evaluation feedback out of
training and prompt selection. Task-authorized evidence documents can contain
the facts needed to answer a query: this is intended retrieval, not automatically
leakage. The boundary concerns unauthorized or unavailable information and
feedback used for selection. Freeze chunking, embeddings, retrieval settings,
and prompt choices before final testing. Log which index/model versions and
retrieved sources produced the answer so failures can be reproduced.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\begin{tikzpicture}[font=\small,b/.style={draw,text width=2.8cm,minimum height=1.1cm},>=Latex]
\node[b] (a) at (0,0) {Query + prediction\\time and access scope};
\node[b,fill=blue!10] (b) at (4,0) {Permitted versioned\\document index};
\node[b,fill=green!10] (c) at (8,0) {Retrieved evidence\\+ frozen generator};
\node[b,fill=orange!10] (d) at (4,-1.9) {Answer + sources\\+ version log};
\draw[->,thick] (a)--(b);\draw[->,thick] (b)--(c);
\draw[->,thick] (c.south)|-(d.east);
\end{tikzpicture}
\caption{Retrieval has its own data-availability and permission boundary.
Benchmark feedback is not an input to the frozen retrieval or prompt policy.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Federated Learning}

\begin{definition}[Federated Learning]
Train models across decentralized devices without sharing raw data.

\textbf{Workflow}:
\begin{enumerate}
\item Server sends global model to clients
\item Each client trains on local data
\item Clients send model updates (not data) to server
\item Server aggregates updates into global model
\item Repeat
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Aggregation} (FedAvg):
\[
\vect{w}_{t+1}=\sum_{k\in S_t}\frac{n_k}{\sum_{j\in S_t}n_j}\vect{w}_{k,t}^{\rm local}
\]
Here $S_t$ is the set of participating clients and $\vect{w}_{k,t}^{\rm local}$ is client $k$'s model after local training initialized from $\vect{w}_t$. The weights normalize over participating clients.
\end{definition}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Benefits of Federated Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Data locality}: Raw training data stays local in the standard protocol; shared updates can still leak information and do not by themselves provide a formal privacy guarantee
\item \textbf{Reduced latency}: On-device inference
\item \textbf{Data governance}: Local training can support data-residency requirements, but does not by itself establish regulatory compliance
\item \textbf{Communication trade-off}: Exchange model updates instead of raw records; large models and many rounds can still be costly
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Challenges}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Non-IID data}: Each client has different distribution
\item \textbf{Communication cost}: Many rounds needed
\item \textbf{Heterogeneous devices}: Different compute power
\item \textbf{Privacy attacks}: Model updates can leak information
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Applications}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Mobile keyboards (Gboard)
\item Healthcare (multiple hospitals, can't share patient data)
\item Finance (banks collaborate without sharing data)
\item IoT devices
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Enhanced privacy}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Differential privacy: Bound contributions and calibrate noise under a declared adjacency and composition accountant
\item Secure aggregation: Conceal individual updates from the aggregator under its protocol assumptions; this is distinct from differential privacy
\item Homomorphic encryption: Compute on encrypted data
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{FedAvg, Client Drift, and a Concrete Privacy Guarantee}
\label{sec:federated-worked}
If client $k$ minimizes a local \emph{mean} loss $F_k(w)$ and $n_k$ is its
sample count, the population-weighted empirical objective is
$F(w)=\sum_k(n_k/\sum_jn_j)F_k(w)$. With all clients participating and one
full local gradient step from the same $w$, averaging gives
$\sum_kp_k[w-\eta\nabla F_k(w)]=w-\eta\nabla F(w)$.
Multiple local steps generally break this equality. Sample weighting must not
multiply an already summed local loss a second time. See the original
\href{https://proceedings.mlr.press/v54/mcmahan17a.html}{FedAvg paper}.

\begin{example}[Two clients with different curvatures]
Client 1 has $n_1=2,F_1=(w-1)^2$; client 2 has $n_2=3,F_2=2(w+1)^2$.
These are mean squared losses: the second can arise from identical examples
$x=\sqrt2,y=-\sqrt2$. Global loss is $F=.4F_1+.6F_2$, with
$F'(w)=3.2w+1.6$ and optimum $w^*=-.5$.
At $w_0=0,\eta=.1$, one local step gives $.2,-.4$, averaging to $-.16$,
the same as one centralized step. Two local steps give $.36,-.64$, averaging
to $-.24$. Two centralized steps instead give $-.2688$.

The two-local-step round map is $w_{t+1}=.472w_t-.24$, whose fixed point
is $-5/11\approx-.454545$, not $-.5$. Objective excess is
$F(w)-F(w^*)=1.6(w+.5)^2$. This simple deterministic example exhibits
persistent client drift at a fixed step size. If only client 1 participates,
its first update is $.2$; normalizing over participating counts does not make
this an unbiased global step $-.16$. Sampling probabilities and the targeted
client population matter. Evaluate on held-out clients or future periods
according to the desired deployment target, rather than on local training loss.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/federated-drift.pdf}
\caption{Two local steps per round versus two centralized steps, with identical
initialization and step size. The right panel displays global objective excess.}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Differential privacy with an explicit adjacency unit]
A randomized mechanism $M$ is $(\epsilon,\delta)$-differentially private when,
for all adjacent datasets $D,D'$ and measurable events $S$,
\[
P(M(D)\in S)\le e^\epsilon P(M(D')\in S)+\delta.
\]
State whether adjacency replaces one record, adds/removes one record, or
changes one entire client's data. These are different privacy promises.
Raw-data locality and secure aggregation alone do not establish this inequality.
\end{definition}
\begin{example}[Sensitivity, Laplace noise, and composition]
For $n=100$ fixed records bounded in $[0,1]$ under replacement adjacency,
the mean has sensitivity $\Delta=1/100=.01$. Release
$M(D)=\bar x+Z$, $Z\sim\mathrm{Laplace}(0,b)$ with $b=\Delta/\epsilon$.
For $\epsilon=1$, $b=.01$ and noise standard deviation is $\sqrt2b=.014142$.
The reverse triangle inequality bounds the ratio of densities by
$\exp(|\bar x-\bar x'|/b)\le e^\epsilon$, proving pure DP ($\delta=0$).
The bound requires the declared clipping/boundedness; an arbitrary unbounded
mean has no such finite global sensitivity.

Publishing $T$ such mechanisms with budgets $\epsilon_t$ has the basic pure-DP
composition budget at most $\sum_t\epsilon_t$, including adaptive releases
under the corresponding per-step conditions. Releasing the noisy mean and
then revealing the original mean defeats the mechanism's privacy claim.
Client-level sensitivity for a federated model requires a different derivation,
usually involving bounded client updates and a declared participation process.
For scalar squared loss $(w-y)^2$, sharing gradient $g=2(w-y)$ at known $w$
reveals $y=w-g/2$ exactly: keeping raw labels local is insufficient by itself.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.97\linewidth]{figures/batch4/privacy-laplace.pdf}
\caption{Adjacent bounded means $.60$ and $.61$, with Laplace scale $.01$.
Their log-density ratio lies between $-1$ and 1 for every released value.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{AutoML (Automated Machine Learning)}

\begin{definition}[AutoML]
Automate the end-to-end process of applying ML to real-world problems.

\textbf{Components}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Data preprocessing
\item Feature engineering
\item Model selection
\item Hyperparameter optimization
\item Architecture search (for neural networks)
\item Ensemble construction
\end{itemize}
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{Neural Architecture Search (NAS)}

\begin{definition}[NAS]
Automatically design neural network architectures.

\textbf{Search space}: Define possible architectures (layers, connections, operations)

\textbf{Search strategy}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Random search
\item Reinforcement learning
\item Evolutionary algorithms
\item Gradient-based (DARTS)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Performance estimation}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Train from scratch (expensive)
\item Early stopping
\item Weight sharing (ENAS)
\item Performance prediction
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Assessment}: Search-discovered architectures can perform well under stated datasets and budgets; compare against tuned baselines with matched training/search resources rather than assuming universal superiority.
\end{definition}

\subsubsection{AutoML Tools}

\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Auto-sklearn}: Automated scikit-learn pipeline
\item \textbf{TPOT}: Genetic programming for pipeline optimization
\item \textbf{H2O AutoML}: Automated model selection and ensembling
\item \textbf{Google Cloud AutoML}: Managed AutoML service
\item \textbf{AutoKeras}: NAS for Keras
\item \textbf{PyCaret}: Low-code ML library
\end{itemize}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{AutoML: Democratization or Threat?}

\textbf{Pros}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Lowers barrier to entry (non-experts can use ML)
\item Frees experts to focus on problem formulation
\item Often finds better models than manual search
\item Reproducible workflows
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Cons}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Can be slow (lots of trials)
\item Black box (hard to understand choices)
\item May not consider domain constraints
\item Expensive (compute cost)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Reality}: AutoML is a tool, not a replacement. ML expertise still needed for:
\begin{itemize}
\item Problem formulation
\item Data quality assessment
\item Interpreting results
\item Debugging failures
\item Ethical considerations
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Automated Search Still Needs an Evaluation Boundary}
\begin{example}[Selection optimism without any biased single-model score]
Two candidate models both have true risk $.2$. Suppose each validation estimate
independently equals $.15$ or $.25$ with equal probability. Each estimate is
unbiased. Selecting the smaller gives $.15$ with probability $3/4$ and $.25$
with probability $1/4$, so the reported selected validation risk has expectation
$.175$, below $.2$. A new independent test estimate remains centered on $.2$.
Automating the search over preprocessing, features, architectures, prompts,
and thresholds does not remove this minimum-selection effect. Include the
whole adaptive procedure inside the outer evaluation boundary, record trial
budgets and random seeds, and compare with fixed-budget baselines. A larger
search budget is an additional experimental resource, not a free improvement.
\end{example}

\subsection{A Quantization Error Calculation and Its Deployment Consequences}
\begin{example}[Symmetric signed 8-bit weights]
Let $s=\max_j|w_j|/127$ and
$q_j=\mathrm{clip}(\mathrm{round}(w_j/s),-127,127)$,
$\tilde w_j=sq_j$. Define a harmless positive scale, such as 1, for an all-zero
weight tensor. Without clipping overflow, $|\tilde w_j-w_j|\le s/2$.
For $w=(-1,-.1,.3,1)$, $s=1/127$, codes are $(-127,-13,38,127)$ and
reconstructed weights are $(-1,-.102362,.299213,1)$.
For $x=(1,2,-1,1)$, original dot product is $-.5$ and reconstructed value is
$-64/127=-.503937$. Its absolute error $1/254$ obeys
\[
|x^\top(\tilde w-w)|\le\|x\|_1\|\tilde w-w\|_\infty
\le 5/254.
\]
Weight-only 32-bit to 8-bit storage has an ideal factor of four before scales,
metadata, and other tensors. It does not imply a fourfold latency improvement:
kernels, hardware, activation precision, and memory traffic determine speed.
Activation clipping ranges are learned on an allowed calibration dataset,
not final test data. This quantizer-calibration role is distinct from probability
calibration. If quantization changes the base scores, revalidate probability
calibration and decisions with appropriate non-test blocks before freezing the
new complete artifact. Include threshold-near inputs in parity checks.
\end{example}
\begin{figure}[H]\centering
\includegraphics[width=.92\linewidth]{figures/batch4/quantization-error.pdf}
\caption{Exact reconstruction errors for the four stated weights. Dashed lines
are the half-step bound; the first and last weights reconstruct exactly.}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Reading and Reproducing Parts 6--8}
The numerical figures are generated by
\texttt{scripts/build\_batch4\_figures.py}, with explicit fixtures in
\texttt{scripts/batch4\_examples.py}. Their manifest records versions, seed,
figure names, and the fixed workflow report. Validation checks analytic
calculations against independent enumeration, finite differences, library
metrics, and saved-artifact predictions. The workflow, service, export adapter,
and validation report distinguish executed paths from optional framework
recipes. The source-specific links in these sections point to primary papers
or official documentation; historical model examples are dated illustrations,
not an inventory of current products.

For the original formulations, see
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1310.4546}{negative sampling},
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2002.05709}{SimCLR},
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1602.04938}{LIME},
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/1705.07874}{SHAP}, and
\href{https://arxiv.org/abs/2005.11401}{retrieval-augmented generation}.
For privacy definitions, the Laplace mechanism, and basic composition, see
\href{https://www.cis.upenn.edu/~aaroth/Papers/privacybook.pdf}{Dwork and Roth's monograph}.
The small numerical fixtures above are constructed here to expose the calculations.

The whole-book audit runs the retained P0 checks, all three expansion-batch
validators, and the final consistency checks. From the repository root, in
the Python environment described in Part 7, use:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=bash,basicstyle=\ttfamily\footnotesize]
python -m pip install -r scripts/requirements-final-review.txt
python scripts/validate_final.py
latexmk -pdf main.tex
\end{lstlisting}
On macOS, the XGBoost wheel also needs a compatible OpenMP runtime; this audit
used Homebrew's \texttt{libomp}. Python package pins do not install that system
library. Inspect rendered figures and changed pages after compilation;
numerical checks do not replace layout review.

\subsection{Future Directions}

\begin{keyidea}
\textbf{Emerging Trends and Open Problems}:

\textbf{1. Multimodal Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Models that understand multiple modalities (text, image, audio, video)
\item CLIP, DALL-E, Flamingo, GPT-4
\end{itemize}

\textbf{2. Continual Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Learn new tasks without forgetting old ones
\item Overcome catastrophic forgetting
\end{itemize}

\textbf{3. Few-Shot and Zero-Shot Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Learn from very few examples
\item Generalize to unseen classes
\end{itemize}

\textbf{4. Causal Machine Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Go beyond correlation to causation
\item Study transport across environments under explicit causal identification and invariance assumptions
\item Counterfactual reasoning
\end{itemize}

\textbf{5. Energy-Efficient ML}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Measure training/inference energy and report hardware, utilization, runtime, and carbon-intensity assumptions
\item Model compression, pruning, quantization
\item Efficient architectures
\end{itemize}

\textbf{6. Trustworthy AI}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Robustness to adversarial attacks
\item Uncertainty quantification
\item Certified defenses
\end{itemize}

\textbf{7. Human-AI Collaboration}:
\begin{itemize}
\item AI as assistant, not replacement
\item Interactive ML
\item Human-in-the-loop systems
\end{itemize}

\textbf{8. Quantum Machine Learning}:
\begin{itemize}
\item Leverage quantum computers for ML
\item Quantum neural networks
\item Any speedup claim needs explicit data-access, hardware, noise, and classical-baseline assumptions
\end{itemize}
\end{keyidea}

\subsection{Conclusion}

\begin{tcolorbox}[colback=green!5!white,colframe=green!75!black,title=Final Thoughts]
Machine Learning is a rapidly evolving field with profound impact on society. As practitioners, we must:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Master fundamentals}: Strong foundation enables understanding new methods

\item \textbf{Stay curious}: New papers, techniques, applications emerge daily

\item \textbf{Think critically}: Not every problem needs ML; not every solution is ethical

\item \textbf{Embrace uncertainty}: Models are tools, not truth

\item \textbf{Prioritize ethics}: Consider impact on individuals and society

\item \textbf{Collaborate}: ML is interdisciplinary---work with domain experts

\item \textbf{Keep learning}: The field evolves faster than any textbook
\end{enumerate}

The journey from mathematical foundations to modern foundation models is remarkable. Yet the most exciting developments are still ahead. Whether through scientific breakthroughs, ethical frameworks, or novel applications, the future of ML will be shaped by thoughtful practitioners who combine technical skill with wisdom.

\textbf{Your role}: Build systems that are not just intelligent, but beneficial.
\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{exercise}
\begin{enumerate}
\item Design a Q-learning agent for a simple grid world. Define states, actions, rewards, and write the Q-update rule.

\item Explain why experience replay and target networks are crucial for DQN stability. What problems do they solve?

\item Compare PCA (from Part 5) and autoencoders for dimensionality reduction. When would you prefer autoencoders?

\item Using a specified embedding corpus, test whether the king/man/woman/queen analogy holds. Explain how corpus statistics, retrieval metric, and exclusions affect the result rather than assuming the identity always works.

\item A facial recognition system has 99\% accuracy but 5\% false positive rate for one demographic group vs 1\% for another. Which fairness definition is violated? Propose a mitigation strategy.

\item You have 100 labeled examples and 10,000 unlabeled examples for image classification. Design a strategy using transfer learning and/or contrastive learning.

\item Compare fine-tuning BERT for sentiment analysis vs training a CNN from scratch. What are the trade-offs?

\item Design a federated learning system for predicting disease from hospital data. Address: communication efficiency, privacy, and non-IID data distributions.
\end{enumerate}
\end{exercise}
